2026-09-17  —  XRn_ENA_OUT/XRn_CONTACT_OUT (PG0-PG7) implemented --
               ARM precondition + new per-channel SM_FAULT_ENABLE_OUTPUT
-----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Change:  Direct request, fulfilling the "remind me later" obligation
        from earlier the same day: "Enable and contactor fiber outputs
        need to be set by a serial command, one for each supply. The
        controller cannot be armed unless these are outputting prior
        to the arm signal, and similarly we cannot transition to the
        ARM state i[f] these are not output."

        Two scope questions asked directly before implementing (not
        assumed): (1) should the ARM check apply to all 4 channels or
        only currently-enabled ones -- chose per-channel gating,
        matching the established Water/Temp/Enerpro/OCP convention;
        (2) should losing these after ARM force a fault or just be a
        one-time precondition -- chose continuous monitoring with a
        real fault response, entering FAULT (not just refusing the
        next FIRE) if lost while ARMED or FIRING.

        New per-channel outputs XR1-4_ENA_OUT (PG0-PG3) and
        XR1-4_CONTACT_OUT (PG4-PG7, docs/pin_mapping_v4.csv's "XREX Pin
        Name" column, confirmed GPO, unused elsewhere) -- owned (pin
        table, GPIO read/write) by xrex_io.c, matching that module's
        established per-channel-XR-signal charter. New
        XREX:CHANnel:ENAOut/CONTactOut set+query commands (reuses ERR
        11/ERR 12, no new error codes).

        New per-channel fault type SM_FAULT_ENABLE_OUTPUT
        (state_machine.h/.c) -- reuses HandleOvercurrentFault(channel)
        DIRECTLY for its response (immediate hard-disable of the
        affected channel, survivors derated 1/N and ramped down exactly
        like a real OCP fault), same "distinct type, shared response"
        pattern SM_FAULT_EXTERNAL_ENABLE already established for
        HandleGeneralFault(). Do NOT confuse the name with
        SM_FAULT_EXTERNAL_ENABLE -- that's a single system-wide INPUT
        interlock (PF13); this is a per-channel check of this
        firmware's own commanded OUTPUT state. New
        XrexIo_EnableOutputsReadyToArm() (pure check, folds into
        ArmConditionsMet()/ERR 13) and XrexIo_PollEnableOutputFaults()
        (continuous check, gated to ONLY run while ARMED/FIRING --
        unlike Water/Temp/Enerpro/OCP's always-on checking -- called
        from the same tick cadence XrexIo_PollOcpFaults() already gets).
        No dedicated FAULT:CLEAR re-validation needed for this fault
        type, matching SM_FAULT_OVERCURRENT's own precedent -- the
        affected channel is already hard-disabled by the time
        FAULT:CLEAR is considered, so the real gate is the next ARM
        attempt's own ArmConditionsMet() check.

  Verify: Rebuilt clean (zero warnings), reflashed, verified live over
        serial:

        1. XREX:CHANnel:ENAOut/CONTactOut set+query confirmed correct
           and independent per channel (toggled channels 1 and 2
           separately, each read back exactly what was written).
           Argument validation (ERR 11 for channel 0/5, ERR 12 for a
           missing value) confirmed.
        2. Per-channel gating confirmed: with zero channels enabled,
           ARM succeeded regardless of ENA_OUT/CONTACT_OUT state
           (channel 1 ended up half-set, channel 2 set-but-mismatched)
           -- directly confirms disabled channels are correctly
           skipped, not blocking ARM.

        NOT YET VERIFIED: the actual refusal path (an ENABLED channel
        with outputs not set blocking ARM) and the continuous
        fault-on-loss-while-ARMED path -- enabling any channel on this
        bench immediately trips the pre-existing OCP fault (nothing
        wired to those pins yet), so a real enabled channel can't
        currently be gotten far enough to test this specific gate.
        Logic mirrors the already-hardware-verified OCP per-channel
        response exactly, so risk is low, but this is a real,
        tracked gap (pending-hardware-calibration.md) until either
        something's wired to the OCP pins or a Transrex simulator
        (separately planned) exists to provide a healthy default.

2026-09-17  —  External-enable moved PF15 -> PF13; external-trigger
               UNCOUPLED from external-enable, PF15 now trigger-only,
               fires only from ARMED; ERR 16 retired
-----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Change:  Direct instruction: "Let's move the 'enable' behavior of
        PF15 to PF13... The TRIGGER from a rising edge behavior should
        remain at PF15." Enable and trigger, previously two facets of
        the SAME physical wire (PF15), are now genuinely independent
        signals on separate pins. PF13 (docs/pin_mapping_v4.csv,
        documented "GPInput_12", confirmed unused elsewhere) checked
        before use, per this project's now-well-established pattern.

        Asked directly, before implementing: should EXTernal:TRIGger
        still require EXTernal:ENAble to be on first (the old ERR 16
        rule), now that they're separate pins? User chose to drop the
        dependency. Real safety is unaffected either way -- SM_Fire()
        itself (state_machine.c) unconditionally re-checks the enable
        interlock every time it's called, including from trigger's own
        edge-detection path, regardless of how trigger was configured.

        state_machine.c: ExternalEnableInputIsHigh() now reads PF13 (was
        PF15); new ExternalTriggerInputIsHigh() reads PF15 independently.
        g_lastExternalEnableLevelWhileArmed renamed to
        g_lastExternalTriggerLevelWhileArmed -- it always tracked
        trigger's own edge-detection baseline, a name that would now
        actively mislead since it sounds like it tracks the new enable
        pin. SM_SetExternalEnableRequired()/SM_SetExternalTriggerRequired()
        no longer couple to each other in either direction.
        SM_GetExternalTriggerInputRaw() (new) backs a new
        EXTernal:TRIGger:INPut? -- previously EXTernal:INPut? covered
        this same pin since it was shared; now that enable and trigger
        are separate, each gets its own raw-read diagnostic.

        main.c: PF13 configured GPIO_PULLDOWN (same fail-safe reasoning
        PF15 originally had for enable -- floating means no permission).
        PF15 keeps GPIO_PULLDOWN too, now purely so a floating trigger
        pin never produces a spurious rising edge.

        commands.c/.h, cmd_parser.c: EXTernal:ENAble/ENAble?/INPut?
        unchanged in shape, now backed by PF13. EXTernal:TRIGger no
        longer returns ERR 16 -- always succeeds. New
        EXTernal:TRIGger:INPut? registered. ERR 16 retired (no longer
        generated) but not renumbered/reassigned, per this project's
        established error-code convention.

  Verify: Rebuilt clean (zero warnings), reflashed, verified live over
        serial:

        1. Command-layer: EXTernal:INPut? (PF13) and
           EXTernal:TRIGger:INPut? (PF15) both read correctly (0,
           floating/unconnected default). EXTernal:TRIGger 1 succeeds
           with EXTernal:ENAble still OFF -- confirms the ERR 16
           coupling is genuinely gone, not just skipped by luck.
        2. Real-hardware trigger mechanism, re-verified post-split
           (PD1/DIAGnostic:GPOut12 still looped to PF15 from the
           original 2026-09-16 test setup): armed with PF15 LOW
           (baseline correctly captured, no spurious fire over 0.3s);
           genuine LOW->HIGH transition on PF15 correctly transitioned
           ARMED -> FIRING; holding PF15 HIGH afterward did not
           re-fire (confirms edge-triggered, not level-triggered).
           Zero channels were enabled for this test (to avoid tripping
           the unrelated, already-known OCP-floating-pin fault from the
           XREX work above) -- this meant the profile's own auto-
           completion never actually ran (the shared HRTIM Master
           needs at least one enabled channel to tick, a real but
           separate, not-yet-investigated behavior), recovered cleanly
           via PID:STOP. Not a regression in today's change -- the
           trigger-fires-a-real-shot path (with real channels enabled)
           was already confirmed working on 2026-09-16, before today's
           restructuring, and today's change didn't touch that
           mechanism, only which pin backs which role.

        NOT YET VERIFIED: PF13's own ARM/PID:PROFile:STARt gating, the
        FIRING-time SM_FAULT_EXTERNAL_ENABLE fault entry, and
        FAULT:CLEAR's re-validation, all against a REAL HIGH/LOW signal
        on PF13 -- nothing is wired to PF13 on the bench yet, so only
        its raw-read default (floating, reads 0) has been confirmed.
        These same behaviors were fully confirmed on 2026-09-16 against
        the old PF15-based design and the underlying code paths are
        unchanged (SM_ExternalEnableOk()/ArmConditionsMet()/
        SM_PollFaults()'s external-enable branch), just now reading a
        different GPIO pin -- low risk, but genuinely unverified until
        something is wired to PF13 and this exact sequence is re-run.

2026-09-17  —  E-stop polarity inverted for new hardware inverter; PG10
               discovered wired to the MCU's real NRST pin via the
               schematic -- E-stop permanently shelved, GPIO config
               removed; DIAGnostic:GPOut11 (PD0) + DIAGnostic:RSTCause?
               added along the way
-----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Change:  Retried the shelved E-stop feature: a hardware inverter was
        added between the fiber-optic receiver and PG10, so "no input"
        (including PG10's own GPIO_PULLDOWN floating default) now needs
        to read as OK, not asserted -- the opposite of the original
        design. Renamed ctrlr_config.h's XREX_POLARITY_NORMALLY_HIGH/LOW
        to the generic FAULT_POLARITY_NORMALLY_HIGH/LOW (a second,
        unrelated consumer -- EMERGENCY_STOP_POLARITY -- made the old
        XREX-scoped name actively misleading), added
        EMERGENCY_STOP_POLARITY defaulting NORMALLY_LOW, and added
        state_machine.c's EmergencyStopAsserted() to apply it.

        Testing needed a SECOND diagnostic output distinct from PD1
        (already dedicated to PF15) -- new DIAGnostic:GPOut11 (PD0,
        "GPOut_11" in pin_mapping_v4.csv's V4 column, confirmed GPO),
        exact mirror of DIAGnostic:GPOut12.

        REAL BUG FOUND: DIAGnostic:GPOut11 1 consistently returned a
        single garbled 0x00 byte instead of "OK\r\n", and the pin never
        actually reached HIGH. Investigated methodically rather than
        guessed: ruled out direct PD0-PG10 electrical contention (user
        confirmed the real topology is fully galvanically isolated --
        PD0 drives a fiber transmitter, through fiber, to an inverting
        receiver, whose output lands on PG10). Built a new TEMPORARY
        DIAGnostic:RSTCause?/:CLEar (reads/clears the real RCC->CSR
        reset-cause flags, stm32g474xx.h's own bit definitions) to get
        a real answer instead of a further guess. Result: PIN=1 (a
        genuine NRST-pin assertion), BOR=0 (ruling out power-rail
        droop) -- a real hardware reset, not corruption.

        User checked the actual schematic: PG10 IS the MCU's real NRST
        pin's net, tied to the same Molex connector the ST-Link plugs
        into -- NOT a stale CSV label as concluded on 2026-09-16 (that
        earlier conclusion is now superseded). This means configuring
        this pin as a GPIO peripheral input AT ALL was unsound the
        entire time, independent of EMERGency:ENAble's state -- our own
        GPIO_PULLDOWN was a weak pull-down fighting NRST's internal
        pull-up on every boot.

        Asked the user how to proceed (AskUserQuestion): chose to
        shelve E-stop again AND remove the GPIO_PULLDOWN config on PG10
        specifically, since that risk exists independent of the
        feature's enable state. main.c's PG10 GPIO_Init() block
        removed entirely -- this pin must never be configured as a
        GPIO peripheral again. state_machine.c's emergency-stop
        software left in place, dormant (EMERGency:ENAble defaults off
        and never reads any pin when off).

  Verify: Rebuilt clean (zero warnings) at each step, reflashed each
        time, verified live over serial:

        1. Inverted polarity, LOW=OK: with EMERGency:ENAble 1 and PD1
           (looped to PF15 at the time) held LOW, STATE? correctly
           stayed IDLE -- confirmed the new "no input = good" default
           behaves correctly in firmware.
        2. The GPOut11 glitch reproduced 100% reliably, isolated
           cleanly (GPOut12 on the SAME session kept working perfectly
           the whole time, ruling out a general regression).
        3. DIAGnostic:RSTCause:CLEar then reproducing the glitch showed
           a CLEAN CSR=04000000 PIN=1 BOR=0 from a zeroed baseline --
           definitive, not inferred.
        4. CRITICAL FINDING: removing main.c's own PG10 GPIO config
           entirely did NOT stop the reset -- re-tested from a cleared
           baseline, PIN=1 still fired. Proves this is a pure board-
           level electrical fact, unfixable from firmware: the inverting
           receiver's own output driver asserts the NRST net regardless
           of what our code does with the "PG10" pin.

        Board left in a known-safe state after every step (all
        channels disabled, STATE? IDLE). E-stop's real FIRING-time
        cutoff behavior and a genuine HIGH-restore FAULT:CLEAR path
        remain unverified and will stay that way until a genuinely free
        pin (not PG10) is identified from the schematic -- see
        pending-hardware-calibration.md item 10 (updated).

2026-09-17  —  XREX pin relabeling + real per-channel Water/Temp/
               Enerpro/OCP fault detection (xrex_io.c, new module);
               NORMALLY_HIGH polarity override; XREX:CHANnel:STATus?
-----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Change:  User added a new "XREX Pin Name" column to docs/
        pin_mapping_v4.csv, naming a variety of pins on this Transrex
        controller by role: XR1-XR4 are Transrex 1-4, each with its own
        _WATER_FLT/_TMP_FLT/_ENERPRO_FLT/_OCP_FLT (fault inputs),
        _FEEDBACK/_DRIVE (relabels of the existing PFM_Input_01..04 and
        HRTIM1_CH* pins, zero functional change), and _ENA_OUT/
        _CONTACT_OUT (new outputs, explicitly DEFERRED -- see below).
        Extracted the full mapping PROGRAMMATICALLY (a Python
        csv.DictReader script, not by eye), given the safety stakes and
        this project's established pattern of catching pin-mapping
        mismatches early (PC14-vs-PF15, PF13-vs-PD1, both 2026-09-16).

        Requested behavior: Water/Temp/Enerpro count as "General Fault"
        (SM_FAULT_GENERAL); OCP is its own fault type
        (SM_FAULT_OVERCURRENT); each fault category independently
        polarity-configurable, defaulting NORMALLY_HIGH; and -- the
        core new behavior -- a channel's own fault pins only count
        toward a fault when that Transrex channel is actually enabled
        (PID_GetChannelEnable()), so e.g. running XR1 alone never
        blocks/stops output because XR2/3/4's fault pins read LOW.

        POLARITY CONFLICT, resolved by direct user confirmation: the
        existing GDS_FAULT_POLARITY = GDS_NORMALLY_LOW (ctrlr_config.h)
        had been set 2026-09-08 from a real hardware snapshot of these
        SAME 12 physical pins (11/12 LOW, 1 HIGH, consistent with
        LOW=healthy). The user's new instruction requires the OPPOSITE
        -- NORMALLY_HIGH -- for Water/Temp/Enerpro. Confirmed directly
        ("During normal non-faulted operation, these pins will be
        normally high... falling edge registers as a fault") as a
        deliberate override, not a mistake, and implemented as such,
        with the contradiction documented in ctrlr_config.h. NOT yet
        independently re-verified against a real healthy (non-floating)
        signal on these pins -- flagged open below.

        EXTI HARDWARE CONFLICT, found before implementation, not after:
        initial plan was EXTI-interrupt-driven OCP detection (user's
        own first choice, on my recommendation). Before writing any
        code, mapped the 4 new OCP pins (PF4/PF5/PF8/PF12) to their EXTI
        line numbers and found PF4(EXTI4)/PF5(EXTI5)/PF8(EXTI8) directly
        conflict with PE4/PE5/PE8 -- already claimed by the existing
        GateDriverStatus EXTI setup (STM32's 16 EXTI lines are shared
        project-wide, one GPIO port per line via SYSCFG_EXTICR); only
        PF12(EXTI12, XR3_OCP) was actually free. Flagged this as a real
        hardware constraint, not a preference; user agreed to poll all
        4 OCP pins uniformly instead of mixing 3-polled-1-EXTI.

        New module Core/Src/xrex_io.c + Core/Inc/xrex_io.h -- owns all
        new XREX-semantic fault interpretation, sitting ON TOP of
        gate_driver.c's existing raw GateDriver_Read() accessor for
        Water/Temp/Enerpro (reused, not duplicated) and owning entirely
        new OCP polling (no prior code existed for this).
        gate_driver.c's own naming/identity/GDS?/GateDriver_* functions
        deliberately left UNCHANGED (per direct labeling-scope decision
        -- "add a secondary label for querying, then document and new
        code" -- the GDS/GateDriverStatus language stays as-is, it was
        chosen for the sibling limiter/HVPS h-bridge supplies project
        and is simply outdated terminology here, not wrong code); only
        GateDriver_CheckFault()'s internal fault-DECISION logic now
        delegates to XrexIo_EvaluateGateDriverFault().

        New XrexIo_PollOcpFaults() called every PID_Update() tick and
        once at boot, alongside the existing SM_PollFaults() call --
        reads PF4/PF5/PF8/PF12 directly (GPIO_PULLDOWN, matching this
        project's established fail-safe-default convention) and calls
        the pre-existing SM_ReportOcpFault(channel) (originally added
        for OCP:TEST:FAULT software injection) on a real fault.

        New XREX:CHANnel:STATus? <ch> (commands.c/.h, cmd_parser.c) --
        per-channel WATER/TMP/ENERPRO/OCP readback by name, backed by
        new XrexIo_GetChannelStatus(). Reuses ERR 11/12 (invalid/
        missing channel), matching every other numbered-channel
        command's convention -- no new error codes needed.

        BUILD GOTCHA hit and fixed (matches AGENTS.md's own documented
        pattern for this exact situation): xrex_io.c compiled cleanly
        but the FIRST link attempt failed with "undefined reference to
        XrexIo_*" from every caller (commands.c, gate_driver.c, main.c,
        pid.c) -- Debug/Core/Src/subdir.mk and Debug/objects.list are
        static, CubeIDE-generated files that don't auto-discover new
        source files added outside the IDE. Hand-patched both (adding
        xrex_io.c/.o/.d entries) -- second build linked clean, zero
        warnings, full `wham_build.py --clean` also clean.

  Verify: Reflashed (python/wham_serial_flash.py) and verified live over
        serial, 2026-09-17:

        1. XREX:CHANnel:STATus? 1-4 -- all four correctly report
           WATER/TMP/ENERPRO/OCP (matching GDS?'s own raw readback for
           the first three); ch=0 and ch=5 correctly ERR 11, missing
           argument correctly ERR 12.
        2. Per-channel gating, the core new behavior: at boot, with
           PID_Init()'s default outputEnabled=1 on channel 1 and NOTHING
           physically wired yet to the new OCP pins (floating,
           GPIO_PULLDOWN -> LOW -> fault under NORMALLY_HIGH), the new
           polling correctly latched STATE? FAULT OVERCURRENT 1 --
           confirming the whole real-hardware chain (pin read -> polarity
           -> SM_ReportOcpFault -> state machine) fires correctly, not
           just compiles. Disabled all 4 channels (PID:CHANnel:ENAble
           <ch> 0) and FAULT:CLEAR -- STATE? stayed IDLE afterward even
           though GDS? still showed all 12 pins LOW and all 4 OCP pins
           LOW -- direct confirmation that a disabled channel's fault
           pins are genuinely excluded, not just narrowly not-yet-
           triggered.
        3. Channel-identity correctness: with ALL channels still
           disabled, enabled ONLY channel 3 (PID:CHANnel:ENAble 3 1) --
           STATE? immediately reported FAULT OVERCURRENT 3, not 1 or any
           other channel, confirming the PF4/PF8/PF12/PF5 -> XR1-XR4
           pin table (irregular, hand-written, not arithmetic) is
           correct. Disabled channel 3 again, FAULT:CLEAR, confirmed
           back to a clean STATE? IDLE with all 4 channels disabled --
           board left in a known-safe idle state.

        NOT YET VERIFIED: the actual physical Water/Temp/Enerpro/OCP
        signal sources are not wired to these pins on the bench yet --
        every real-hardware check above exercised the fail-safe
        floating/LOW state, not a genuine HIGH-healthy vs LOW-faulted
        transition from a real sensor/gate-driver signal. The
        NORMALLY_HIGH polarity default in particular is a direct-
        instruction override of a previously hardware-confirmed value
        and needs its own independent real-signal confirmation once
        these pins are actually connected -- flagged in
        pending-hardware-calibration.md.

        DEFERRED, not part of this change: XRn_ENA_OUT (PG0-PG3) and
        XRn_CONTACT_OUT (PG4-PG7) -- per-channel enable/contactor

2026-09-16  —  DIAGnostic:GPOut12 added (PD1); external-enable AND
               external-trigger CONFIRMED on real hardware, exact
               numbers below -- both previous same-day entries closed
-----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Change:  Board reconnected this same day, after being unavailable for
        the two entries directly below. Before any real-hardware
        verification could happen, needed a way to drive PF15 (Fiber_
        Enable) with precise, repeatable timing -- a hand-operated bench
        jumper/switch works but can't hit exact moments (e.g. "exactly
        mid-flat-top") the way this project's other verification work
        already relies on. Direct request: configure a diagnostic
        output the operator could physically loop to PF15, so the whole
        interlock/trigger sequence could be driven from the serial
        console instead.

        Same pin-mapping-mismatch pattern caught again, same day: user
        first said "GPOut_12 (PF13)" -- checked docs/pin_mapping_v4.csv
        before writing any code and found PF13 is actually documented
        "GPInput_12" (an INPUT) there; the pin genuinely named
        "GPOut_12" in the V4 column is PD1 (confirmed GPO). Flagged
        directly, same as the earlier PC14-vs-PF15 correction this same
        day; user confirmed PD1 was the intended pin.

        New DIAGnostic:GPOut12 <0|1> / DIAGnostic:GPOut12? (commands.c/
        .h, cmd_parser.c) -- a plain HAL_GPIO_WritePin()/ReadPin() pair,
        not a new module (not enough behavior to justify one, matching
        gate_driver.c's own "GPIO config in main.c, logic wherever it's
        used" precedent). GPIO config: main.c's MX_GPIO_Init(), push-
        pull output, driven LOW before the pin is even enabled so it
        never glitches HIGH on boot. Generic -- nothing PF15-specific
        baked into the pin config itself, just today's reason for
        wanting it.

        REAL-HARDWARE VERIFICATION, once PD1 was looped to PF15 by the
        operator (confirmed working immediately -- EXTernal:INPut?
        tracked DIAGnostic:GPOut12 exactly, both directions):

        1. ARM gating (external-enable): with EXTernal:ENAble 1 and
           PF15 LOW, ARM correctly refused (ERR 13). Drove PF15 HIGH --
           ARM succeeded, STATE? -> ARMED.
        2. PID:PROFile:STARt's own re-check (closing the ARM-to-START
           gap): ARMED with PF15 HIGH, dropped PF15 LOW, PID:PROFile:
           STARt correctly refused with the new ERR 15 and stayed
           ARMED (not silently falling through to the generic ERR 12).
           Restored PF15 HIGH, retried -- succeeded, STATE? -> FIRING.
        3. FIRING-time fault + ramp-down: real 3-channel shot (600A/
           1000A/1400A, channels 1/2/3, open loop -- PID:LOOPMODE 0 0,
           the SAME test exercising the new ch=0-means-all convenience
           on real hardware for the first time), confirmed real output
           (16400/24000/... Hz matching setpoint) mid-flat-top, then
           dropped PF15 -- STATE? immediately reported "FAULT
           EXTERNAL_ENABLE" (not GENERAL -- confirms the distinct fault-
           type reporting works). Channel 1's output measured smoothly
           descending 16400 -> 6333 (at +0.51s post-drop) -> 5000/floor,
           running=0 (at +0.98s post-drop) -- matches
           FAULT_RAMP_DOWN_TIME_S=1.0s almost exactly, confirming
           HandleGeneralFault()'s ramp-down mechanism is genuinely being
           reused end to end for this new fault source, on real
           hardware, not just by code inspection.
        4. FAULT:CLEAR re-validation: attempted while PF15 was still
           LOW -- correctly refused (STATE? stayed FAULT EXTERNAL_
           ENABLE). Restored PF15 HIGH, retried -- succeeded, STATE? ->
           IDLE, FAULT? -> 0.
        5. External trigger, edge- not level-triggered: EXTernal:
           TRIGger 1 with PF15 already HIGH, ARM succeeded (baseline
           captured HIGH at arm time) -- confirmed NO spurious auto-fire
           over 0.5s with the signal just sitting HIGH (the exact
           "already-HIGH-at-arm-time must not look like an edge"
           behavior this feature's design depended on). Dropped PF15 to
           LOW while still ARMED -- STATE? correctly stayed ARMED, no
           fault (confirms the fault check really is FIRING-only, not
           also active while merely ARMED, matching direct instruction).
           Raised PF15 back to HIGH -- a genuine LOW-to-HIGH transition
           -- and STATE? immediately showed FIRING, real output
           confirmed at the correct setpoint (16400 Hz), with zero
           manual PID:PROFile:STARt ever sent for this shot.

        One real methodology snag hit and diagnosed correctly along the
        way, not a firmware bug: an early attempt to test the FIRING-
        time fault split the "fire" and "drop PF15 mid-shot" steps
        across two SEPARATE python/serial invocations -- the real
        wall-clock gap between those two separate tool calls (tool
        round-trip + reasoning time, not anything under firmware's
        control) exceeded the shot's own 5-second duration, so the
        shot had already completed normally before the second script
        even ran (STATE? IDLE, FAULT? 0 -- no fault, nothing wrong).
        Recognized immediately from the FAULT?=0 clean state (a real
        fault would have stayed latched), not assumed a bug -- redone
        as a single continuous script with explicit, controlled
        time.sleep() calls, exactly the same "one continuous script,
        no precisely-timed step split across tool calls" lesson this
        project has hit and recorded before (docs/changelog.txt,
        2026-09-15 General Fault entry).

        Both previous same-day entries below (external-enable interlock,
        external trigger) are now CONFIRMED, not just build-verified --
        their own "NOT YET VERIFIED ON REAL HARDWARE" status is closed
        by this entry.

  Verify: clean arm-none-eabi-gcc --clean build, zero warnings. Real
        hardware, as detailed above -- this entry IS the verification.

2026-09-16  —  External trigger added (rising edge on PF15 fires a shot
               while ARMED); PID:LOOPMODE now accepts ch=0 for "every
               channel" -- NOT YET VERIFIED ON REAL HARDWARE, board
               still unavailable
-----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Change:  Direct follow-up request, same session as the external-enable
        interlock just below: expand PF15's role so a rising edge, while
        ARMED, fires a shot -- "the same behavior as running
        PID:PROF:STAR when in closed loop operation, or our open loop
        start call when in open loop operation. Closed vs open loop
        should be set by SCPI command."

        Real clarification needed and resolved before writing any code:
        asked directly whether "our open loop start call" meant the
        legacy pfm.c TABLE:*/FIRE path (the only genuinely open-loop,
        zero-PID-involvement mechanism in this codebase), or something
        that didn't exist yet and would need building. User's answer
        revealed the real architecture: open- vs. closed-loop has
        ALWAYS been the per-channel PID:LOOPMODE flag, checked inside
        the SAME PID_Update() loop both PID:START and
        PID:PROFile:STARt already share -- there was never a second,
        structurally-different "open-loop start call" to invoke. Once
        that was clear, confirmed the design directly: the trigger
        always calls SM_Fire() (PID:PROFile:STARt's own function, the
        state-machine-gated path, not the ungated PID:START), and the
        "closed vs open loop, set by SCPI command" request becomes a
        plain convenience for setting every channel's PID:LOOPMODE at
        once, not a second code path the trigger branches on.

        PID:LOOPMODE (cmd_pid_loopmode(), commands.c): `ch=0` now means
        "every channel at once" -- reuses PID:LOG's own existing
        `ch=0`-means-all convention (added 2026-09-10, PID_ArmLogAll())
        rather than inventing a new command. Real channels are still
        1..HRTIM_NUM_CHANNELS as always.

        External trigger itself (state_machine.h/.c): reuses PF15, the
        SAME pin the external-enable interlock (below) already uses --
        not a second signal. Structurally DEPENDS on external-enable
        being on: SM_SetExternalTriggerRequired(1) refuses (new ERR 16)
        unless SM_GetExternalEnableRequired() is already 1, and turning
        external-enable back OFF forces trigger back off too --
        enforced both directions so an operator can never end up with
        triggering active while the "lose the signal -> fault"
        protection it depends on is silently disabled. Per direct
        instruction ("as usual, if we lose the enable signal we should
        interpret this as a general fault"), this is the EXISTING
        SM_FAULT_EXTERNAL_ENABLE mechanism (previous entry), reused
        as-is -- no second fault path.

        Edge detection, not level detection: a baseline PF15 level is
        captured fresh the instant SM_Arm() succeeds (so a signal
        already HIGH at the moment of arming doesn't look like a rising
        edge on the next check), then kept current by SM_PollFaults()
        on every call while still ARMED. Checked from INSIDE
        SM_PollFaults() itself (not a new, separate poll function) --
        shares that function's already-established call-site guarantee
        (main loop + every real PID_Update() tick) rather than adding a
        second entry point every caller would need to remember. While
        merely ARMED (not yet FIRING), the only caller actually reaching
        SM_PollFaults() is the main loop -- its own iteration rate is
        unbounded/undocumented (the SAME pre-existing "NOTE TO REVISIT"
        this project already carries for fault-poll cadence), so
        trigger latency while ARMED inherits that same limitation, not
        a new one. If the resulting SM_Fire() call itself fails (e.g.
        profile timing never set), the state simply stays ARMED with
        the baseline now recording HIGH -- a genuine falling-then-rising
        edge is needed to retry, not just PF15 remaining HIGH.

        New wire commands (commands.c/.h, cmd_parser.c): EXTernal:TRIGger
        <0|1>, EXTernal:TRIGger?. New ERR 16 (EXTernal:TRIGger refused,
        EXTernal:ENAble must be on first).

  Verify: clean arm-none-eabi-gcc build, zero warnings. *** NOT YET
        VERIFIED ON REAL HARDWARE *** -- board still unavailable this
        session (see the previous entry's own verify note -- same
        board, same reason). Nothing here has been exercised on actual
        silicon: not the ch=0 PID:LOOPMODE convenience, not the
        EXTernal:TRIGger refusal when EXTernal:ENAble is off, not a
        real rising edge actually firing a shot from ARMED, not the
        edge-vs-level distinction, not the arm-time baseline capture.
        Next session (or whenever the board is back): rebuild, reflash,
        and verify all of this alongside the external-enable checklist
        from the previous entry -- they share the same pin and can
        reasonably be tested together (bench jumper/switch to PF15,
        drive it through a realistic ARM -> rising edge -> FIRING ->
        drop -> FAULT -> restore -> FAULT:CLEAR sequence).

  Files:  Core/Inc/state_machine.h, Core/Src/state_machine.c,
        Core/Inc/commands.h, Core/Src/commands.c, Core/Src/cmd_parser.c,
        docs/command_reference.md

2026-09-16  —  External-enable interlock added (PF15, "Fiber_Enable"):
               new SM_FAULT_EXTERNAL_ENABLE fault type, EXTernal:ENAble
               commands -- NOT YET VERIFIED ON REAL HARDWARE, board
               unavailable this session
-----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Change:  Direct request: a config parameter to "set external enable
        true"/"set external enable false" on pin PC14 -- when true, the
        controller should refuse to output a shot unless that pin reads
        HIGH, and should register a fault if the signal is lost during
        a shot.

        Pin correction BEFORE any code was written: PC14 is documented
        in docs/pin_mapping_v4.csv as "STM_Enable_Pin", direction GPO
        (General Purpose Output) -- the STM32 DRIVES that pin outward
        per the schematic, the opposite direction needed for a signal
        the controller is supposed to READ. Flagged directly rather
        than silently reconfiguring a documented output pin as an
        input; user confirmed the real intended pin is PF15
        ("Fiber_Enable"), which IS documented GPI (input) in the same
        CSV -- the correct pin for this purpose.

        Implementation, in state_machine.c/.h (this project's existing
        top-level IDLE/ARMED/FIRING/FAULT machine -- the natural home,
        since this interlock directly gates ARM/FIRE and is itself a
        new fault source, exactly the two things that module already
        owns):
          - New SM_FAULT_EXTERNAL_ENABLE fault type -- system-wide, like
            SM_FAULT_GENERAL, and reuses its exact ramp-down response
            (HandleGeneralFault() called directly) -- reported as its
            own distinct type purely for operator diagnostics (STATE?
            -> "OK FAULT EXTERNAL_ENABLE"), not a different physical
            response.
          - ArmConditionsMet() (previously a pure stub, always returning
            1 -- see the 2026-09-13 entry) now has its FIRST real
            condition: refuses ARM unless the interlock is off, or on
            AND PF15 reads HIGH.
          - SM_Fire() (the ARMED -> FIRING transition, PID:PROFile:STARt)
            ALSO re-checks the interlock immediately before firing --
            closes a real gap where PF15 could drop in the window
            between a successful ARM and the eventual START; ARM alone
            does not guarantee the condition still holds at the actual
            moment of firing. cmd_pid_profile_start() (commands.c)
            checks this itself first, for a precise ERR 15, rather than
            relying on SM_Fire()'s own generic failure return --
            matching that function's existing "distinct failure
            reasons, reported distinctly" precedent for the
            PID:PROFILE:TIMING-not-set case.
          - SM_PollFaults() checks PF15 continuously, but ONLY while
            SM_STATE_FIRING -- per direct instruction ("if that input
            is lost during a shot"), NOT while merely IDLE/ARMED
            (nothing outputting yet to protect there; the next
            PID:PROFile:STARt attempt simply fails its own re-check
            instead). Whether ARMED should also actively fault on loss
            was NOT decided either way -- not asked for, flagged rather
            than silently added.
          - SM_ClearFault() re-validates PF15 is back HIGH before
            actually clearing an EXTERNAL_ENABLE fault, same "only
            clear if the condition is actually gone" treatment
            PC10/GateDriverStatus already get (see the 2026-09-15
            entry on that same philosophy).

        GPIO config: main.c's MX_GPIO_Init() (matching gate_driver.c's
        own established precedent -- plain-input GPIO config lives in
        main.c, the read/fault logic lives in the owning module, not a
        new hand-rolled Msp-init pattern). PF15 is GPIO_PULLDOWN, NOT
        this project's usual GPIO_NOPULL for actively-driven inputs
        (GateDriverStatus, PFM_Input, QUADSPI) -- a DELIBERATE fail-safe
        choice specific to this one signal: an unconnected/floating
        PF15 must read LOW (no permission granted), never an undefined
        level that could accidentally read HIGH and silently permit
        firing. GateDriverStatus's own NOPULL is fine on its pins
        because floating-reads-as-fault is already the safe direction
        there; the same reasoning would be UNSAFE here, where
        floating-reads-as-enabled would be the dangerous direction
        instead. Plain polled input, no EXTI -- SM_PollFaults()'s own
        existing cadence (main loop + every real PID_Update() tick,
        ~1kHz while FIRING) was judged sufficient; an interrupt-driven
        path was considered and not built, flagged as a real decision
        to revisit if tighter latency ever matters, not silently
        assumed already covered.

        New wire commands (commands.c/.h, cmd_parser.c): EXTernal:ENAble
        <0|1>, EXTernal:ENAble?, EXTernal:INPut? (raw PF15 level,
        independent of whether the interlock is on -- same diagnostic
        role PFMIN:DEBUG:RAW?/REG? played for the fiber-patching
        investigation, previous entry). Two ':'-levels (not one
        compound "EXTEnable" token) specifically so "ENAble"/"INPut"
        each get their own independent SCPI short-form abbreviation
        (EXT:ENA / EXT:INP) -- a single compound word would only let
        the unrecognizable fragment "EXTE" be typed as a short form,
        since this project's scpi_token_match() only recognizes a
        LEADING uppercase run, not capitals resuming mid-word. New
        ERR 15 (PID:PROFile:STARt refused, interlock on + PF15 LOW).
        STATE? extended for the new fault type.

  Verify: clean arm-none-eabi-gcc build, zero warnings. *** NOT YET
        VERIFIED ON REAL HARDWARE *** -- the board's USB-serial adapter
        was unavailable for the rest of this session (unplugged,
        "won't be for a while," per direct statement). Nothing in this
        entry has been exercised on actual silicon: not the GPIO_PULLDOWN
        default, not ARM/PID:PROFile:STARt actually refusing, not a
        real FIRING-time fault triggering the ramp-down, not
        FAULT:CLEAR's re-validation. Next session (or whenever the
        board is back): rebuild, reflash, and work through the same
        kind of real-hardware verification this project's other fault
        types got (OCP:TEST:FAULT/GENERAL:TEST:FAULT-style software
        injection isn't applicable here since PF15 is a REAL external
        signal, not something to fake in software -- verification needs
        either real external hardware driving PF15, or a bench jumper/
        switch to ground/3V3 to simulate it manually).

2026-09-15  —  GENERAL:TEST:FAULT added; PFM_Input channels 2/3's
               measuredHz feedback investigated end to end -- root
               cause found to be a physical fiber-patching mix-up on
               the bench, NOT a firmware bug, and fixed by re-patching
-----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Change:  Direct request: "Run a series of tests without the DSLogic
        with faults at various times. Check the results from the
        measured PFM's ... and check the fault response times." Two
        things needed before that test battery could run at all:

        1. GENERAL:TEST:FAULT -- new software fault-injection command
        (SM_ReportGeneralFault(), state_machine.h/.c; cmd_general_test_fault(),
        commands.c/.h; cmd_parser.c), the General-Fault counterpart to
        the existing OCP:TEST:FAULT. No real General Fault trigger
        (PC10/HRTIM1_FLT6, GateDriverStatus) had ever been exercised at
        an operator-chosen moment (e.g. mid-ramp-up) -- only steady-
        state/flat-top, via OCP:TEST:FAULT's reuse of the same ramp
        machinery. Verified on real hardware: IDLE-state case (immediate
        fault, clean FAULT:CLEAR) and a FIRING-state case (fired mid-
        ramp-up on a 4-channel shot, confirmed via STATE? still FIRING
        immediately before the command; all 4 channels' knee points
        landed within 0.06ms of each other, confirming a genuine
        system-wide simultaneous trigger, ramp-to-floor duration matched
        FAULT_RAMP_DOWN_TIME_S almost exactly). A real test-methodology
        lesson surfaced and recorded along the way: status-polling
        before a precisely-timed trigger command drifts the real
        trigger instant later than intended (serial round-trip latency,
        not a firmware issue) -- confirmed via DSLogic capture that the
        fault landed ~163ms into flat-top instead of the intended
        ~0.5s-into-ramp; fixed methodology (single wall-clock sleep, no
        polling before the timed command) for the OCP work earlier this
        session; the General Fault test above still shows some of the
        same drift (targeted ~0.5s, landed ~1.16s -- past the 1.0s
        ramp) despite that lesson, worth remembering it needs re-
        applying every time, not just once.

        2. PFM_Input measuredHz investigation. The requested test
        battery needed WHAM channels 2/3's real-hardware feedback
        (PID:STATus?'s measuredHz, backed by pfm_input.c's continuous-
        mode capture) to be trustworthy -- it was not: both read 0
        persistently, regardless of confirmed-correct real HRTIM
        output on each channel (channel 1 worked throughout; channel 4/
        Phase X has no PFM_Input measurement wiring at all, out of
        scope per direct user statement).

        Investigated in order, each step with real evidence, each
        candidate explanation ruled out before moving to the next:
          - NOT a serial-polling timing artifact -- confirmed via
            careful wall-clock-timed isolated single-channel tests,
            repeated for ch2/ch3/ch4 independently, each showing
            correct setpoint/output ramp/hold/ramp-down but measuredHz
            stuck at 0 on every single poll, the whole shot, every time.
          - NOT a firmware code bug -- added two TEMPORARY diagnostic
            commands specifically for this: PFMIN:DEBUG:RAW? <ch>
            (non-destructive read of pfm_input.c's internal per-channel
            accumulator/flags -- does NOT reset avgCount like
            PID:STATus?'s own consumption does, so repeated polling can
            watch it accumulate, or not, live) and PFMIN:DEBUG:REG?
            <ch> (raw TIMx peripheral + GPIO register readback --
            CR1/CCER/DIER/SR/CNT/CCRx/CCMR1/MODER/AFR/IDR -- bypassing
            every software layer entirely). Results for ch2 (PD4/
            TIM2_CH2) and ch3 (PB2/TIM5_CH1): haveFirstRise NEVER
            became 1 (the capture ISR never saw one edge, the whole
            shot); CR1=1 (counter genuinely running); CCER=0x11 (both
            capture channels hardware-enabled, correct); CCMR1=0x101
            (IC2 correctly mapped DIRECTLY to TI2, ruling out a
            channel-mapping bug); MODER=2/AFR=2 (GPIO genuinely in
            Alternate Function mode, correct AF number, exactly
            matching pfm_input.c's own kDesc[] table); CCR stuck at
            literal 0x00000000, never latching; IDR (the pin's live
            logic level, read directly, zero software interpretation)
            read 0 on every single sample. Every firmware-controlled
            register was correct and symmetric with working channel 1
            -- nothing left in software to blame. Cross-checked via
            `git diff` between a real 2026-09-13 reference shot (a
            DSLogic-vs-firmware comparison plot the user supplied,
            showing ch3's measured genuinely tracking setpoint on THIS
            SAME code) and current HEAD: zero changes to pfm_input.c or
            to pid.c's PfmInput_StartContinuous() call site in that
            whole window -- ruling out a code regression outright.
          - NOT a stuck-DMA-state-from-repeated-soft-reflash artifact
            -- a full controller power cycle (true POR, not the
            bootloader's warm jump-and-reflash used ~8 times this
            session) produced an identical symptom afterward.
          - NOT a wiring continuity gap -- user directly confirmed
            continuity end to end.
          - NOT a dead/unpowered/unseated fiber-optic receiver -- user
            directly confirmed receiver power and fiber seating/latch
            on physical re-inspection.

        ROOT CAUSE, found by frequency-fingerprinting: widened
        PFMIN:DEBUG:RAW?/REG? to accept all 6 physical PFM_Input ports
        (PFM_INPUT_NUM_CHANNELS) instead of being capped at
        HRTIM_NUM_CHANNELS=4 (a real self-inflicted bug caught and
        fixed BEFORE it shipped: a replace_all edit accidentally
        widened FOURTEEN unrelated commands' own per-channel bounds
        checks the same way -- e.g. PID:STATus?/PID:CHANnel:ENAble
        would have silently accepted nonexistent channels 5/6 --
        reverted surgically back to HRTIM_NUM_CHANNELS everywhere
        except the 3 commands that genuinely operate on all 6 physical
        ports: PFMIN:DATA?, PFMIN:DEBUG:RAW?, PFMIN:DEBUG:REG?; also
        fixed those two's error message/code to correctly say "Invalid
        PFM_Input channel (1-6)" / ERR 8, not the stale "Invalid PID
        channel" / ERR 11 text copied from OCP:TEST:FAULT's own
        precedent). Drove a 3-channel shot at three distinct, easily-
        recognizable commanded frequencies (600A/1000A/1400A ->
        16400/24000/31600 Hz) and read every physical port's real
        captured frequency: PFM_Input_02 was receiving Phase W's real
        31610 Hz signal (not Phase V's own 24000 Hz), PFM_Input_03 was
        receiving nothing at all, and PFM_Input_04 (a port firmware
        doesn't even normally read) was receiving Phase V's real
        24004 Hz signal. This is a physical fiber-PATCHING mix-up, not
        a receiver failure and not firmware -- Phase V's fiber was
        plugged into port 04 instead of 02, Phase W's fiber was plugged
        into port 02 instead of 03, and port 03 was genuinely empty.
        Guided the user through two re-patch iterations (first attempt
        landed Phase V on port 03 instead of 02, leaving ports 02/03
        simply swapped with each other; second attempt, crossing those
        two specific fibers, landed everything correctly) -- each
        re-verified via the same frequency-fingerprinting technique
        before declaring success. Final state: PFM_Input_01/02/03 read
        16403/24004/31610 Hz exactly matching Phase U/V/W's own
        commanded values, and PID:STATus?'s measuredHz for all 3 WHAM
        channels now correctly tracks each channel's own setpoint --
        the original symptom that triggered this whole investigation.

  Verify: clean arm-none-eabi-gcc build (zero warnings) after every
        change in this entry. GENERAL:TEST:FAULT verified on real
        hardware as described above. The PFM_Input fix was verified
        entirely through its own investigation process (the frequency-
        fingerprinting IS the verification) -- re-confirmed with a
        fresh 3-channel shot after the final re-patch showing all three
        channels' measuredHz matching setpoint within measurement noise.

2026-09-15  —  OCP derate step now bypasses the slew-rate clamp -- lands
               on the literal (100*1/N)% target instead of a smaller,
               clamp-limited step
-----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Change:  Direct request, in response to "double check the percentage
        by which the non-OCP-faulted channels reduce their outputs. In
        the case of 3 output channels outputting and one faulting, we
        should see the non faulted channels each reduce their overall
        output frequency by ~33% to compensate." Re-analyzed both the
        code and the previously-captured DSLogic data (same 3-channel,
        N=3 shot from earlier today) and confirmed the derate step was
        NOT hitting the literal target: PID_BeginOvercurrentRampDown()'s
        derateFactor math correctly computes 0.6667 for N=3, but the
        result was passed through the SAME hard slew-rate clamp
        (ClampOutputSlew(), 2000 Hz/tick) as every other output write in
        pid.c -- so the actual step was whatever the clamp allowed, not
        the computed target, e.g. channel 1 (Phase U): 16420 Hz ->
        measured ~14400 Hz (target was ~10933 Hz); channel 3 (Phase W):
        31645 Hz -> measured ~29600 Hz (target was ~21067 Hz). After
        that one clamped tick the channel doesn't hold at any derated
        value at all -- it immediately proceeds into the existing ramp-
        to-floor (PID_BeginFaultRampDown()), so the 33%-derated value
        was never actually reached, just transited through incidentally.
        This was a real, deliberately-flagged tension from when the OCP
        feature was first built earlier today (see this file's OCP
        entry, and pid.h/state_machine.h's own comments at the time).
        Presented the finding with a comparison table and asked directly
        how it should behave; user chose "bypass the clamp for this one
        step" over "keep it slew-clamped."

        Fix: new ClampOutputRangeOnly() (pid.c, static) -- range clamp
        to [PID_OUTPUT_MIN_HZ, PID_OUTPUT_MAX_HZ] ONLY, deliberately no
        slew limiting. This is the FIRST exception to ClampOutputSlew()
        anywhere in this file. Used for exactly one write:
        PID_BeginOvercurrentRampDown()'s derate step (replacing
        ClampOutputSlew(desiredHz, lastOutputHz) with
        ClampOutputRangeOnly(desiredHz)) -- every other output write in
        pid.c is untouched and still goes through the full slew clamp.
        Justification, documented at length in ClampOutputRangeOnly()'s
        own comment: the slew clamp exists specifically to prevent a
        real, DSLogic-confirmed hardware glitch (2026-09-10 entry) --
        that risk is not waived lightly, but OCP is already the faster,
        more aggressive fault path (the faulted channel itself is hard-
        disabled with no slew limit either), so a single larger step on
        the survivors is consistent with that same urgency, not a new
        risk class. pid.h and state_machine.h's doc comments updated to
        match (previously described the OLD, slew-clamped behavior and
        flagged it as an unresolved tension -- now resolved).

  Verify: clean arm-none-eabi-gcc build, zero warnings. Rebuilt,
        reflashed, re-ran the same real-hardware pattern as the earlier
        OCP entry (3-channel open-loop shot, 600A/1000A/1400A on
        channels 1/2/3, channel 4 disabled, OCP:TEST:FAULT 2 mid-flat-
        top) and polled PID:STATus? repeatedly post-fault. Confirmed:
        channel 2 (faulted) hard-disabled immediately, state/output
        frozen at its pre-fault value (not touched by the derate/ramp
        code, as designed); channels 1/3 (survivors) observed already
        descending from their derated starting points toward the floor
        within the first serial round-trip after the fault (e.g.
        channel 1: 16400 Hz pre-fault -> 9679 Hz observed ~0.3-0.7s
        post-fault, consistent with a ramp that started from the
        literal ~10933 Hz derated target, not the ~14400 Hz clamp-
        limited value seen before this fix) -- STATE? showed
        "FAULT OVERCURRENT 2" throughout, FAULT:CLEAR returned cleanly
        to IDLE. The true single-tick instantaneous value (the derated
        target held for exactly one PID_Update() tick, up to 1ms, before
        ProcessFaultRampDown() starts moving it toward the floor) is
        confirmed by CODE INSPECTION -- PID_BeginOvercurrentRampDown()
        writes st->lastOutputHz to the range-clamped-only derate target
        and calls PID_BeginFaultRampDown() (which captures that exact
        value as faultRampStartHz) WITHOUT invoking ProcessFaultRampDown()
        itself; the next write only happens on the following real tick.
        This single-tick value cannot be captured over this serial link
        (round-trip latency is two to three orders of magnitude slower
        than one 1kHz tick) -- a DSLogic re-capture, with microsecond-
        scale real edges, would be the way to see it directly if ever
        wanted; not done this round (not yet asked for).

2026-09-15  —  OCP slew-clamp-bypass fix independently confirmed on real
               hardware via a fresh DSLogic capture -- derate step lands
               within ~0.2ms of the literal (100*1/N)% target, not the
               old clamp-limited value
-----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Change:  Direct follow-up request ("let's run a fresh dslogic
        capture") to independently verify the slew-clamp-bypass fix
        from this same day's immediately preceding entry, the same way
        the original OCP feature itself was verified -- real DSLogic
        data, not just firmware self-report.

        Real methodology issue found and fixed FIRST, worth recording:
        the first attempt (operator manually armed DSLogic, "fire when
        ready") used a test script that polled PID:STATus? on all three
        channels via slow serial round-trips BEFORE triggering the
        fault -- each round-trip took far longer in real wall-clock
        time than intended (serial read-timeout mechanics, not a
        firmware issue), so real elapsed time between PID:PROFILE:START
        and the eventual OCP:TEST:FAULT command drifted well past what
        the script's own sleep() calls suggested. A quick diagnostic
        confirmed the shot had very likely already auto-completed
        (STATE? back to IDLE) before the fault command was even sent --
        meaning that capture would have shown a normal completed shot
        followed by an isolated fault-disable event, not the intended
        fault-during-FIRING derate+ramp behavior. Caught before wasting
        the user's time decoding it: fixed the test methodology (single
        wall-clock-timed sleep, no status polling before the fault
        command, confirmed via a dry run that STATE? still read FIRING
        immediately before the fault) and asked the operator to re-arm
        DSLogic for a clean re-fire, rather than assuming the first
        capture was good.

        Second attempt (corrected timing): PID:PROFILE:START, an exact
        2.5s wall-clock sleep (comfortably inside the 1s-ramp/3s-flat-
        top window, confirmed STATE? still FIRING right before the
        fault), OCP:TEST:FAULT 2 sent immediately with no intervening
        commands. Same real-hardware pattern as the original OCP
        verification: 3-channel open-loop shot, 600A/1000A/1400A on
        channels 1/2/3, channel 4 disabled.

        Decoded with the SAME corrected complementary-pair channel
        mapping established earlier this day (DSLogic Ch0/2/4 <-> WHAM
        channels 1/2/3, the non-inverted phase of each pair) -- a fresh
        streaming/chunked decoder (75 blocks/channel, 1.25B samples/
        channel at 250MHz/8ch, same multi-block-file handling as
        before) extracted rising-edge timestamps for the 3 active
        channels.

        RESULTS -- the fix is confirmed, cleanly and quantitatively:
          - WHAM Ch1 (Phase U): pre-fault 16426.0 Hz steady state.
            Literal 33% derate target = 16426.0 * (1 - 1/3) = 10950.7 Hz.
            First edge after the fault instant (at +0.19ms): 10951.0 Hz
            -- within 0.3 Hz of the exact target. (Old, slew-clamped
            behavior, from the earlier same-day capture: ~14400 Hz --
            nowhere close.)
          - WHAM Ch3 (Phase W): pre-fault 31654.7 Hz. Literal target =
            31654.7 * (1 - 1/3) = 21103.1 Hz. First edge after the fault
            (at +0.09ms): 21100.6 Hz -- within 2.5 Hz of the exact
            target. (Old behavior: ~29600 Hz.)
          - WHAM Ch2 (Phase V, the faulted channel): pre-fault 24038.3
            Hz, then ZERO edges recorded anywhere after the fault
            instant for the rest of the 5-second capture -- confirms
            the immediate hard disable, no ramp, exactly as designed.
          - Both survivors then descend smoothly from their derated
            starting points and reach the 5000 Hz floor (PFM_TURNON_FREQ_HZ)
            at almost exactly 1.0s after the fault (0.9985s and 0.9986s
            respectively -- matching FAULT_RAMP_DOWN_TIME_S=1.0f to
            within a fraction of a percent), then go completely silent
            (no further edges) -- confirms the ramp-down duration and
            the final disable, both on the real physical output,
            independent of any firmware self-report.

        Two verification plots generated and shared: a zoomed view of
        the first 10ms (shows the instant derate step landing on the
        literal target line) and a full 1.05s view of the complete
        ramp-to-floor for all three channels.

  Verify: this entry's own DSLogic capture/decode/plot IS the
        verification -- no further action needed. Board confirmed
        clean (STATE? IDLE, FAULT? 0, all channels/loop-modes restored)
        after both the discarded first attempt and the real capture.

2026-09-15  —  OCP fault fix independently confirmed on real hardware
               via DSLogic; dslogic_shot_capture.py's WHAM<->DSLogic
               channel mapping corrected -- it was wrong all session
               (and very likely explains the earlier "Ch2/Ch3 wiring
               swap" finding too)
-----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Change:  Direct request: a real 3-second shot (channels 1/2/3, open
        loop, 600A/1000A/1400A) with the operator manually arming
        DSLogic and a mock OCP fault (`OCP:TEST:FAULT 2`) triggered
        mid-flat-top, specifically to close the "not yet scope/DSLogic-
        confirmed" gap this project's own memory had flagged against
        the frozen-ramp bugfix from this same day's earlier entry.

        Decoded the resulting multi-block .dsl capture (75 blocks/
        channel, 1.25B samples/channel, 250MHz/8ch -- too large to
        unpack fully into memory at once; dsl_viewer.py's own
        `DslCapture` explicitly rejects multi-block files for exactly
        this reason) with a purpose-built streaming decoder: one block
        (2MiB) at a time, unpack -> find edges via a vectorized diff ->
        discard, keeping only edge timestamps -- peak memory bounded to
        one block regardless of total capture length, plus a fast-path
        that skips fully-constant blocks without unpacking at all.

        First pass (channels 0-3 only, using the WHAM_TO_DSLOGIC_CHANNEL
        mapping this file has used all session, {1:0, 2:1, 3:2, 4:3})
        produced a confusing result: channels 0/1 showed IDENTICAL
        frequency trajectories, channels 2/3 ALSO showed identical
        trajectories (both pairs matching down to sub-1% precision),
        and channel 3's own real commanded frequency (31600 Hz) never
        appeared on ANY of the 4 probed channels at all. Reported this
        honestly as an apparent bench wiring/probe issue rather than
        assuming the fix was still broken (the fix's own signature --
        smooth continuous ramp-down vs. instant clean silence -- was
        still clearly, unambiguously present in the data either way).

        THE ACTUAL EXPLANATION, confirmed by the user immediately after:
        this board's real DSLogic wiring is 8 channels for 4 WHAM
        channels, not 4-for-4 -- Ch0/Ch1 = Phase U/UN, Ch2/Ch3 = Phase
        V/VN, Ch4/Ch5 = Phase W/WN, Ch6/Ch7 = Phase X/XN, i.e. each
        WHAM/HRTIM channel's COMPLEMENTARY output pair (HRTIM1_Set-
        ChannelOutputEnable()'s own "output-1-ACTIVE/output-2-INACTIVE"
        design) is wired out to two ADJACENT DSLogic channels, each
        carrying the same frequency. The "duplication" was never a
        wiring defect -- it was the correct, expected behavior of a
        complementary pair, decoded through a wrong assumed mapping.
        Re-decoded channels 4-7 with the corrected understanding: Phase
        W (channel 4/5, WHAM channel 3) shows the exact same clean
        ramp-up -> flat -> smooth-ramp-down-to-floor shape as channel 1,
        ending at the same time; Phase X (channel 6/7, WHAM channel 4,
        never enabled this shot) shows ZERO edges across the entire
        5-second capture. Every one of the 3 simultaneously-active WHAM
        channels, all three genuinely different commanded frequencies,
        now decodes correctly and unambiguously -- see the plots sent
        to the user this session for the full, clean picture.

        This strongly implicates the earlier, separately-documented
        "Ch2/Ch3 DSLogic wiring swap, confirmed 4 times" finding
        (2026-09-13/09-14, dslogic-tool project memory) as the SAME
        complementary-pair confusion, misread as a physical swap, not a
        real bench defect -- that investigation only ever ran 2-channel
        tests (one pair active, one pair disabled), which can't
        distinguish "swapped" from "duplicated-via-wrong-mapping" the
        way today's 3-distinct-frequency test could. Not provably the
        same root cause without re-running that exact test with the
        corrected mapping, but treated as the working hypothesis going
        forward rather than a still-open physical-wiring todo.

        Fixed `python/dslogic_shot_capture.py`'s WHAM_TO_DSLOGIC_CHANNEL
        -- {1:0, 2:1, 3:2, 4:3} -> {1:0, 2:2, 3:4, 4:6} -- and its
        module docstring's wiring description. This is the mapping the
        LIVE per-shot DSLogic cross-check (wired into `shot`,
        wham_console.py) has been using ALL SESSION -- every previous
        DSLogic-cross-check plot this session used the wrong channels
        for WHAM channels 2/3/4 (channel 1/Phase U was accidentally
        correct, since Ch0 was never part of any pair confusion).
        Confirmed the underlying capture library (`~/dslogic-tool`'s
        `set_enabled_channels()`) has no contiguous-channel assumption,
        so pointing it at {0,2,4,6} instead of {0,1,2,3} needed no other
        changes. Deliberately still captures only the non-inverted
        phase of each pair (U/V/W/X, not UN/VN/WN/XN) -- confirmed
        today they carry identical frequency information, so capturing
        both would be redundant, not additional verification.

  Verify: python3 -m py_compile. Not yet re-run through a live `shot`
        with the corrected mapping this session (today's verification
        used a standalone script + a manually-armed DSLogic capture,
        not the wham_console.py `shot` command's own automatic
        cross-check plot) -- the next real `shot` with DSLogic connected
        will be the first real exercise of the corrected mapping through
        that actual code path.

2026-09-15  —  OCP (per-channel overcurrent) fault populated: isolate +
               derate + ramp; found and fixed TWO real, pre-existing
               fault-handling bugs while verifying it on real hardware
               (verified working end-to-end on real hardware)
-----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Change:  Direct request: populate SM_FAULT_OVERCURRENT (left as a stub
        alongside General Fault, 2026-09-13). Exact behavior specified:
        on channel N's OCP fault pin tripping, immediately disable that
        channel's HRTIM output, simultaneously decrease every OTHER
        enabled channel's output by (100 * 1/N)% (N = channels enabled
        at the fault instant, the faulted one included), and ramp the
        shot down with the faulted channel excluded. Real per-channel
        OCP pin mapping explicitly deferred ("we will define the
        mapping here at a later time").

        state_machine.h/.c: new SM_ReportOcpFault(channel) -- the real
        entry point whatever eventually detects a per-channel OCP
        condition (a future EXTI ISR once pins are defined) is meant to
        call; new SM_GetFaultChannel() (STATE? now reports
        "OK FAULT OVERCURRENT <ch>", 1-based, in addition to the
        existing "OK FAULT GENERAL"). EnterFault() extended to carry a
        channel alongside the fault type. A second SM_ReportOcpFault()
        call while already faulted does NOT re-run the redistribute-
        and-ramp sequence (only makes sense once) but still always
        hard-disables its own channel -- true simultaneous multi-channel
        OCP is flagged as a real open question, not silently resolved
        either way (the literal single-channel case this was specified
        for is fully correct).

        pid.h/.c: new PID_BeginOvercurrentRampDown(channel) -- disables
        `channel` immediately (PID_SetChannelEnable(), the same path
        `enable <ch> off` uses -- no ramp for it at all), steps every
        OTHER enabled channel down by the derate factor (one-time write,
        still through the existing ClampOutputSlew() -- see its own doc
        comment for a real, deliberately-not-silently-resolved tension:
        a large derate on a channel near PFM_MAX_FREQ_HZ can't actually
        land in one tick under the existing 2000 Hz/tick slew clamp,
        despite being asked for "simultaneously" -- kept the clamp
        active rather than carve out the first exception to it anywhere
        in this file), then hands off to the EXISTING, unmodified
        PID_BeginFaultRampDown() to ramp the survivors on down exactly
        like General Fault, from their newly-derated starting point.

        commands.c/.h, cmd_parser.c: new OCP:TEST:FAULT <ch> -- software
        fault injection (calls SM_ReportOcpFault(ch-1) directly), added
        specifically because no real OCP pin exists yet to trigger this
        with -- same TEMPORARY-debug-command precedent as PFMIN:DMASTAT?
        /qspi_test.c. Not gated/dangerous in the operator-console sense
        -- triggering a fault only ever stops/reduces output, the same
        "safe direction" reasoning FAULT:CLEAR/PID:STOP already get.

  Two REAL, PRE-EXISTING bugs found and fixed while verifying this on
  real hardware -- neither is new; both predate this session and were
  masked because General Fault's own ramp-down had never actually been
  exercised on real hardware before (every prior changelog entry for it
  says "real-hardware verification PENDING -- board still disconnected"):

  Bug 1 -- fault ramp-down was silently frozen on real hardware, for
  BOTH fault types: EnterFault() (state_machine.c) called the FULL
  PFM_ForceStop() unconditionally, for every fault, before either fault
  handler even ran. PFM_ForceStop() calls HRTIM1_PWM_Stop(), which
  stops the shared HRTIM Master counter BY DESIGN (see its own comment:
  prevents the Master-repetition ISR from firing forever after a normal
  stop) -- but that is the EXACT SAME counter PID_Update() needs to keep
  firing to drive ProcessFaultRampDown()/the new OCP equivalent forward,
  one tick at a time, for up to FAULT_RAMP_DOWN_TIME_S more seconds.
  Confirmed on real hardware with a temporary debug query
  (PID_DebugRampStat(), removed once resolved): every surviving
  channel's SOFTWARE ramp state correctly interpolated and correctly
  WROTE new shadow-register values every call, but the elapsed-ticks
  counter never advanced past 0 -- PID_Update() was never called again
  at all. The REAL PHYSICAL output would have frozen at a static level
  (whatever the counter's position was at the instant of the fault),
  not smoothly ramped to zero, for the entire ramp window -- the exact
  opposite of General/Overcurrent Fault's whole documented point.
  gate_driver.c's GateDriver_CheckFault() had the identical issue for
  its OWN PFM_ForceStop() call, run from the EXTI ISR the instant a
  real GateDriverStatus pin trips -- BEFORE state_machine.c's EnterFault()
  even runs, so fixing EnterFault() alone would not have fixed a REAL
  GateDriverStatus-triggered General Fault, only OCP:TEST:FAULT (which
  doesn't route through gate_driver.c at all).

  Fix: new PFM_ForceStopSoft() (pfm.c/pfm.h) -- identical bookkeeping to
  PFM_ForceStop() WITHOUT the HRTIM1_PWM_Stop() call. PFM_ForceStop()
  itself refactored to call it internally (zero behavior change for
  every OTHER existing caller -- pfm.c's own 2 internal early-stop
  cases, GateDriver_FaultClear() -- all unaffected). Used conditionally
  at exactly the two call sites that needed it: EnterFault() uses the
  soft stop when g_stateBeforeFault == SM_STATE_FIRING (a ramp might
  begin); GateDriver_CheckFault() uses it when PID_IsRunning() (pid.c
  currently has an active shot) -- both fall back to the full stop
  otherwise (IDLE/ARMED, or the legacy pfm.c TABLE:FIRE path is what's
  actually active), unchanged from today. PC10/HRTIM1_FLT6 investigated
  and confirmed NOT affected -- that path forces outputs safe
  autonomously in silicon (hrtim.h's own documented design), independent
  of counter state, so General Fault via PC10 was never relying on the
  counter staying alive for output safety in the first place (though its
  own software ramp math is equally moot there, since hardware has
  already zeroed the output by the time software runs).

  Bug 2 -- FAULT:CLEAR could never actually clear a GateDriverStatus (or
  likely PC10) fault at all: SM_ClearFault()'s own long-standing comment
  claimed "HRTIM1_FaultClear()/GateDriver_FaultClear() above already
  force-stop HRTIM unconditionally" -- but a full search confirmed
  NEITHER function is called ANYWHERE in this codebase. Concretely:
  GateDriver_FaultClear() is the only thing that ever resets
  g_gdsFaultLatched back to 0 (gate_driver.c) -- since nothing called
  it, GateDriver_FaultIsLatched() would stay 1 forever once a
  GateDriverStatus fault ever latched, and SM_ClearFault()'s own
  re-check would see it "still faulted" on every single FAULT:CLEAR
  attempt, forever -- a full power cycle would be the only recovery.
  Found while investigating Bug 1 above (same area, unrelated root
  cause) -- fixed alongside it rather than left for later now that it's
  found. Fix: SM_ClearFault() now actually calls HRTIM1_FaultClear()
  and GateDriver_FaultClear() (after the existing unconditional
  PID_Stop(), so hardware is already fully stopped by the time they
  run) before its own re-check -- exactly what the comment already
  (wrongly) claimed was happening.

  Verify: python3 -m py_compile-equivalent (clean arm-none-eabi-gcc
        build, zero warnings) after every change in this entry. Full
        real-hardware verification, repeated across 3 build/reflash
        cycles as each bug was found and fixed: a real 3-channel open-
        loop shot (600A/1000A/1400A demand, channels 1/2/3, channel 4
        disabled), OCP:TEST:FAULT 2 mid-flat-top. Confirmed via
        PID:DEBUG:RAMPSTAT? (temporary, removed) that elapsed ticks
        genuinely progressed tick-by-tick to completion (0 -> 211 -> 444
        -> 676 -> 908 -> done, matching real elapsed wall time) once
        both fixes landed -- frozen at 0 forever before them. Confirmed
        channel 2 hard-disabled immediately (PID:CHANnel:ENAble? 0
        throughout); channels 1/3 smoothly stepped down and ramped to
        the PFM_TURNON_FREQ_HZ floor (16400->12360->10170->7989->5808->
        5000 and 31600->23966->18210->12527->6820->5000); STATE? showed
        "FAULT OVERCURRENT 2" throughout and FAULT:CLEAR correctly
        returned to IDLE. Also verified the IDLE-fault edge case
        (OCP:TEST:FAULT with nothing FIRING -- immediate disable +
        FAULT, no ramp needed, matches PID_BeginFaultRampDown()'s own
        existing "nothing to ramp" path) and the derate-math edge case
        (N channels enabled including the faulted one, matching "at the
        time of the fault" literally). Board confirmed clean (STATE?
        IDLE, FAULT? 0, all channels re-enabled/restored) after every
        test. Not verified: a REAL per-channel OCP pin trip (no
        hardware exists yet -- OCP:TEST:FAULT is the verification path
        until it does); true simultaneous multi-channel OCP (flagged as
        an open question, not this session's scope); Bug 2's fix
        against a REAL GateDriverStatus/PC10 fault specifically (no way
        to trigger either without physical access to those pins this
        session -- fixed by code inspection + the missing-call-site
        confirmation, not re-verified against a live trip).

2026-09-14  —  wham_llm_console.py: default model switched qwen3:4b ->
               qwen3:1.7b (4-9x faster, real benchmark) -- with a
               newly-found, not-fully-resolved reliability caveat
-----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Change:  Direct request: "can any optimizations be made to make the
        llm respond even faster? Not sure if we could use a quantized
        model or similar?"

        On quantization specifically: both qwen3:4b and qwen3:1.7b are
        ALREADY Q4_K_M (4-bit) -- `ollama pull` uses this by default,
        not full precision. Tried pulling more aggressive tags
        (qwen3:4b-q4_0, qwen3:4b-q3_K_M) directly from Ollama's library
        -- neither exists; only one quant is published for this model.
        Not pursued further (hand-importing a GGUF from elsewhere) --
        diminishing returns expected against the much bigger lever
        below.

        Real head-to-head benchmark instead, same real system prompt
        and real requests against real hardware: qwen3:1.7b (really a
        2.0B-parameter checkpoint per `ollama show`) vs the previous
        default qwen3:4b -- 9.6s vs 41.6s on a simple health check
        (4.3x), 3.3GB vs 5.1GB memory footprint, and on the "generate a
        report" recipe that qwen3:4b's own two real attempts (see this
        file's earlier 2026-09-14 speedup entry) never once called the
        `report` tool for, qwen3:1.7b called it correctly. Presented
        this to the user with the tradeoff found on a THIRD test (a
        complex multi-parameter shot setup): qwen3:1.7b's own reply
        text said "I'll start output without your go-ahead" -- a
        notably more cavalier tone than qwen3:4b's equivalent replies
        ("Confirm to proceed?"). NOT an actual safety gap -- the real
        gate (Executor.is_dangerous()) is enforced in code, independent
        of what the model's own text claims, and it still asked for
        real y/N confirmation before PID:PROFile:STARt regardless --
        but a real behavioral difference worth knowing before
        switching. User chose to switch the default anyway.

        DEFAULT_MODEL -> qwen3:1.7b. Header docstring rewritten with
        the full benchmark, the quantization finding, and the tone
        caveat -- both models remain fully supported/documented
        (`--model qwen3:4b` or live `/model qwen3:4b` to switch back),
        this isn't "one replaced the other," it's a documented choice
        with real numbers behind it either way.

        A SECOND reliability finding surfaced during final verification
        AFTER the switch was made, not before -- disclosed to the user
        immediately rather than left undocumented: re-running the exact
        same safe health-check prompt twice against the real console
        (not a raw API probe) showed real run-to-run inconsistency.
        Run 1: answered "healthy" with ZERO actions executed at all --
        confirmed via the transcript, no `$ status`/`$ FAULT?` lines,
        a pure assertion with no verification, directly against the
        system prompt's own "NEVER invent command results... query it
        first" instruction. Run 2: DID call status/FAULT?, but stated
        its conclusion in the SAME round's "say" text before the
        results came back, then repeated essentially the same
        conclusion in a second round after seeing them -- better, but
        still shows a tendency to assert first and verify second (or
        not at all) rather than the other way around. Both runs
        happened to land on the actually-correct answer (the board
        genuinely was healthy) -- no harm done, but that's luck against
        this model's real behavior, not evidence the behavior is fine.
        Not reverted -- the user's choice stands -- but flagged clearly
        rather than left for them to discover the hard way.

  Verify: python3 -m py_compile. Ran the real console end-to-end
        against real hardware with the new default three times (one
        initial confirmation run, two reproducibility runs for the
        verification-skipping finding) -- all read-only/safe requests
        only, no dangerous actions queued or confirmed, per this same

        into a non-interactive test of this console).
