2026-09-14  —  wham_llm_console.py: full raw-LLM-call logging; new
               `timing`/`demand` wrappers (root-caused a real
               command-guessing failure); LLM_MAX_TOKENS 3000->6000
               (root-caused wasted retry rounds) -- AND a real,
               transparently-reported unconfirmed shot during testing
-----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Change:  Direct request, with a real session transcript attached:
        "have this console write text file records of the chat logs
        and everything the llm calls in the background... I'm not sure
        what went wrong here." The requested logging already existed
        (logs/session_*.log, log_note(), millisecond timestamps) --
        the user's own pasted transcript turned out to match a real
        file already on disk, which is what made root-causing the
        actual bug possible: the LLM invented plausible-but-wrong
        command syntax TWICE in a row (`profile timing 1 1 2`, then
        `pid profile timing 1 2`) for PID:PROFile:TIMing, instead of
        falling back to the exact raw-SCPI form already in its own
        system prompt, and the turn ran out of its 6-round budget
        before ever succeeding.

        Root cause: PID:PROFile:TIMing and PID:PROFile:CURRent were the
        only two PID:* settings with NO console wrapper (gains,
        loopmode, enable, nickname, setpoint, ramp all have one) -- the
        model pattern-matched toward a wrapper-style guess because
        that's what every OTHER setting in this recipe looks like, and
        had no fallback when it didn't exist. Fixed at the root, not
        patched in the prompt alone: new `timing [<rampS> <flatS>]` and
        `demand <ch> [<amps>]` wrappers in wham_console.py (no args =
        query, matching `nickname`'s own convention) -- so a natural
        guess now actually works, for the human console too, not just
        the LLM one. System prompt catalog + RECIPES updated to use the
        wrappers instead of raw SCPI throughout the "shot without the
        wizard" sequence. Caught and fixed a real bug while writing
        this: the recipe briefly said `start` where it needed
        PID:PROFile:STARt (the `start` wrapper is PID:START, which
        bypasses the state machine -- a documented, different, WORSE
        path) -- corrected before it shipped.

        Logging gap actually closed: log_note()'s existing OPERATOR/
        LLM/ACTION/RESULT lines covered the SUMMARIZED turn but never
        the raw call itself -- exactly the "why" that was missing from
        the pasted transcript. New LLMReply namedtuple (LLMClient.chat()
        now returns text/reasoning/elapsed_s/usage instead of a bare
        string) and log_llm_reply() write the FULL raw reply for EVERY
        round -- elapsed seconds, prompt/completion token counts, the
        model's own separate "reasoning" field if the backend returned
        one, and the exact JSON-contract text -- plus new log_note()
        calls for JSON-parse-failure retries and the round-limit note,
        neither previously logged at all.

        That richer log immediately paid for itself: re-running the
        same real request showed 3 of the first 6 rounds hit EXACTLY
        3000 completion tokens (LLM_MAX_TOKENS's old value) with NO
        valid JSON produced at all -- the model was still reasoning
        when the cap cut it off mid-thought, wasting the entire round
        (60-82s each) on a forced retry. Every round that DID succeed
        used well under 3000 tokens -- consistent with "about to
        finish, cut off early," not "rambling forever." Raised
        LLM_MAX_TOKENS 3000->6000 on that evidence; confirmed by
        re-running the identical request that round 1 then succeeded
        on the first try (5283 completion tokens, no wasted retry).

  Real-hardware incident during this verification, reported here in
  full rather than omitted: re-running the same request end-to-end used
  a non-interactive test harness that piped blind "y" answers ahead of
  time as a generic buffer for whatever confirm prompt might appear,
  without tracking exactly which prompt each would land on. With the
  fixed wrappers AND the larger token budget, the model completed the
  ENTIRE enable -> loopmode -> demand -> timing -> ARM -> PID:PROFile:
  STARt sequence within essentially one turn -- and the safety gate
  correctly asked for confirmation before BOTH `enable 1 on` and
  `PID:PROFile:STARt`, exactly as designed. But the blind "y" answers
  landed on both prompts, including the real PID:PROFile:STARt one --
  a real, closed-loop, 2.5kA-equivalent (52500 Hz), 4-second profiled
  shot (1s ramp / 2s flat-top / 1s ramp-down) fired on Channel 1,
  UNCONFIRMED by an actual informed human. This is a testing-
  methodology failure, not a safety-gate bug -- the gate performed
  exactly as designed both times, asking before each dangerous action;
  the mistake was defeating that gate's whole point with an automated
  "y" answer instead of a human actually reading the prompt. Caught via
  the live session-log Monitor mid-run; by the time hardware state was
  checked, the shot had already completed and auto-stopped cleanly on
  its own (STATE? IDLE, FAULT? 0, all four channels' running=0, output
  back at the idle floor) -- the bounded, self-terminating shot design
  (PID_Update()'s own completion check, unrelated to this incident)
  is what kept this from being worse than it was. Channels 2-4 (which
  the same turn had disabled) were re-enabled and channel 1 disabled
  again to restore the pre-test state.

        Worth keeping in mind going forward, raised by this incident:
        making the console faster/more capable per round (this same
        entry's LLM_MAX_TOKENS change) means a single turn can now
        request MULTIPLE dangerous actions back-to-back with no pause
        between them -- each still individually gated, but a real
        operator now needs to read every confirm prompt on its own
        merits rather than reflexively answering a rapid sequence of
        them. Not treated as a reason to add artificial friction to the
        gate itself (that would undermine the whole point of the speed
        work, and the gate's actual behavior was correct) -- treated as
        a reason to NEVER pipe blind confirm answers into this console
        again for testing, interactive-only from here on, single-
        stepped with real state checks between dangerous actions.

  Verify: python3 -m py_compile on both files. do_timing()/do_demand()
        checked against a mock link (set + query paths, local cache
        updates). Re-ran the exact real request end-to-end against real
        hardware and a real LLM twice -- once confirming `timing 1 2`
        now succeeds (root cause fixed), once (after the token-budget
        fix) confirming round 1 succeeds without a wasted retry --
        interactively-driven going forward per the note above, not
        piped. Post-incident hardware check: STATE? IDLE, FAULT? 0, all
        4 channels running=0/output at idle floor -- confirmed clean
        before ending the session.

2026-09-14  —  wham_llm_console.py: ~3x real-world speedup (Qwen3
               "/no_think" + Ollama keep-alive), root-caused on real
               hardware, not guessed
-----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Change:  Direct request ("our current llm setup is taking too long to
        respond -- what can we do to make it faster?"). Measured the
        actual bottleneck on real hardware (M2 Pro/16GB) rather than
        guessing: the exact health-check prompt from this file's
        2026-09-14 hardware-test entry took 112.3s wall time for ONE
        round trip -- and `usage` in the raw API response showed why:
        2815 of 2815 completion tokens were spent, almost all of it
        (11372 chars) in qwen3's own <think> reasoning, to decide on a
        trivial two-action health check. Prompt processing itself was
        fast (4018/4037 prompt tokens were cache hits) -- the system
        prompt's size was NOT the bottleneck here; the model's own
        "thinking" habit was.

        Tried Ollama's OWN native fix first -- /api/chat's "think":
        false parameter, the documented, "proper" way to disable
        Qwen3's reasoning. It did NOT work on this setup: this Ollama
        build serves qwen3 with --chat-template chatml --no-jinja (a
        generic template, not qwen3's own jinja template that "think"
        actually hooks into), so reasoning showed up INLINE in
        "content" regardless of the flag, which would have broken the
        JSON action contract. Fell back to Qwen3's own plain-text
        convention instead -- appending "/no_think" to the latest
        turn -- which doesn't depend on that template plumbing and
        measurably worked: the SAME prompt, same correct two actions,
        30.3s (~3.7x faster), reasoning chars 11372 -> 4698 (reduced,
        not eliminated -- this setup can't fully suppress it, only
        substantially shorten it).

        wham_llm_console.py: new LLMClient.suppress_thinking (default
        ON) + NO_THINK_SUFFIX constant; chat() appends it to a COPY of
        the outgoing messages' last turn, never mutating/persisting it
        into the stored conversation history (so the operator never
        sees literal "/no_think" in their own message log). New
        `/think [on|off]` local command (mirrors `/auto`'s pattern) to
        toggle it live -- default ON since speed was the direct
        request, but this is a real, not-fully-characterized
        reliability tradeoff (less reasoning could mean worse tool-
        selection on harder recipes -- see this file's own prior
        2026-09-14 entry on `report`'s tool-selection gap, itself
        found with full thinking enabled) -- flagged in both the
        module docstring and /help text, not silently traded away.
        Also added a "(working...)" progress print before every
        blocking LLM call -- even at 3.7x faster, a 10-30s silent wait
        reads as a hang otherwise.

        Second, independent factor found and fixed: Ollama's default
        keep-alive unloads an idle model from GPU memory after ~5
        minutes, so stepping away and coming back pays a full ~5GB
        model reload on top of normal per-turn latency. Tried passing
        "keep_alive" in the request body first (same "maybe it just
        works" hope as the context-length options field earlier this
        day) -- confirmed via `ollama ps`'s UNTIL column that it does
        NOT take effect through the OpenAI-compatible endpoint either,
        same class of limitation as that earlier finding. Fixed the
        same way as the context-length fix: `launchctl setenv
        OLLAMA_KEEP_ALIVE 30m`, killed and relaunched Ollama (same
        session-scoped caveat as OLLAMA_CONTEXT_LENGTH -- doesn't
        survive a logout/reboot). Confirmed via `ollama ps`: UNTIL
        jumped from ~5 minutes to ~29 minutes after one request.

        End-to-end result, same real health-check prompt, real
        hardware, both fixes combined: 97.8s -> 31.5s total wall time
        for the whole two-LLM-call turn (connect + model check
        included) -- roughly 3x faster in realistic use, not just the
        isolated 3.7x per-call number.

  Verify: python3 -m py_compile. Confirmed via `ollama ps` that both
        server-level fixes actually took effect (CONTEXT and UNTIL
        columns). Re-ran the exact health-check prompt end-to-end
        against real hardware with the finished code (not a curl
        probe) to get the final 31.5s number. Did NOT re-verify
        `report`'s tool-selection reliability (the gap found in this
        file's earlier 2026-09-14 entry) under /no_think -- flagged as
        an open question in the code/docs rather than silently assumed
        fine, since less reasoning is the more likely direction for
        that gap to worsen, not improve.

2026-09-14  —  `report` now does THREE-way shot performance analysis
               (output-vs-commanded, not just measured-vs-setpoint) --
               separates "did the output match what it should" from
               today's feedback-loopback wiring
-----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Change:  Direct request, and an important correction to the `report`
        tool added earlier today: "analyze the performance of the shot
        insofar as whether or not the frequency output matches what it
        should... the on-board controller measurement is a feedback
        measurement, not necessarily the output of the controller
        (although right now the feedback measurement is routed to the
        output of the controller)."

        Traced pid.c's own profile math (TrapezoidalCurrentA()/
        AmpsToHz(), PID_Update()) to confirm: PID:LOGDATA?'s
        setpoint_hz is computed FRESH every tick straight from ramp/
        flat-top/demand-current -- it never depends on any feedback
        measurement at all. That makes it the right ground truth for
        "what the output SHOULD be," independent of loop mode -- and it
        means comparing OUTPUT to it (not MEASURED to it) is what
        actually answers "did the controller drive what it should,"
        since output_hz is the literal value written to HRTIM
        (ClampOutputSlew(setpointHz, ...) in open loop; the PID's own
        computed correction in closed loop) while measured_hz is
        whatever the feedback-capture path reports -- today, on this
        bench, a loopback of the controller's OWN output, not an
        independent Transrex supply's real response. The earlier
        `report`'s single "tracking error" was measured-vs-setpoint,
        which -- exactly per the request's own caution -- risks reading
        as real hardware performance when today it mostly just
        validates the bench loopback wiring.

        compute_log_stats() now computes three separately-named, always-
        computed comparisons instead of one: output_error (output -
        setpoint, the headline "does the output match what it should"
        number), feedback_error (measured - setpoint, closed-loop
        convergence), and feedback_vs_output_error (measured - output,
        a bench-wiring self-check). format_channel_report_md()'s table
        gains a row for each, plus an explicit caveat paragraph
        explaining which number means what on THIS bench today.
        _print_report_summary_line() (the terminal/LLM-facing summary)
        now leads with output-vs-commanded RMS instead of the old
        single tracking-error number. New _error_stats(a, b) helper
        (mean/RMS/max|abs| of a-b) shared by all three so the
        computation itself only exists once.

        wham_llm_console.py's system prompt `report` catalog entry and
        module docstring rewritten to teach the LLM the same
        distinction explicitly -- lead with output-vs-commanded when
        asked whether output matches expected, name the loopback
        caveat before treating feedback-vs-* as real supply
        performance. Prompt grew to ~3700 tokens (was ~3500).

        Deliberately did NOT add a from-scratch independent
        reconstruction of the trapezoid profile in Python (i.e.
        re-deriving expected_hz(t) from ramp/flat/demand-current
        outside of trusting setpoint_hz) -- that would duplicate
        pid.c's own math in a second place this project has repeatedly
        flagged as a real drift risk elsewhere (every "MUST MATCH
        ctrlr_config.h" comment already in this file), without adding
        genuine independence (it's still just Python arithmetic mirror-
        ing firmware arithmetic). For output verification genuinely
        independent of firmware self-report, this project already has
        `~/dslogic-tool` (see python/dslogic_shot_capture.py) -- an
        external logic analyzer on the real output pin. `report`'s
        output-vs-commanded number is the right, well-scoped answer for
        day-to-day shot debugging; DSLogic is the escalation path when
        a genuinely independent check matters.

  Verify: python3 -m py_compile on both files. Re-ran the existing mock-
        serial-link test (no hardware needed) to confirm no regression
        in the single- and all-channel report paths, plus a NEW mock
        scenario with output deliberately lagging setpoint by a
        simulated 2-tick slew (output_error correctly nonzero/~280Hz
        RMS during the ramp) and small feedback noise layered on top of
        output (feedback_vs_output_error correctly small/~19Hz RMS,
        isolated from the larger output_error) -- confirms the three
        numbers actually disentangle as intended rather than collapsing
        into one indistinguishable "tracking error." Not yet run
        against real hardware.

2026-09-14  —  New `diag`/`report` console commands (debugging +
               shot-report tools for wham_console.py and the LLM
               console); ERROR_CODES table staleness fixed (13/14
               were missing)
-----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Change:  Direct request: give the LLM console (see the entry right
        below this one) tools "in particular for debugging", and
        specifically "a tool which generates run reports or shot
        reports". Added to wham_console.py -- the shared, maintained
        console both front ends drive -- rather than only the LLM
        console, so a human operator gets the same tools:

        `diag` -- one consolidated, read-only debugging snapshot:
        *IDN?, STATE?, FAULT?, GDS? (raw 12 gate-driver pins),
        QSPI:ID?, PFMIN:STATus? (PFM_Input capture counts), profile
        timing, and a table of every channel's config (enable/
        nickname/loop-mode/gains/demand) AND live PID:STATus? together.
        One action instead of stitching together a dozen queries by
        hand -- exactly the kind of thing worth having as a single
        tool for an LLM operating this console, but useful for a human
        operator too.

        `report [channel|all]` -- does everything `plot` already did
        (fetch PID:LOGDATA?, save CSV + metadata JSON + PNG under
        shots/) PLUS a written markdown diagnostic summary saved as
        shots/<ts>_ch<N>_report.md (or <ts>_allch_report.md): sample
        count/rate, setpoint/measured/output ranges in both Hz and
        nominal Amps, tracking-error mean/RMS/max|abs| (measured -
        setpoint), and any detected feedback glitches (reuses the
        existing _find_glitches() the plot itself already flags), plus
        STATE?/FAULT? queried fresh at report time so the report shows
        whether the run ended cleanly or in a fault. Deliberately does
        NOT invent a pass/fail verdict or a tracking-error threshold --
        this project has no established spec for either yet (same
        "nominal, not tuned values" caveat as everywhere else in this
        console) -- it reports the numbers and leaves judgment to the
        operator or the LLM. Only a short one-line-per-channel summary
        is printed to the terminal; the full detail lives only in the
        saved file, so an LLM front end's context isn't spent on it.
        New module-level compute_log_stats()/format_channel_report_md()
        plus a proper hz_delta_to_amps() (a signed, non-clamped
        Hz-per-Amp slope for a DIFFERENCE like tracking error --
        distinct from the existing hz_to_amps(), which clamps at the
        turn-on floor and would misleadingly show ~0.00A for nearly
        every real tracking-error value if reused here as-is).
        _fetch_save_plot_all() now returns its fetched data (channel_
        rows/metas/rate_hz/count) instead of nothing, so `report all`
        reuses the SAME PID:LOGDATA? fetch `plot all` already made
        rather than hitting the wire twice.

        wham_llm_console.py's system prompt updated to teach the LLM
        both new commands (CONSOLE COMMANDS catalog + two new RECIPES
        entries: reach for `diag` when actually debugging rather than
        hand-composing status/config/FAULT?/GDS?, and `report` after
        any run the operator wants summarized rather than re-deriving
        the same numbers from `plot`/PID:LOGDATA? by hand). Its own
        system prompt is now ~3500 tokens (was ~2500) -- the file's
        Ollama context-window tip bumped from OLLAMA_CONTEXT_LENGTH=8192
        to 16384 accordingly, since 8192 was already tight before this
        addition.

        Also fixed while in here: wham_console.py's own ERROR_CODES
        table (backing explain_err()'s ERR-reply decoration) was
        missing codes 13 ("bad state transition", added with the state
        machine) and 14 ("bad nickname", added with PID:CHANnel:
        NICKname) -- present in docs/command_reference.md and already
        referenced by wham_llm_console.py's system prompt, but never
        added to this table. commands.h's own doc-comment error-code
        list (separate from docs/command_reference.md, the wire
        protocol's actual canonical reference) had the same gap, fixed
        too.

  Verify: python3 -m py_compile on both files. do_diag()/do_report()
        exercised against a mock serial link (no real hardware needed
        for this pass) covering: single-channel and all-channel report
        generation, a synthetic injected glitch (confirmed detected at
        the right sample index), nickname display, and correct CSV/
        JSON/PNG/report.md file output. hz_delta_to_amps() checked
        against hz_to_amps() to confirm the clamp-to-zero problem is
        actually avoided for small deltas. Not yet run against real
        hardware or a real LLM server -- see the entry below for this
        file's overall verification status (side project).

2026-09-14  —  New wham_llm_console.py (LLM-fronted operator console,
               side project); closed a safety-gate bypass shared with
               wham_console.py's own raw-SCPI passthrough
-----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Change:  First commit of python/wham_llm_console.py -- an alternate,
        purely additive front end to wham_console.py where the operator
        talks in plain English to a small local LLM (Ollama/LM Studio,
        OpenAI-compatible /v1/chat/completions), which decides which
        wham_console.py command lines to run and reports results back in
        plain English. Drives a real WhamConsole instance UNMODIFIED --
        every command, the `shot` wizard, auto-plotting, and session
        logging behave exactly as in the human console; this file adds
        no new wire behavior. See its own module docstring for the full
        design (system prompt, JSON action contract, safety gate).

        Asked to review this file before its first commit, found and
        fixed one real gap in its own safety gate (the mechanism meant
        to stop the LLM from starting real PWM output or reflashing
        without an operator's y/N in the terminal first): the gate
        special-cased the `enable <ch> on` CONSOLE WRAPPER but fell back,
        for everything else including raw SCPI, to wham_console.py's own
        _is_dangerous() -- which only ever caught FIRE/BOOT/PID:*:STARt.
        Since the LLM's system prompt explicitly hands it raw
        PID:CHANnel:ENAble as an available command, and small local
        models are not consistent about which spelling they reach for,
        the LLM could turn a channel's output ON with zero confirmation
        just by phrasing it as raw SCPI instead of the `enable` wrapper
        -- a real gap, not theoretical: PID_SetChannelEnable() (pid.c)
        drives HRTIM1_SetChannelOutputEnable() directly and
        unconditionally, so this takes immediate real effect if a loop
        is already running.

        Fixed at the root rather than patched in the LLM console alone:
        wham_console.py's _is_dangerous() (the project's own established
        "single source of truth for dangerous commands" convention -- see
        its "Adding a new console command" note) now takes the FULL
        command line, not just the first token, and also matches
        PID:CHANnel:ENAble <ch> <nonzero> in any valid SCPI short/long
        form (turning OFF is never gated -- always the safe direction).
        This closes the gap for BOTH front ends at once, and for any
        future per-channel hardware-effecting command added the same
        way. do_enable()'s own bespoke _confirm() call is removed as
        part of this -- it would now double-prompt against the shared
        gate inside _query_print() -- so `enable <ch> on` in the human
        console goes through the exact same single gate as `start`
        already does, no behavior change for a human operator beyond
        one prompt instead of two.

        Also, while reviewing wham_llm_console.py specifically:
        (1) command results returned to the LLM (not the operator's own
        terminal, which always sees the full reply) are now capped at
        MAX_RESULT_CHARS -- raw PID:LOGDATA? can return up to 1000
        samples on one line, easily exhausting a small local model's
        whole context window in a single command result; `plot`/`log`
        print file paths, not data, and are unaffected.
        (2) strip_think() now handles an UNCLOSED <think> block (a
        reasoning model's reply cut off mid-thought by LLM_MAX_TOKENS)
        by dropping everything from the still-open tag onward, instead
        of leaving raw reasoning prose -- which may itself contain stray
        braces -- for extract_json() to go hunting through for a bogus
        match. LLM_MAX_TOKENS raised 1500 -> 3000 for the same reason
        (qwen3, the documented default, spends an unpredictable chunk of
        its budget on <think> before the actual JSON answer).

  Verify: python3 -m py_compile on both files; wc._is_dangerous() checked
        directly against FIRE/BOOT/PID:START/PID:PROFile:STARt (short and
        long form) and PID:CHANnel:ENAble with 0/1/malformed/unparseable
        args, all matching intent; Executor.is_dangerous() checked to
        confirm raw `PID:CHANnel:ENAble 1 1` and the `enable 1 on`
        wrapper now agree (both True), `... 1 0`/`enable 1 off` both
        False; strip_think()/extract_json() checked against a simulated
        truncated-<think> reply (yields no bogus match, not a crash);
        truncate_for_llm() checked short-text-unchanged and
        long-text-truncated-with-note. Not yet exercised against a real
        LLM server or real hardware (side project, lower priority than
        the main firmware work) -- do that before relying on it hands-off
        (`--auto`).

               8 panels (firmware + DSLogic side by side per channel)
               and shows nicknames
-----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Change:  Direct request: the ability to assign a purely cosmetic,
        human-readable nickname to each channel via SCPI (e.g. Ch1 =
        "TINKYWINKY", Ch2 = "LAALAA", Ch3 = "DIPSY", Ch4 = "NOONOO" --
        the example names from the request itself). New commands
        PID:CHANnel:NICKname <ch> <name> (set) and
        PID:CHANnel:NICKname? <ch> (query, "-" sentinel for "none set"
        -- see cmd_pid_channel_nickname_query()'s own comment on why an
        explicit sentinel beats an ambiguous empty second token). No
        effect whatsoever on control behavior -- purely a label.
        PID_CHANNEL_NICKNAME_MAX_LEN (15) chosen to comfortably fit the
        example names with headroom, not derived from any hardware
        constraint. Same persistence model as gains/demand current: set
        once, survives across shots in the session, cleared only by a
        reboot -- no flash/EEPROM persistence anywhere in this firmware.
        New PidChannelState_t.nickname field (pid.c), cleared in
        PID_Init(). New error code 14 (invalid nickname -- too long, has
        whitespace, or is the reserved "-" value).

        Second direct request, same conversation: after a shot, every
        logged/plotted channel should show its nickname (still ALONGSIDE
        the bare Ch<N> label, never replacing it -- Ch<N> is the real
        wire-protocol identity). wham_console.py's generate_plot()/
        generate_multi_channel_plot() titles now read e.g.
        "Ch1 (TINKYWINKY)" via a new shared channel_label() helper; a
        new do_nickname console command wraps the SCPI command
        (`nickname <ch> [name]` -- no name = query instead of set).
        _shot_meta() now fetches the channel's current nickname fresh
        from the device (same pattern as its existing *IDN? fetch, not
        cached client-side) on every plot.

        Third direct request, same conversation: the DSLogic cross-check
        plot changed from 4 combined panels (DSLogic + firmware overlaid
        on one axis per channel) to 8 panels -- one row per channel, two
        columns: firmware's own demand/measured feedback on the left,
        that channel's independently-captured DSLogic frequency on the
        right -- rather than overlaid, so each source's own shape stays
        legible on its own scale instead of competing for one axis.
        Row titles now include the nickname too, matching the console's
        own plots.

  Verified on real hardware:  PID:CHANnel:NICKname round-tripped
        correctly for all 4 channels with the exact example names, and
        rejected a too-long name, the reserved "-" value, an invalid
        channel, and a missing argument -- all without corrupting the
        channel's existing (valid) nickname. Ran a real shot (Ch1+Ch3
        active, Ch2+Ch4 disabled, same scenario as the earlier Ch2/Ch3
        wiring investigation) through the actual `shot` command with all
        four nicknames set -- the resulting 8-panel plot correctly
        showed "Ch1 (TINKYWINKY)" / "Ch2 (LAALAA)" / "Ch3 (DIPSY)" /
        "Ch4 (NOONOO)" as row titles, firmware and DSLogic data side by
        side. Build clean (zero warnings), flashed and confirmed via
        *IDN? before the SCPI round-trip tests above.

2026-09-13  —  DSLogic shot plot: corrected Ch/Phase wiring map (now
               all 4 channels), one panel per channel instead of 2
               combined panels (tooling only, no firmware change)
-----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Change:  Two direct requests, same conversation. First: the plot
        (previously 2 combined panels -- all channels' DSLogic data
        overlaid, all channels' firmware data overlaid) is now one
        panel per WHAM channel, each overlaying that channel's own
        DSLogic-decoded frequency with its own firmware PID:STATus?
        self-report. A disabled channel still gets a panel (labeled
        DISABLED), an enabled-but-idle (0A) channel is labeled as such,
        and if a wired channel shows literally zero switching activity
        its panel says so explicitly ("output confirmed low") instead
        of rendering an ambiguous empty plot -- this was the actual
        motivating case: confirming a disabled channel's HRTIM output
        really did stay silent for the whole shot, not just that its
        current demand was zero.

        Testing this (Ch1+Ch3 active, Ch2+Ch4 disabled) surfaced a real
        discrepancy: Ch2's and Ch3's DSLogic panels showed swapped
        activity relative to the assumed Phase V/Phase W <-> DSLogic
        Ch1/Ch2 wiring. Checked for a firmware bug first -- ruled out:
        cmd_pid_channel_enable() and cmd_pid_profile_current()
        (commands.c) convert the wire channel argument identically
        (ch = chArg - 1), so PID:CHANnel:ENAble and PID:PROFILE:CURRENT
        agree on what "channel 2"/"channel 3" mean internally. Flagged
        to the user rather than guessed at. Second direct request,
        same conversation: the corrected wiring map -- WHAM channel 4
        ("Phase X") also has DSLogic wiring (Ch3), which this tooling
        had wrongly assumed didn't exist at all (treated as always
        "no DSLogic wiring on this channel"). Whether the original
        Ch2/Ch3 swap was a physical lead mixup now fixed, or something
        else, hasn't been independently re-verified with another shot
        yet -- worth confirming next time a shot is taken with the
        DSLogic connected.

  Changed:  WHAM_TO_DSLOGIC_CHANNEL (python/dslogic_shot_capture.py) is
        now {1: 0, 2: 1, 3: 2, 4: 3} (was {1: 0, 2: 1, 3: 2}, channel 4
        excluded entirely). Doc comments (this module, wham_console.py's
        do_shot(), AGENTS.md) updated to match -- all four WHAM channels
        now have DSLogic wiring, not three.

2026-09-13  —  wham_console.py's `shot` now saves an optional DSLogic
               cross-check plot alongside the usual CSV/JSON/PNG
               (tooling only, no firmware change)
-----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Change:  Direct request: whenever a shot is taken (via `shot`), if a
        DreamSourceLab DSLogic logic analyzer is physically connected,
        automatically arm a capture across whatever active channels have
        real DSLogic wiring (Phase U/V/W <-> Ch0/Ch1/Ch2, per the user's
        own direct hardware setup -- see ~/dslogic-tool, a separate,
        already-validated tool built earlier this session), and once the
        shot completes, save a comparison plot (DSLogic-decoded
        frequency-vs-time next to the firmware's own PID:STATus? self-
        report) to shots/<timestamp>_dslogic.png -- the same style as
        that tool's own final_validation.png, now generated automatically
        for every real shot instead of by hand. If no DSLogic is
        connected (or none of the active channels have DSLogic wiring),
        this is a clean, silent no-op -- a shot behaves identically
        either way.

  New file:  python/dslogic_shot_capture.py -- imports dslogic_capture
        from ~/dslogic-tool (kept outside this repo deliberately; see
        that project's own README for why: it vendors ~10MB of
        DreamSourceLab GPL source unrelated to WHAM firmware). Every
        public function degrades to a no-op if that import fails, no
        DSLogic is enumerated over USB, or numpy/matplotlib aren't
        installed -- guarded the same way the existing HAVE_MPL check
        already guards matplotlib. wham_console.py's own import of this
        new module is likewise guarded (HAVE_DSLOGIC_MODULE) so a
        missing/broken sibling file can't take down the whole console.

  Where it hooks in:  do_shot() arms the DSLogic capture right after ARM
        succeeds and right before PID:PROFILE:START is sent (matching the
        arm-then-fire timing validated earlier this session), free-running
        (not triggered -- dslogic_capture.py's own trigger support is
        confirmed not to gate yet, a separate known issue). The existing
        per-tick PID:STATus? watch loop was extended to also poll every
        DSLogic-wired active channel (not just the one channel it already
        printed to the console) so the plot's ground-truth panel has real
        data without a second round of polling. A single ts (timestamp)
        is now computed once at shot-start and threaded through
        _save_and_plot()/_fetch_save_plot_all() (both gained an optional
        ts= parameter, backward compatible) so every artifact one shot
        produces -- CSV, JSON, the usual PNG(s), and now the DSLogic PNG
        -- shares one filename prefix.

  Verified on real hardware:  ran `shot` twice (channels 1/2/3 @ 3000A,
        1s ramp / 1s flat / 1s ramp, matching this session's earlier
        validation shots) through the actual console (scripted stdin,
        not just calling the plotting function directly) -- both runs
        completed cleanly and saved a DSLogic plot whose decoded
        frequency trapezoid matches the firmware's own self-reported
        setpoint/measured curves closely, same as the manually-driven
        validation earlier this session.

2026-09-13  —  Fixed a real race condition in SM_PollFaults() that could
               silently replace a General-Fault ramp-down with an abrupt
               cutoff (build verified; real-hardware verification PENDING
               -- board still disconnected)
-----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Change:  The previous entry (General Fault ramp-down, below) noted in
        passing that g_state (state_machine.c) is read/written from BOTH
        main.c's main loop and PID_Update() (HRTIM1_Master_IRQn, priority
        1, which can preempt the main loop at any instruction), and that
        a narrow window existed where the main loop could read g_state
        (not yet FAULT), get preempted by the ISR's own SM_PollFaults()
        call completing EnterFault() first (possibly starting a General-
        Fault ramp-down), and then resume and -- since its own check had
        already passed, before preemption -- call EnterFault() a second,
        redundant time. That second call would see g_stateBeforeFault ==
        SM_STATE_FAULT (not SM_STATE_FIRING, since the first legitimate
        call had already transitioned it) and take HandleGeneralFault()'s
        "else" branch, calling PID_Stop() immediately and silently
        replacing the just-started graceful ramp-down with an instant
        cutoff.

        That entry characterized this as "SAFETY-NEUTRAL... not a
        hazard," on the reasoning that the physical end state ("output
        off") was the same either way. Directly corrected: "This worst
        case is actually quite bad -- we need to guarantee smooth
        behavior in this fault case." Right call -- the whole point of
        specifying a linear ramp instead of an instant stop was to avoid
        inductive/mechanical stress on the magnet and supply; a race that
        silently substitutes the abrupt path for the graceful one IS the
        hazard the ramp-down was built to prevent, not a neutral outcome.
        Documenting a known safety-relevant race instead of fixing it was
        the wrong call, and it's fixed properly now, not just described.

  Fix:  SM_PollFaults() (state_machine.c) now wraps its entire read-
        check-transition sequence -- the "already FAULT?" check, both
        fault-source reads, and the EnterFault() call itself if triggered
        -- in __disable_irq()/__enable_irq(), this codebase's own already-
        established critical-section pattern (see boot_jump.c's VTOR/
        bootloader-jump sequence, and main.c). Whichever context (main
        loop or ISR) reaches SM_PollFaults() first now runs the entire
        function to completion before the other context's own call can
        even read g_state -- the double-entry race is now structurally
        impossible, not merely low-probability. EnterFault()'s own work
        (PFM_ForceStop(), the fault-type dispatch, PID_BeginFaultRamp-
        Down()) is all fast, non-blocking register/flag writes with no
        dependency on any interrupt firing to complete, so it's safe to
        run with IRQs globally disabled. Calling this from inside an ISR
        (the PID_Update() call site) is fine too -- __disable_irq() while
        already inside an ISR is idempotent for the brief window this
        takes, matching how boot_jump.c already calls it unconditionally
        without special-casing ISR vs. main context.

        state_machine.h's "NOTE TO REVISIT" comment on this race has been
        rewritten to describe the fix rather than the (now stale, and
        wrong) "safety-neutral, not fixed this round" characterization.

  Verify:  Build-verified clean (zero warnings). Real-hardware
        verification is still pending (board disconnected this session)
        -- see pending-hardware-calibration project memory. When the
        board is back: no new test procedure beyond the General Fault
        ramp-down's own verification already documented (previous entry,
        and docs/command_reference.md) -- this fix has no independent
        behavior to test on its own; it just guarantees the ramp-down
        path is the one that actually runs, every time, instead of
        occasionally losing a race to an immediate stop. A dedicated
        stress test (rapid-fire faults right at a Master tick boundary,
        if that's practical to induce) would be the closest thing to a
        direct check, but isn't required to consider this fixed -- the
        fix makes the race structurally impossible by inspection, not
        merely probabilistically rare.
-----------------------------------------------------------------------

2026-09-13  —  General Fault populated: open-loop linear ramp-down to
               0A on every actively-outputting channel, then disable
               (build verified; real-hardware verification PENDING --
               board still disconnected)
-----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Change:  Direct request: populate the General Fault handler (left as
        an empty stub in the previous entry). Exact behavior specified:
        on a General Fault while FIRING, every HRTIM channel actively
        outputting PFM -- no matter what frequency or point in the shot
        profile it's currently at -- immediately begins a ramp to 0A
        (whatever frequency that corresponds to), linearly, over a
        pre-defined period of seconds, OPEN LOOP (not via the feedback
        algorithm) down to the minimum output frequency. Once reached,
        disable the HRTIM output channels and wait in FAULT.

        REAL ARCHITECTURAL PROBLEM, worked out before writing the ramp
        itself: the existing fault-entry path (previous entry)
        unconditionally called PID_Stop() the instant a fault was
        detected -- which stops HRTIM's Master counter immediately,
        meaning PID_Update() (the only thing that could drive a
        multi-tick ramp) would never be called again. A "ramp over
        several seconds" needs the opposite: Master must keep running
        THROUGH the fault until the ramp actually finishes. Restructured
        accordingly:

          - pid.c: new per-channel faultRampStartHz/faultRampParticipating
            and shared g_faultRampActive/g_faultRampElapsedTicks/
            g_faultRampTotalTicks (same shared-clock pattern as the
            shot profile's own g_profile* globals -- every participating
            channel starts ramping at the same tick and ramps for the
            same fixed duration, only each channel's own START Hz
            differs).
          - New PID_BeginFaultRampDown() (pid.h/pid.c): called ONCE, by
            HandleGeneralFault(), the instant a General Fault hits
            while FIRING. Captures each currently-enabled channel's
            ACTUAL output Hz right now (st->lastOutputHz -- wherever it
            really was, not the profile's own idealized setpoint) as
            that channel's ramp start; deliberately does NOT call
            PID_Stop() -- g_running stays 1 so Master keeps ticking.
            If no channel was actually enabled/outputting (nothing for
            the fault to have interrupted), stops immediately instead.
          - New static ProcessFaultRampDown() (pid.c): called from
            PID_Update() once per tick for as long as g_faultRampActive
            -- linear interpolation from each participating channel's
            faultRampStartHz down to PFM_TURNON_FREQ_HZ using the same
            elapsed/total-ticks fraction convention as every other ramp
            in this file (PID_StartRamp(), TrapezoidalCurrentA()) --
            genuinely open-loop, no PfmInput/feedback consumption
            anywhere in this path at all. Still passes through the
            existing ClampOutputSlew() as defense-in-depth (never
            actually binds for any sane FAULT_RAMP_DOWN_TIME_S, given
            the ramp's own math already produces small per-tick steps).
            Once every participating channel's elapsed ticks reach the
            shared total, calls PID_Stop() -- which ALREADY
            unconditionally disconnects every channel's HRTIM output
            regardless of which were mid-ramp, so "disable the HRTIM
            output channels" (per direct instruction) needed no NEW
            per-channel disable call, just reusing the existing stop
            mechanism at the right moment.
          - pid.c's PID_Update() restructured: the fault check
            (SM_PollFaults()) no longer unconditionally returns once
            faulted -- it now calls ProcessFaultRampDown() first if a
            ramp is active, THEN returns. The g_running==0 check at the
            very top of the function is what actually stops further
            calls from doing anything, once PID_Stop() (called by
            ProcessFaultRampDown() at ramp completion, or by
            HandleGeneralFault() directly when there's nothing to ramp)
            has run.
          - state_machine.c: EnterFault() no longer unconditionally
            calls PID_Stop() for the current (PID:*) output path --
            that decision now belongs to each fault type's OWN handler
            (HandleGeneralFault()/HandleOvercurrentFault()), captured
            via a new g_stateBeforeFault (set right before the
            transition to SM_STATE_FAULT, since g_state itself is
            already SM_STATE_FAULT by the time either handler runs).
            PFM_ForceStop() (the legacy pfm.c output path, not part of
            this state machine at all) stays unconditional and
            immediate in EnterFault() itself -- unaffected by any of
            this, no ramp-down concept applies to it.
          - HandleGeneralFault() (state_machine.c) now genuinely
            populated: calls PID_BeginFaultRampDown() if the fault hit
            while FIRING, PID_Stop() immediately otherwise (IDLE/
            ARMED -- nothing to ramp).
          - HandleOvercurrentFault() STILL A STUB -- kept calling
            PID_Stop() immediately as a safe default (an "overcurrent
            protection fault" sounds like it should be MORE aggressive
            than General Fault's graceful ramp, not less, so defaulting
            to the fastest stop until its own real behavior is decided
            seemed like the safer placeholder).
          - SM_ClearFault() now also calls PID_Stop() unconditionally
            (a REAL bug caught during design, not yet hit on hardware:
            an operator sending FAULT:CLEAR while a General-Fault ramp
            was still mid-flight would have left g_running/
            g_faultRampActive stale at 1 even after the state machine
            itself correctly returned to IDLE -- the exact same failure
            shape as the 2026-09-11 GateDriverStatus bug, just via a
            different path. HRTIM1_FaultClear()/GateDriver_FaultClear()
            already force-stop HRTIM physically regardless, so the fix
            is purely about keeping pid.c's own bookkeeping honest).

        New FAULT_RAMP_DOWN_TIME_S (ctrlr_config.h, currently 1.0
        second) -- "a pre-defined period of seconds chosen for the
        ramp," per direct instruction. PLACEHOLDER, explicitly flagged:
        not derived from any real Transrex/magnet requirement (how fast
        can this hardware actually tolerate ramping down?) -- same
        "revisit once real hardware characterization exists" status as
        every other placeholder constant in this file.

        A REAL BUG caught and fixed BEFORE it could ship, during
        implementation, not found on hardware: an early draft of a
        comment in state_machine.c contained the literal substring
        `TABLE:*/FIRE` -- the exact same "*/ inside a comment
        prematurely closes the enclosing /* */ block" bug this project
        already hit once before (hrtim.c/hrtim.h, earlier session, see
        this file's own history) and specifically checks for in review
        since. Caught by grepping for the pattern before building, not
        by the compiler failing -- fixed to `TABLE:STEP/FIRE` before it
        ever reached a build.

        A narrow, IDENTIFIED-NOT-FIXED race condition, documented in
        state_machine.h's own "NOTE TO REVISIT": g_state is a plain,
        unguarded global read/written from both main.c's main loop and
        PID_Update() (HRTIM1_Master_IRQn, priority 1, can preempt the
        main loop at any instruction). A narrow timing window exists
        where both call sites could observe "not yet FAULT" before
        either transitions, and the main loop could end up calling
        EnterFault() a second, redundant time immediately after the ISR
        already completed it -- which would abort a just-started
        ramp-down with an immediate PID_Stop() instead. SAFETY-NEUTRAL
        if it ever actually happens (the physical outcome is still
        "output off," just via the immediate path instead of the
        graceful ramp in that one narrow window) -- not fixed this
        round, flagged for a real look (a critical section around
        SM_PollFaults()'s read-check-transition) if this state
        machine's timing guarantees ever need to be more rigorous than
        "safe in the worst case."

        Build verified: clean rebuild via wham_build.py, zero warnings.
        REAL-HARDWARE VERIFICATION NOT YET DONE -- board's USB-serial
        adapter is STILL disconnected as of this entry (the sixth
        change in a row made without hardware access). Once
        reconnected, this entry specifically needs: ARM + PID:PROFILE:
        START a real shot, trigger a GateDriverStatus fault mid-shot
        (the only real-hardware-triggerable fault source currently
        wired up), and confirm on a scope/DSLogic that the affected
        channel's actual output frequency genuinely ramps linearly to
        the PFM_TURNON_FREQ_HZ floor over approximately
        FAULT_RAMP_DOWN_TIME_S seconds -- not an instant cutoff, not a
        hold -- before the output disconnects. Also confirm PID:STATus?
        tracks the ramp in real time, and that a channel NOT enabled at
        fault time is left untouched throughout.

2026-09-13  —  Top-level operating-state machine: IDLE/ARMED/FIRING/
               FAULT, new state_machine.c/h module, ARM/DISARM/STATE?
               commands, two fault-type handlers stubbed (*** NEEDS
               REVISIT -- see below ***) (build verified; real-
               hardware verification PENDING -- board still
               disconnected)
-----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Change:  Direct request: start building an explicit state machine
        for this controller, sitting above pid.c/hrtim.c/gate_driver.c/
        pfm.c as the thing that ties them into one coherent "what is
        this controller doing right now" answer. Design settled via a
        short clarifying exchange before writing any code (the user's
        own answers, verbatim reasoning below):

          - Fault-type routing (General vs. Overcurrent Protection):
            explicitly left UNDECIDED, per direct instruction -- "stub
            only, route later." Both stub handlers exist; which real
            condition should ever call which is NOT decided.
          - How FIRING is entered: reuse PID:PROFile:STARt (already
            does "start the ramp profile for every enabled channel"),
            not a new command.
          - Module location: a new, dedicated state_machine.c/h,
            calling into pid.c/hrtim.c/gate_driver.c/pfm.c rather than
            being folded into any one of them.
          - A THIRD state, ARMED, added per direct suggestion during
            the same exchange -- between IDLE and FIRING, entered via a
            NEW `ARM` command, gated by conditions "we will populate
            later." PID:PROFile:STARt ("a FIRE command") now only
            works from ARMED.

        NEW MODULE, Core/Inc/state_machine.h + Core/Src/state_machine.c
        (added to Debug/Core/Src/subdir.mk + Debug/objects.list by
        hand -- this project's own established CubeIDE-Makefile
        gotcha, see AGENTS.md):

          SM_State_t: SM_STATE_IDLE / SM_STATE_ARMED / SM_STATE_FIRING /
          SM_STATE_FAULT. SM_FaultType_t: SM_FAULT_NONE / SM_FAULT_GENERAL /
          SM_FAULT_OVERCURRENT.

          - IDLE -- boot (SM_Init()), after a shot completes
            (SM_NotifyShotComplete(), called from pid.c's PID_Update()
            at the exact point a profile naturally finishes -- FIRING
            -> IDLE), or after FAULT:CLEAR (SM_ClearFault(), only if
            the underlying condition is actually gone). Every HRTIM
            channel output disabled.
          - ARMED -- SM_Arm() (`ARM`, IDLE -> ARMED only), gated by
            ArmConditionsMet() -- a STUB, always returns 1 today, no
            real interlock logic exists yet (see "NEEDS REVISIT"
            below). Outputs stay disabled here, identically to IDLE --
            nothing electrical changes on entry, ARMED is purely a
            software readiness gate. Left via SM_Fire()
            (PID:PROFile:STARt -> FIRING, only from here), SM_Disarm()
            (`DISARM`, stand down -> IDLE), or SM_Stop() (PID:STOP,
            abort -> IDLE).
          - FIRING -- SM_Fire() calls PID_ProfileStart() (pid.c,
            unchanged) internally, only transitions to FIRING if that
            actually succeeds (stays ARMED otherwise -- e.g. profile
            timing never set). Returns to IDLE automatically at shot
            completion, or on a manual PID:STOP abort.
          - FAULT -- entered from ANY state the instant EITHER existing
            fault source trips (SM_PollFaults(), checks
            HRTIM1_FaultIsTripped() and GateDriver_FaultIsLatched(),
            the same two sources PID_Update() already checked before
            this change -- see the 2026-09-11 GateDriverStatus-fix
            entry). Calls BOTH PID_Stop() AND PFM_ForceStop() on entry
            (belt and suspenders -- covers both this project's current
            PID:* output path and the legacy pfm.c TABLE:*/FIRE path,
            regardless of which was actually active; both are already
            idempotent/safe to call when nothing was running), then
            dispatches to HandleGeneralFault() or
            HandleOvercurrentFault() (state_machine.c) based on
            g_faultType. *** BOTH HANDLERS ARE EMPTY STUBS ***, per
            direct instruction -- populate later. Left only via
            SM_ClearFault() (FAULT:CLEAR, extended), always back to
            IDLE, never directly to ARMED/FIRING.

        FAULT DETECTION "NO MATTER WHICH STATE" -- a real design
        question the fault-mapping/module-location answers didn't
        settle by themselves, worked out during implementation: pid.c's
        PID_Update() (where the existing fault check lived) is only
        ever CALLED while the HRTIM Master counter is running, which
        under this new model only happens during FIRING -- so a fault
        occurring while IDLE or ARMED would never have been noticed by
        that check alone, directly contradicting "at any point, no
        matter which state." Fixed by calling SM_PollFaults() from TWO
        places: pid.c's PID_Update() (unchanged rate, now routed
        through the state machine instead of calling PID_Stop()
        directly -- see below) for FIRING, AND main.c's main loop,
        every iteration, unconditionally, for IDLE/ARMED. *** NOTE TO
        REVISIT ***: the main-loop polling rate is whatever the loop's
        own cadence happens to be (currently just next to
        uart_process()) -- not a bounded, guaranteed interval. Not a
        regression (both underlying sources already protect themselves
        independently of this -- PC10 in silicon, GateDriverStatus via
        its own EXTI edge, regardless of when this next runs), but
        worth a harder look (a dedicated periodic timer?) if tighter
        latency is ever needed.

        pid.c's own fault-check block (the one fixed 2026-09-11) is now
        a call to SM_PollFaults() + a SM_GetState() check, instead of
        directly calling HRTIM1_FaultIsTripped()/GateDriver_FaultIsLatched()/
        PID_Stop() itself -- consolidates fault-handling into ONE place
        (state_machine.c) instead of it being spread across pid.c and
        gate_driver.c. pid.c no longer includes gate_driver.h directly
        (nothing left in that file using it).

        NEW COMMANDS (commands.c/commands.h/cmd_parser.c): `ARM`,
        `DISARM`, `STATE?` -- bare, top-level, matching this project's
        existing bare `FIRE` (system-wide state, not one subsystem's
        namespace). New error code 13: an invalid state-machine
        transition for the current state. PID:PROFile:STARt now checks
        SM_GetState()==ARMED first (ERR 13 if not) before calling
        SM_Fire(). PID:STOP now also calls SM_Stop() (abort, ->IDLE,
        never touches FAULT). FAULT:CLEAR now also calls SM_ClearFault().

        wham_console.py updated to match: the `shot` wizard now sends
        `ARM` immediately before `PID:PROFILE:START` (without this
        fix, every existing `shot` run would have started failing with
        ERR 13 the moment this firmware change landed); `status`/
        `monitor` now show `STATE?` at the top. The module's own
        "Adding a new console command" naming-safety list updated to
        include `arm`/`disarm`/`state` -- these are bare, single-level
        commands with no `*`-prefix exception the way `*IDN?` has, so
        the same collision rule applies to them as literally as to
        `fire`/`pid`.

        *** THINGS ADDED BEYOND THE LITERAL REQUEST, FLAGGED FOR
        REVIEW, NOT SILENTLY ASSUMED WANTED ***:
          - `DISARM` itself -- the user's description covered ARM and
            FIRE explicitly but not a way to stand down from ARMED
            without either firing or faulting; added as ARM's obvious
            companion (a real usability/safety gap without it), but
            flagging it since it wasn't explicitly requested.
          - `HandleGeneralFault()`/`HandleOvercurrentFault()` being
            called from state_machine.c's EnterFault() with BOTH
            PID_Stop() AND PFM_ForceStop() (not just one) -- a
            deliberate "belt and suspenders" choice given this codebase
            still has two coexisting output paths, not something the
            user specified explicitly.

        *** KNOWN GAP, NOT FIXED THIS ROUND ***: PID:START (the
        simpler, non-profile closed-loop start) is NOT gated by this
        state machine at all -- it can start real output regardless of
        STATE?'s value, and never moves the state machine out of IDLE/
        ARMED. Only PID:PROFile:STARt was wired up as "fire," per
        direct instruction to reuse that specific command -- this
        inconsistency is real and worth resolving later, not an
        oversight to silently paper over.

        *** REMINDER, PER DIRECT INSTRUCTION: this state machine is
        explicitly a first pass -- revisit once the work described in
        this entry is complete. Open items, all flagged above and
        collected here for one place to check: (1) fault-type routing
        (General vs. Overcurrent) is completely undecided; (2) both
        fault handlers are empty; (3) ArmConditionsMet() is a stub,
        always allows arming; (4) main-loop fault-poll cadence is
        unbounded; (5) PID:START bypasses the state machine entirely.
        ***

        Build verified: clean rebuild via wham_build.py (all source
        files, since a new one was added), zero warnings. REAL-
        HARDWARE VERIFICATION NOT YET DONE -- board's USB-serial
        adapter is STILL disconnected as of this entry (the fifth
        change in a row made without hardware access -- see the four

2026-09-11  —  *IDN? now reports the exact git commit a firmware build
               was made from (FW_GIT_COMMIT); new wham_build.py closes
               the stale-.bin gotcha as a side effect (build verified;
               real-hardware verification PENDING -- board still
               disconnected)
-----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Change:  Direct request: a way to track which git commit is actually
        flashed on the controller, verifiable via `*IDN?`. Standard
        embedded-firmware technique -- bake the commit hash into a
        generated header, included by the identity command:

          - New `python/gen_git_version.py`: runs `git rev-parse
            --short=8 HEAD` (+ `git status --porcelain` for a dirty-
            tree check) and writes `Core/Inc/git_version.h`
            (`FW_GIT_COMMIT "<hash>"`, `FW_GIT_DIRTY 0|1`). Never fails
            the build over a git problem -- no git repo, no git on
            PATH, no commits yet all fall back to a valid
            `FW_GIT_COMMIT "unknown"` header rather than blocking
            compilation. Deliberately NOT tracked in git (`.gitignore`)
            -- its whole job is capturing "what commit was THIS build
            from," which is redundant with git's own history for a
            clean build (a fresh clone always regenerates it correctly
            for HEAD) and would create spurious diffs on every single
            commit if tracked; the one case that isn't reconstructable
            from git alone -- a build made with uncommitted changes --
            isn't lost either, since it gets baked into the `.elf`/
            `.bin` themselves, which this project deliberately DOES
            track in `Debug/`.
          - `cmd_idn()` (commands.c) now reports a 4th space-separated
            field: `FW_GIT_COMMIT`, with a `-dirty` suffix from
            `FW_GIT_DIRTY` when the working tree had uncommitted
            changes at build time -- flagged explicitly rather than
            silently reporting a hash that doesn't fully describe what
            was actually compiled. `commands.c` now `#include`s the
            new `git_version.h`.

        Also new `python/wham_build.py` -- the canonical way to build
        this project from here on: `gen_git_version.py` -> `make -j4
        all` -> the `.bin` regeneration, as ONE command. This directly
        closes a real, previously-documented gotcha (2026-09-10 entry,
        above): this project's `.cproject` has no "Convert to binary"
        post-build step, so a bare `make` never touches the `.bin` at
        all -- flashing a stale one "succeeds," verified even, while
        silently not containing the latest changes. Three manual steps
        to remember (in the right order, every time) is exactly the
        kind of gap that already bit this project once; one command
        removes the gap rather than just re-documenting it more loudly.
        `docs/command_reference.md`'s `*IDN?` section and `AGENTS.md`'s
        "Build & verify" section both updated to point at it as the
        default; also fixed two unrelated stale references found while
        in that section (`Core/Inc/version.h`, which no longer exists
        -- see the 2026-09-08/09 condensing into `ctrlr_config.h` -- and
        a leftover `Debug/WHAM-PFMG474-V4.elf` filename from the
        sibling project this was reseeded from).

        Build verified: clean rebuild via `wham_build.py`, zero
        warnings, `git_version.h` correctly generated (confirmed
        `74994292-dirty` while this round's own changes were
        uncommitted). REAL-HARDWARE VERIFICATION NOT YET DONE -- the
        board's USB-serial adapter is STILL disconnected as of this
        entry (the fourth change in a row made without hardware
        access -- see the three entries above, all still also
        pending). Once reconnected: build with `wham_build.py` fresh
        against whatever commit this repo is on by then, flash, and
        confirm `*IDN?`'s 4th field matches `git rev-parse --short=8
        HEAD` exactly (no `-dirty` suffix, assuming a clean tree at
        build time) -- then work through the other three pending
        verifications in order.

2026-09-11  —  Per-channel output enable/disable (PID:CHANnel:ENAble)
               -- a genuine "no PFM waveform at all" switch; console
               updated to match (build verified; real-hardware
               verification PENDING -- board still disconnected)
-----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Change:  Direct request: a specific command to enable/disable each of
        the 4 output channels, where disabled means the channel's
        HRTIM output is genuinely never triggered -- distinct from
        every existing per-channel switch. `PID:LOOPMODE`'s open-loop
        mode still drives a real, uncorrected PFM waveform; a 0A
        `PID:PROFile:CURRent` still drives a real PFM waveform (at
        `PFM_TURNON_FREQ_HZ`, representing 0A, not "off"). Neither was
        what was asked for.

        New `HRTIM1_SetChannelOutputEnable(channel, enable)` (hrtim.c/h)
        connects or disconnects ONE channel's output pins LIVE, at any
        time -- reuses the exact mechanism `HRTIM1_PWM_Start()`'s
        existing `channelEnabled[]` parameter already established
        (disconnecting via `OENR`, forcing the same complementary
        output-1-ACTIVE/output-2-INACTIVE level `HRTIM1_PWM_Start()`
        already does at cold start when re-enabling) -- no new HRTIM
        mechanism invented, just made callable for one channel,
        independently, outside the one-shot bring-up call.
        Deliberately does NOT stop the channel's own counter either
        way (same reasoning `HRTIM1_PWM_Start()`'s own disabled-channel
        path already documented: keeps it synchronized so a later
        re-enable is instant and clean, no re-sync dance needed).

        New `outputEnabled` per-channel state (pid.c, defaults to 1 --
        every channel always output, today's only prior behavior,
        unchanged unless explicitly disabled) and
        `PID_SetChannelEnable()`/`PID_GetChannelEnable()` (pid.h/pid.c).
        Setting it: updates the stored state (picked up by the NEXT
        `PID_Start()`/`PID_ProfileStart()` via `channelEnabled[]`, now
        built from real per-channel state instead of hardcoded all-1s)
        AND, if the loop is already running, applies it immediately via
        `HRTIM1_SetChannelOutputEnable()` -- live, mid-shot control, not
        only pre-configuration. `PID_Update()`'s own per-channel loop
        now skips a disabled channel completely at the top (no
        setpoint, no feedback consumption, no PID math, no log entry --
        same "continue" pattern as the other per-channel early-outs).

        New commands `PID:CHANnel:ENAble <ch> <0|1>` / `PID:CHANnel:ENAble?
        <ch>` (commands.c/commands.h/cmd_parser.c), following the exact
        same pattern as every other set/query pair added this project
        (PID:LOOPMODE, PID:GAINS, ...).

        wham_console.py updated to match: new `enable <ch> <on|off>`
        wrapper (asks to confirm only when turning ON -- the direction
        that starts real new output, disabling never asks, always the
        safe direction); `_refresh_config()`/`config` now also reads
        `PID:CHANnel:ENAble?` per channel; the `shot` wizard now asks
        "Ch{N} enabled? yes/no" FIRST for each channel, skipping every
        other prompt for a disabled one, and sends
        `PID:CHANnel:ENAble` explicitly for every channel every run
        (matching this project's "explicit, no implicit magic"
        convention -- same reasoning as `TABLE:BEGIN` before every
        upload -- so a channel enabled in a previous session doesn't
        silently stay enabled just because a later wizard run didn't
        mention it).

        Build verified: clean rebuild, zero warnings. REAL-HARDWARE
        VERIFICATION NOT YET DONE -- board's USB-serial adapter is
        STILL disconnected as of this entry (the third change in a row
        made without hardware access -- see the two entries above).
        Once reconnected, verify ALL THREE pending changes before
        assuming any is solid: (1) per-channel
        `PFM_MAX_CURRENT_A_PER_CHANNEL` clamping, (2) the
        GateDriverStatus fault-recovery fix, (3) this entry --
        specifically confirm `PID:CHANnel:ENAble 2 0` while a shot is

        (scope/DSLogic it if possible, not just PID:STATus?), and that
        `PID:CHANnel:ENAble 2 1` afterward resumes it cleanly from
