2026-09-11  —  REAL BUG FIXED: GateDriverStatus fault during closed-
               loop PID operation left an operator with no way to
               resume without a reboot (build verified; real-hardware
               verification PENDING -- board still disconnected)
-----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Change:  Found while explaining this project's fault-handling
        architecture to the user, then fixed on direct request. Two
        independent fault sources exist: PC10/HRTIM1_FLT6 (real
        hardware fault input, autonomous in silicon) and
        GateDriverStatus_01..12 (software/EXTI-driven, gate_driver.c).
        PID_Update() already polled HRTIM1_FaultIsTripped() every tick
        and correctly called PID_Stop() when tripped -- but never
        checked GateDriver_FaultIsLatched() at all. GateDriver_
        CheckFault()'s PFM_ForceStop() (pfm.c) predates the PID
        architecture (2026-09-09) and was never integrated with it: it
        stops HRTIM output directly (including the Master counter, so
        PID_Update() genuinely stops being invoked), but with nothing
        checking the GateDriverStatus latch inside PID_Update() itself,
        g_running was NEVER cleared by this fault source.

        CONFIRMED impact, not just theoretical: PID_IsRunning()/
        PID:STATus? kept reporting running=1 after a GateDriverStatus
        fault, even with hardware output already genuinely stopped.
        Far more serious: PID_Start()'s own idempotency check
        (`if (g_running != 0U) return 1U;`) then silently turned every
        subsequent PID:START into a no-op -- no reconnect, no
        restarted feedback capture, nothing, and no error reported
        either (a plain "OK" comes back, indistinguishable from a
        real, successful start). FAULT:CLEAR didn't help -- it only
        ever cleared the two hardware/EXTI latches
        (HRTIM1_FaultClear()/GateDriver_FaultClear()), never touched
        g_running. Net effect: an operator had NO way to resume
        closed-loop operation after a GateDriverStatus fault short of
        a full reboot -- silently, with no error message pointing at
        why PID:START "succeeded" but nothing actually happened.

        Fixed with the minimal, already-proven mechanism -- extended
        PID_Update()'s existing fault check
        (`if (HRTIM1_FaultIsTripped() ...)`) to also check
        `GateDriver_FaultIsLatched()`, calling the same real
        PID_Stop() either way. No new recovery path designed or
        needed: this is the exact same bookkeeping (stop the Master
        counter so the ISR doesn't spin forever under already-safed
        outputs, clear g_running so IsRunning()/a later PID_Start()
        both see truthful state) the PC10 path already did correctly
        -- the GateDriverStatus source was just never plugged into it.
        pid.c now includes gate_driver.h.

        Build verified: clean rebuild, zero warnings. REAL-HARDWARE
        VERIFICATION NOT YET DONE -- board's USB-serial adapter is
        still disconnected as of this entry (see the previous entry);
        the board is still running firmware WITHOUT this fix. Once
        reconnected: reflash, then verify directly -- trip a
        GateDriverStatus fault while PID:START is active (or during a
        PID:PROFILE:START shot), confirm PID:STATus? correctly reports
        running=0 immediately, and confirm a subsequent PID:START
        (after FAULT:CLEAR) genuinely reconnects output rather than
        silently no-opping. Also worth re-confirming the PC10 path
        still behaves identically (this change only added a second
        condition to an existing check, but re-verify given how
        serious this exact failure mode turned out to be).

2026-09-11  —  Per-channel Transrex full-range current --
               *** MUST BE CALIBRATED BEFORE FINAL DEPLOYMENT ***
               (build/link verified; real-hardware verification
               PENDING -- board disconnected this session)
-----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Change:  Direct correction: "100kHz corresponds to 5kA" was always
        stated as an approximation, and the 4 physical Transrex
        supplies this controller drives may each have a genuinely
        different full-range output current -- there was no reason to
        assume all four are identical, and nothing has measured any of
        them yet. `PFM_MAX_CURRENT_A` (a single float, ctrlr_config.h)
        replaced with `PFM_MAX_CURRENT_A_PER_CHANNEL` -- a
        HRTIM_NUM_CHANNELS-entry array (ch1..ch4, 0-based internally
        like every other per-channel state in this codebase). Every
        entry is CURRENTLY the same 5000.0f placeholder -- unchanged
        numerically from before, but now explicitly a PER-CHANNEL
        placeholder rather than a single shared one, and flagged with
        a *** MUST BE CALIBRATED BEFORE FINAL DEPLOYMENT *** banner
        comment (ctrlr_config.h) giving the actual required procedure:
        drive each Transrex independently (open-loop is simplest -- a
        known, repeatable commanded frequency) at PFM_MAX_FREQ_HZ and
        measure that supply's REAL output current with real
        instrumentation (a calibrated sensor/shunt on that specific
        supply -- NOT inferred from this controller's own PFM_Input
        feedback, which measures the PFM signal, not load current, and
        cannot validate this mapping on its own).

        PFM_TURNON_FREQ_HZ/PFM_MAX_FREQ_HZ stay single, shared values
        -- nothing so far indicates the turn-on threshold or the top
        of the PFM frequency range themselves differ per Transrex,
        only the CURRENT the top of that range actually produces.
        Revisit if bench testing ever shows otherwise.

        pid.c: new `g_pfmMaxCurrentA[HRTIM_NUM_CHANNELS]` (initialized
        from the macro), with a `_Static_assert` confirming the
        initializer list's entry count actually matches
        HRTIM_NUM_CHANNELS at compile time (so a future edit to either
        one, done inconsistently, fails the BUILD rather than silently
        indexing past the array or leaving a channel's ceiling at 0).
        AmpsToHz() and PID_SetProfileCurrent() (both pid.c) now take
        the channel into account, clamping/converting against THAT
        channel's own entry instead of one value shared by all four --
        meaningful now that entries can genuinely differ once real
        calibration data replaces the placeholder (e.g. a channel with
        a lower real full-range current will correctly refuse a demand
        beyond its own ceiling, something the old shared-constant code
        could never express).

        wham_console.py NOT yet updated to match (still uses a single
        module-level PFM_MAX_CURRENT_A constant for its Amps-axis
        plotting) -- flagged as a follow-up, not done this round; the
        Hz-axis data itself (driven entirely by on-wire values) is
        unaffected, only the console's own plotted Amps conversion
        would need the same per-channel treatment once real per-
        channel values exist to plug in.

        Build verified: clean rebuild, zero warnings, _Static_assert
        passes. REAL-HARDWARE VERIFICATION NOT YET DONE THIS ROUND --
        the board's USB-serial adapter disconnected mid-session
        (/dev/cu.usbserial* stopped enumerating entirely) before
        reflashing could happen; the previously-flashed firmware
        (without this change) is still what's actually running on the
        board as of this entry. Reflash and verify (PID:PROFILE:CURRENT
        clamping, a real shot) once reconnected -- do not assume this
        change is live on hardware from this entry alone.

2026-09-10  —  Simultaneous all-channel waveform logging
               (PID_ArmLogAll()); wham_console.py generates one
               combined plot across every channel from a single shot
-----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Change:  Direct request: "make sure we generate one plot showing the
        shot data from all 4 channels." The waveform log
        (PID_ArmLog()/PID:LOG, 2026-09-10) only ever recorded ONE
        channel at a time -- getting all 4 channels' data meant either
        4 separate runs of the same shot (real feedback noise means no
        two runs are bit-for-bit identical, so not a genuinely
        simultaneous comparison) or a firmware change. Did the latter:

          - g_logSetpoint/g_logMeasured/g_logOutput (pid.c) changed
            from flat [PID_LOG_MAX_SAMPLES] arrays to
            [HRTIM_NUM_CHANNELS][PID_LOG_MAX_SAMPLES] -- a row per
            channel. RAM cost: 48000 bytes total at today's 4
            channels/1000 samples, up from 12000 -- a real, deliberate
            trade-off (bss went from 59.6% to 86.7% of this MCU's 128
            KiB -- confirmed it still links; see docs/memory_report.html
            for current headroom, worth watching before adding much
            more RAM-hungry state).
          - New PID_ArmLogAll(maxSamples, decim) (pid.h/pid.c) arms
            EVERY channel at once, sharing ONE decimation counter/
            sample index -- REAL restructure needed in PID_Update():
            the decimation decision used to happen INSIDE the
            per-channel loop (once per channel that matched
            g_logChannel), which would have advanced the counter 4x
            per real tick under all-channels mode, corrupting the
            timebase exactly the way the previous PID_ArmLog() timebase
            bug did. Moved to a single logThisTick decision computed
            ONCE per PID_Update() call, before the per-channel loop,
            then applied identically to every armed channel inside it
            -- every channel's sample `i` now genuinely comes from the
            same real Master tick.
          - PID:LOG's `<ch>` argument: `0` now means "arm every
            channel" (real channels are 1..N everywhere else in this
            project, leaving 0 a natural unused sentinel rather than a
            whole new command). PID:LOGDATA? gained an OPTIONAL channel
            argument -- omitted, it's UNCHANGED, original behavior
            (valid only when a single channel is armed); given, it
            works in either mode (any channel under all-channels mode,
            or the one actually-armed channel under single-channel
            mode -- ERR 12 for a mismatch in either failure case).
          - python/wham_console.py: `log`'s channel argument now
            accepts `all`; the `shot` wizard's log-target prompt offers
            `all` alongside a channel number or `none`. New
            generate_multi_channel_plot() -- one PNG, one row of
            subplots per channel (shared time axis, each channel's own
            setpoint/output/measured), phase-shaded with the one shared
            ramp/flat-top/ramp-down timing. `plot all` replots an
            already-armed all-channels log without a new shot. Saves
            one combined wide-format CSV (t_s plus 3 columns per
            channel) + one metadata JSON alongside the PNG, same
            shots/ convention as the single-channel path.

        Verified on real hardware: a real 4-channel shot (1.0s ramp /
        1.0s flat-top / 1.0s ramp-down; ch1 3000A closed-loop with real
        fiber feedback, ch2/3/4 1500A/4000A/800A open-loop with no
        feedback wired) produced one PNG with all 4 channels correctly
        shown on the same time axis -- ch1's closed-loop tracking (with
        its own already-documented occasional feedback glitches,
        unaffected by this change) alongside ch2/3/4's open-loop output
        exactly matching their own demand-current trapezoids (AmpsToHz-
        converted peaks: 1500A->~33.7kHz, 4000A->~81kHz, 800A->~20.2kHz,
        all correct). Also verified the protocol edge cases directly:
        `PID:LOGDATA?` with no argument still works exactly as before
        when a single channel is armed; correctly ERRs when all-
        channels mode is armed and no channel is given; `PID:LOGDATA?
        <ch>` correctly ERRs for a channel that isn't the one actually
        armed in single-channel mode.

2026-09-10  —  PID:GAINS?/LOOPMODE?/PROFile:TIMing?/PROFile:CURRent?
               query commands; a real newlib-nano printf-float bug
               found and fixed getting them working; wham_console.py
               now does a live device readback instead of guessing
-----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Change:  Direct response to a gap flagged while building
        wham_console.py (previous entry): PID:GAINS/PID:LOOPMODE/
        PID:PROFILE:TIMING/PID:PROFILE:CURRENT were write-only over
        the wire, forcing any host tool to remember what it itself
        last sent rather than being able to ask the device -- exactly
        the kind of thing that goes stale after a reconnect or if
        another tool/operator touches the board. Added the missing
        query variants:

          - PID_GetGains()/PID_GetLoopMode() (existed already, just
            never wired to a command)/PID_GetProfileTiming()/
            PID_GetProfileCurrent() (pid.h/pid.c) -- straightforward
            readbacks of the same per-channel/shared state the
            setters already maintain. PID_GetProfileTiming() returns
            0 (mapped to ERR 12) when timing was never successfully
            set this boot -- a real, distinct "not configured" state
            (PID_ProfileStart() itself refuses to run in it), not
            silently reported as a bogus "0 0".
          - PID:GAINS? <ch>, PID:LOOPMODE? <ch>, PID:PROFile:TIMing?,
            PID:PROFile:CURRent? <ch> (commands.c/commands.h/
            cmd_parser.c) -- see docs/command_reference.md for exact
            reply formats.

        REAL BUG, caught on real hardware before calling this done:
        the first working build compiled and linked with zero
        warnings, but every one of these new query replies came back
        as "OK   " -- literally blank where the numbers should be
        (e.g. "PID:GAINS? 1" -> "OK   " instead of "OK 1.5 12.5
        0.25"). Root cause: this project links against newlib-nano
        (--specs=nano.specs), which strips floating-point conversion
        support out of printf/snprintf by default to save flash --
        %f/%g silently produce NOTHING (not a crash, not a build
        warning) unless the link also includes -Wl,-u,_printf_float.
        Every existing OK reply in this firmware happened to be
        integer-only (%u/%lu) until these four commands, so this gap
        was invisible until now. Fixed by hand-adding
        -Wl,-u,_printf_float to Debug/makefile's link line (+~6KB
        flash -- tracked in the usual memory-footprint commit).
        Documented in AGENTS.md's build-gotchas section alongside the
        existing subdir.mk/objects.list one, including the
        CubeIDE-UI-equivalent project setting (Project Properties ->
        C/C++ Build -> Settings -> MCU Settings -> "Use float with
        printf from newlib-nano") in case a full IDE-driven makefile
        regeneration ever silently drops the hand-added flag again.
        Confirmed fixed on real hardware: all four query commands
        round-trip exactly what was set (PID:GAINS 1 1.5 12.5 0.25 ->
        PID:GAINS? 1 -> "OK 1.5 12.5 0.25"; PID:PROFILE:CURRENT 1
        2750.5 -> PID:PROFILE:CURRENT? 1 -> "OK 2750.5"), and
        PID:PROFILE:TIMING? correctly returns ERR 12 before any
        PID:PROFILE:TIMING has ever been sent.

        wham_console.py updated to match: a new _refresh_config()
        queries all four live from the device (on every connect, and
        on demand via `config`), replacing the session-local-memory-
        only approach documented (and explicitly caveated as such) in
        the previous entry. `config`'s own docstring and the
        channel_config cache's comment updated to describe what they
        actually are now -- a live device readback, not a guess.

        Verified end to end: full `shot` wizard run (unchanged from
        the previous entry's test) still works correctly after these
        changes -- programmed, watched to auto-stop, logged, and
        plotted with no regressions from either the new commands or
        the printf-float linker change.

2026-09-10  —  wham_console.py: interactive operator console, the new
               maintained front end for this board
-----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Change:  New `python/wham_console.py` -- a `cmd.Cmd`-based REPL
        meant to be THE day-to-day way a human operator works with a
        real board, superseding hand-typed raw SCPI sessions
        (`python/scpi.py`, still present, now a minimal fallback) for
        routine use. Per direct instruction, this is a MAINTAINED
        front end -- extend it as new commands land, rather than
        leaving operators to fall back on raw SCPI for everything new
        (see the file's own "Adding a new console command" section at
        the bottom).

        Capabilities:
          - Raw SCPI passthrough for ANY command -- typing something
            not recognized as a console command (see `help`) sends it
            to the controller verbatim and prints the reply, ERR
            replies decorated with their known meaning
            (docs/command_reference.md's error-code table, duplicated
            here for inline lookup).
          - `shot` -- a guided wizard for the full production shot
            profile: prompts for Ramp Time/Flat Top Time and, per
            channel, Demand Current/loop mode/gains, shows a summary,
            confirms, programs every PID:* command in the right order,
            watches the shot live (PID:STATus? polling), then
            automatically fetches PID:LOGDATA? and saves a CSV +
            JSON metadata + PNG plot under shots/ (gitignored --
            operator-session output, not source). Re-running `shot`
            defaults every prompt to the previous answer, so repeating
            an unchanged shot is just pressing Enter through it. Also
            auto-sizes the log decim so ~1000 samples cover the whole
            programmed shot duration -- encodes the exact lesson
            learned the hard way in the 2026-09-10 shot-demo entries
            above (a mismatched decim silently under-covers a shot).
          - `plot [channel]` -- fetches whatever's currently logged
            (PID:LOGDATA?) right now and saves the same CSV/JSON/PNG,
            without running a new shot -- for replotting after an
            ad-hoc bench test done via raw PID:LOG/PID:RAMP/
            PID:SETPOINT commands.
          - `status [channel]` / `monitor [interval]` -- live,
            multi-channel PID:STATus? tables (monitor loops until
            Ctrl-C).
          - `gains`/`loopmode`/`setpoint`/`ramp`/`start`/`stop`/`log`
            -- friendlier wrappers over the equivalent PID:* commands
            (argument validation, open/closed instead of raw 0/1, and
            -- gains/loopmode/profile current -- remembered in a
            session-local `config` summary, SINCE THE FIRMWARE HAS NO
            QUERY COMMAND for any of PID:GAINS/PID:LOOPMODE/
            PID:PROFILE:TIMING/PID:PROFILE:CURRENT -- explicitly
            labeled as session memory, not a device readback, and
            cleared on reconnect, so the console never claims to know
            something it can't actually verify. A real, flagged gap
            worth a future firmware fix (add the missing `?` query
            variants) but not implemented this round -- out of scope
            for a host-tooling change.
          - A hard-per-tick safety-confirmation gate before anything
            that starts real PWM output (FIRE, PID:START,
            PID:PROFILE:START in any valid SCPI short/long form, BOOT)
            -- `confirm off` to disable for scripted use. Documented as
            a best-effort heuristic, not a clone of cmd_parser.c's own
            short/long-form matcher -- see `_is_dangerous()`'s own
            comment on the exact limitation and how to extend it.
          - `connect`/`disconnect`/`ports`/`reflash` (shells out to
            the existing `wham_serial_flash.py`, closing/reopening
            this console's own link around it) -- connection/firmware-
            lifecycle management without leaving the tool.
          - Every command sent and reply received is timestamped into
            a per-session transcript under logs/ (gitignored)
            automatically -- an audit trail with zero operator effort,
            matching how a real control-room console logs everything.

        Design note worth recording: console meta-command names
        (connect, status, shot, plot, gains, ...) were chosen
        specifically so NONE of them can ever be typed (in any case)
        as the leading identchar token of a real SCPI mnemonic --
        cmd.Cmd dispatches on an exact, case-sensitive match against
        `do_<token>`, so a name like `fault` or `pid` would silently
        SHADOW raw passthrough for that whole command family whenever
        an operator happened to type it in matching case (e.g. a
        lowercase "fault?" would hit a hypothetical `do_fault` instead
        of reaching the device, while "FAULT?" would still pass
        through -- a real, confusing inconsistency, caught during
        design rather than left for an operator to discover). Documented
        explicitly in the file's own header comment and "Adding a new
        console command" section so this constraint survives future
        edits.

        docs/command_reference.md was also stale (documented only
        through QSPI:ID?, missing the entire PFMIN:*/PID:* command
        families added across the last several sessions) -- brought
        up to date alongside this tool, including the error-code table
        (was missing codes 8-12) and a note on the new hard output
        slew-rate clamp.

        Verified on real hardware: full `shot` wizard run (1.0s ramp /
        1.0s flat-top / 1.0s ramp-down, 3000A, channel 1, closed-loop)
        -- programmed correctly, watched live to completion (auto-stop
        detected, no PID:STOP needed), fetched the log, and produced a
        correct plot showing the output slew-rate clamp's effect (clean
        Output trace, glitches only in raw Measured) automatically,
        with zero hand-written one-off script code. Also verified:
        `plot` re-fetching an already-armed log without a new shot,
        disconnect/reconnect, and the danger-confirmation gate
        correctly blocking a raw `PID:START` until confirmed.

2026-09-10  —  Hard per-tick output slew-rate clamp, configurable in
               ctrlr_config.h; CONFIRMED on real hardware it stops the
               DSLogic-verified output glitch from reaching HRTIM
-----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Change:  Direct response to the previous entry's DSLogic-confirmed
        finding (a single bad feedback sample could make PID_Update()
        genuinely WRITE a wildly wrong value to HRTIM for one tick,
        not just log one). Added PID_OUTPUT_MAX_SLEW_HZ_PER_TICK
        (ctrlr_config.h, default 2000 Hz/tick, PLACEHOLDER pending
        real Transrex/magnet slew-tolerance characterization -- same
        status as PFM_TURNON_FREQ_HZ/PFM_MAX_FREQ_HZ) and a new
        ClampOutputSlew() (pid.c): bounds how much ANY channel's
        actual output-to-HRTIM write may differ from the PREVIOUS
        tick's actual output, in either direction. Applied
        UNCONDITIONALLY -- open-loop and closed-loop alike, whether
        the desired value came from PID math, a plain PID_SetSetpoint()
        step, PID_StartRamp(), or the shot-profile generator -- since a
        hard clamp meant to protect real downstream hardware has to
        apply regardless of which code path computed the number. Sits
        as a second, independent stage after the existing
        [PID_OUTPUT_MIN_HZ, PID_OUTPUT_MAX_HZ] range clamp. The
        existing directional anti-windup check (2026-09-09) needed no
        new logic to cover it -- it already compares the FINAL written
        value against the raw PID output and blocks integration only
        when that difference would worsen the same-direction
        saturation, which is exactly correct whether range-clamping or
        slew-clamping (or both) caused the difference.

        Also fixed a small related correctness issue while in this
        code: PID_Start() used to reset lastOutputHz to 0 -- an
        artificial value 0 was never actually a real, physically-
        achievable output (the hardware floor is PID_OUTPUT_MIN_HZ,
        3000 Hz), so the very first tick after every PID_Start() would
        have had the slew clamp count a fake, oversized "jump" from a
        starting point that never really existed, needlessly slowing
        the first real response. Now initializes to PID_OUTPUT_MIN_HZ.

        NOTE, understood trade-off, not accidental: a hard clamp
        cannot distinguish "PID reacting correctly to a large real
        error" from "PID reacting to one bad sample" -- both produce a
        big desired jump, and this clamp bounds both equally. That's
        the whole point of a HARD clamp (per direct instruction): it
        protects real hardware from the failure mode directly, rather
        than first trying to correctly classify every possible bad
        sample. Outlier-rejecting PfmInput_ConsumeAveragePeriod()'s
        result before it reaches PID math (the other option raised in
        the previous entry) remains a reasonable COMPLEMENTARY future
        addition, not a replacement -- not implemented this round.

        CONFIRMED on real hardware: re-ran the identical 1.0s/1.0s/1.0s,
        3000A, channel-1 shot from the previous two entries. The
        logged Output trace is now completely clean -- largest
        consecutive-logged-sample delta across the whole shot was 2613
        Hz (log decimation is 3 ticks/sample, so up to 3x
        PID_OUTPUT_MAX_SLEW_HZ_PER_TICK=2000 is physically possible
        between logged samples; 2613 is comfortably under that, and
        nowhere close to the previous ~20000-90000 Hz spike
        magnitudes). The underlying feedback-measurement anomaly this
        clamp works around is UNCHANGED and still visible in the same
        shot's logged Measured column (raw, unfiltered feedback, by
        design, so the anomaly stays diagnosable) -- exactly the
        intended split: the clamp protects what reaches real hardware,
        it doesn't hide or fix the root cause in the measurement path.
        Plots (scratchpad only, sent to the user) show this precisely:
        Output is glitch-free, Measured still shows the same spikes as
        before.

2026-09-10  —  Independent DSLogic capture CONFIRMS the shot profile
               against ground truth, AND upgrades the single-tick
               "feedback dropout" finding to a confirmed REAL output
               glitch, not a measurement artifact
-----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Change:  User captured the same 1.0s/1.0s/1.0s, 3000A, channel-1
        shot (previous entry, above) with a DSLogic U3Pro32 logic
        analyzer (250 MHz, probing channel 1's real HRTIM output pin
        directly) as an independent, firmware-blind ground truth,
        specifically to cross-check the firmware's own self-reported
        PID:LOGDATA? trace against reality. Decoded the .dsl capture
        (a zip archive; libsigrok/DSView's native per-channel bit-
        packed raw format -- 8 channels, 75x ~2MB chunks each,
        concatenated in filename order = that channel's full 1-bit/
        sample stream at the session's sample rate) with a one-off
        numpy script (scratchpad only, not part of the repo):
        rising-edge-to-rising-edge period -> instantaneous frequency,
        then a 1ms windowed average of RAW PERIODS (deliberately
        matching PfmInput_ConsumeAveragePeriod()'s own averaging
        convention, for a genuinely apples-to-apples comparison, not
        just a similar-looking one).

        Two of the capture's 8 channels (edge count ~253k over the
        3.0s window, vs. the other 6 channels' ~30k -- which matches a
        CONSTANT PFM_TURNON_FREQ_HZ=5000 idle signal exactly: 5000 Hz
        x 3.0s x 2 edges/period = 30000) carried channel 1's real
        signal -- almost certainly two probe points on the same
        physical path (e.g. either side of the fiber link): distinct
        files, but identical edge counts and an essentially identical
        frequency-vs-time shape throughout.

        RESULT: excellent agreement. Interpolating the DSLogic ground
        truth onto the firmware's own logged timestamps and comparing
        against the firmware's self-reported Output: median absolute
        residual 109.8 Hz over a 3000-62000 Hz signal (well under
        0.5%), mean residual -150 Hz (no systematic bias). The full-
        shot and ramp-up-detail overlay plots (scratchpad only --
        dslogic_vs_firmware.png/dslogic_vs_firmware_rampup.png, sent
        to the user) show the DSLogic trace tracking the firmware's
        self-reported Output essentially exactly, everywhere, at every
        visible timescale.

        UPGRADED FINDING, real and worth flagging clearly: the
        isolated single-tick spikes documented in the previous entry
        were hypothesized there as a feedback-measurement artifact
        (PfmInput's averaging window seeing stale/incomplete data).
        DSLogic's independent, firmware-blind capture shows the EXACT
        SAME spikes, at the same times, at the same magnitudes, on the
        real HRTIM output pin -- which means the firmware's self-
        reported "Output" value during those ticks was NOT a logging
        or measurement artifact: the PID controller genuinely computed
        and wrote a wildly wrong PER value to HRTIM for that one tick
        (e.g. ~89 kHz against a 62 kHz setpoint, confirmed physically
        present on the wire), not just claimed to. The most likely
        mechanism is unchanged (a single missed/delayed capture edge
        feeding one bad "measured" sample into that tick's PID math,
        which then computes and WRITES a real bad output before the
        next tick's clean feedback corrects it within 2-3 ticks) --
        what's upgraded is the confidence that this is a real, externally-
        verified physical event, not a self-consistent bug confined to
        the firmware's own reporting.

        Practical implication, flagged for the user's decision, not
        acted on unprompted: on a bench loopback this is harmless (a
        ~1ms-long correction), but a real Transrex supply/magnet
        wouldn't have a firmware-level guard against a one-tick command
        excursion like this today. Two independent, complementary
        hardening options worth considering before real hardware is in
        the loop: (1) outlier-reject PfmInput_ConsumeAveragePeriod()'s
        result before it reaches PID_Update()'s error calculation
        (e.g. reject a period more than ~2x/0.5x the previous tick's),
        or (2) a maximum per-tick output slew-rate clamp in
        PID_Update() independent of the PID math entirely (a hard
        ceiling on how much outputHz can change tick-to-tick,
        regardless of what the raw PID computation says) -- the second
        is more robust since it bounds the failure mode directly
        rather than relying on correctly classifying every possible
        bad sample. Neither implemented this round.

2026-09-10  —  Shot-profile CONFIRMED end-to-end on real hardware; a
               real waveform-log timebase bug found and fixed; gains
               re-tuned for the shot's own timescale; a real single-
               tick feedback-dropout characteristic documented
-----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Change:  Demonstrated the shot-profile feature (previous entry,
        below) end-to-end on real hardware: programmed a real 1.0s
        ramp-up / 1.0s flat-top / 1.0s ramp-down shot at 3000A demand
        current on channel 1 (the channel with a real fiber feedback
        loopback wired), ran it closed-loop, logged setpoint/output/
        measured at 333 Hz for the whole 3.0s shot, and plotted the
        result (scratchpad only, not part of the repo -- static PNGs
        sent directly to the user). Two real, hardware-confirmed
        issues surfaced doing this and were fixed/documented before
        calling the demonstration valid:

        1. REAL BUG, waveform-log timebase: PID_ArmLog()'s logging
           block sat INSIDE the "closed-loop, have fresh feedback"
           and "open-loop" branches of PID_Update()'s per-channel
           loop -- a tick with no fresh feedback (see below) never
           reached it, so it didn't count toward `decim` or get
           logged. PID_GetLogSampleRateHz() reports a fixed
           PID_LOOP_RATE_HZ/decim rate that a host-side plotter uses
           to reconstruct each sample's timestamp from its index --
           an assumption that silently breaks whenever any ticks are
           skipped this way, compressing the reported time axis.
           Confirmed on real hardware before the fix: a 3.0s shot's
           log (PID:LOG 1 1000 3, nominally 1000 samples over the
           whole 3.0s) stopped 611 samples short, covering only
           ~1.83s of its own claimed timebase. Fixed (Core/Src/pid.c)
           by moving the logging block OUTSIDE all the feedback-
           freshness branching -- it now runs once per REAL Master
           tick for the armed channel unconditionally, logging
           whatever setpoint/measured/output currently hold (freshly
           computed, or held over from the last tick that had
           feedback). A stale-feedback stretch now shows up as a
           genuine flat/staircase segment in the log -- the honest
           picture of what the loop is actually doing -- instead of
           silently vanishing from the time axis. Verified: the same
           3.0s shot logged the full 1000/1000 samples, covering
           exactly 3.003s.

        2. Gains re-tuned for THIS shot's timescale: the 2026-09-09
           gains (Kp=0.02, Ki=0.5) were tuned for a 10ms micro-ramp
           and took 1.8+ SECONDS to converge on a full-scale step --
           far too slow for a 1s shot-profile ramp segment (confirmed
           by re-running the original shot with these old gains
           first: measured never got within half of setpoint before
           the shot ended). A bench step-response sweep (scratchpad
           gain_tune.py/gain_tune2.py, both directions -- up AND down,
           since the real shot ramps both ways) found Kp=1.5/Ki=15
           starts oscillating and Kp=2.5/Ki=25 is fully unstable
           (rail-to-rail between PID_OUTPUT_MIN_HZ/MAX_HZ) -- settled
           on Kp=1.0/Ki=10/Kd=0, a comfortable stability margin with
           full-scale step convergence to >99% within ~1s and no
           overshoot in either direction. With these gains the actual
           shot tracked its own setpoint closely throughout (within
           the DMA-chunk feedback lag described below) and converged
           essentially exactly during the 1s flat-top hold. NOTE:
           these gains are specific to this bench loopback (a near-
           unity-gain "plant" -- HRTIM output looped straight back via
           fiber to PFM_Input, no real physical inertia) and will need
           re-tuning again against a real Transrex supply, whose own
           physical response time constant is unknown and was
           explicitly flagged as a real possibility during the
           original shot-profile design discussion ("There could be a
           lot of time delay in the response of our supply to the
           demand (on the order of milliseconds)").

        A real, DOCUMENTED-NOT-FIXED characteristic of the existing
        (pre-dating this session) capture pipeline, observed clearly
        for the first time under this demonstration's continuous
        multi-second frequency sweep (earlier tests were either a
        static hold or one 10ms ramp, neither exercised this): roughly
        once every ~500ms, a single PID_Update() tick's
        PfmInput_ConsumeAveragePeriod() read back a period read almost
        exactly DOUBLE the true value (i.e. ~half the true frequency)
        -- the classic signature of one missed capture edge -- causing
        the PID to react to a fake large error for exactly that one
        tick (a momentary output spike, e.g. ~89kHz against a 62kHz
        setpoint) before the next tick's clean data corrected it within
        2-3 ticks (~6-9ms). Confirmed via the raw log (shot_demo_parsed
        .csv, scratchpad only): every occurrence is a single isolated
        sample, immediately preceded and followed by clean, consistent
        readings -- not a sustained divergence, and it did not disturb
        the shot's overall shape, convergence, or auto-stop timing.
        Root cause not yet investigated (candidates: fiber/optical
        noise, or a genuine edge case in PFM_DMA_BUF_LEN=32's half-
        buffer chunk boundary) -- flagged as a real follow-up (e.g.
        outlier rejection in the averaging, or instrumenting the DMA
        capture path directly) but explicitly NOT blocking, since the
        shot-profile feature itself is now proven correct independent
        of this pre-existing capture-pipeline characteristic.

2026-09-10  —  Windowed-average feedback + real production shot
               profile (Ramp/Flat-Top/Demand Current) + Amps<->Hz +
               open/closed-loop mode, CONFIRMED on real hardware
-----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Change:  Replaces the single-latest-sample feedback and the ad hoc
        static-setpoint/PID:RAMP-only demand of 2026-09-09 with the
        real production architecture, fully specified across a design
        discussion before any code was written (see this project's own
        session notes for the back-and-forth; summarized here as the
        final, implemented design):

          - Core/Src/pfm_input.c, Core/Inc/pfm_input.h: new
            PfmInput_ConsumeAveragePeriod(channel, &avgPeriodTicks,
            &sampleCount) -- averages RAW periods (not frequencies,
            per direct instruction: convert to Hz only after
            averaging) over everything captured since the last
            consume, then resets the accumulator ("reciprocal
            frequency counting", standard practice for this kind of
            feedback). The averaging window's boundary is NOT synced
            to the Master heartbeat exactly -- doesn't need to be, per
            direct instruction, since the goal is a solid measurement
            of actual supply output, not exact period alignment.
            Lock-free by construction, not by an added critical
            section: HRTIM1_Master_IRQn (priority 1) can always
            preempt the DMA capture ISR (priority 3) but never the
            reverse, so a read+reset from Master's context can never
            race a concurrent accumulate from the DMA ISR.

          - Core/Inc/ctrlr_config.h: PFM_TURNON_FREQ_HZ (5000,
            placeholder pending bench characterization) /
            PFM_MAX_FREQ_HZ (100000) / PFM_MAX_CURRENT_A (5000.0f, ~5kA
            nominal) -- the Amps<->Hz calibration layer, nested inside
            the existing hardware-register safety clamp
            (PID_OUTPUT_MIN_HZ/MAX_HZ, 3000/150000). Below
            PFM_TURNON_FREQ_HZ the Transrex supply outputs 0A; above
            PFM_MAX_FREQ_HZ its output saturates around
            PFM_MAX_CURRENT_A.

          - Core/Inc/pid.h, Core/Src/pid.c: the real shot-profile
            engine.
              * TrapezoidalCurrentA(elapsedTicks, demandCurrentA):
                0A -> linear up-ramp -> demandCurrentA -> flat-top ->
                linear down-ramp -> 0A, computed ON THE FLY once per
                Master heartbeat from a compact {rampTicks,
                flatTopTicks, demandCurrentA} state -- NOT a stored
                per-period table (not enough RAM for one, per direct
                instruction; this is the entire reason the profile is
                parametric rather than tabulated).
              * AmpsToHz(currentA): LINEAR PLACEHOLDER mapping
                [0, PFM_MAX_CURRENT_A] -> [PFM_TURNON_FREQ_HZ,
                PFM_MAX_FREQ_HZ], with 0A landing EXACTLY on
                PFM_TURNON_FREQ_HZ (no "off but nonzero" output state
                between 0A and turn-on), per direct instruction. Real
                Transrex SCR/phase-controlled-rectifier physics may
                not be linear (a cosine-like relationship between
                firing angle and output current is a real possibility)
                -- flagged in both ctrlr_config.h and pid.c as
                provisional, explicitly deferred to post-hardware-
                characterization, not silently assumed correct.
              * ONE shared/synchronized shot clock (g_profileElapsedTicks
                et al.), not one per channel -- per direct instruction,
                every channel's ramp/flat-top/ramp shape must stay
                perfectly synchronized in time even though each
                channel has its own peak demandCurrentA. Checked for
                completion BEFORE any channel is touched each tick.
              * PID_SetProfileTiming(rampTimeMs, flatTopTimeMs) /
                PID_SetProfileCurrent(channel, demandCurrentA) /
                PID_ProfileStart() / PID_IsProfileActive(): the new
                public API. PID_ProfileStart() both zeroes the shared
                clock and calls PID_Start() -- per direct instruction,
                closed-loop correction begins the instant PFM output
                does, not as a separate step.
              * End of shot (shared clock reaches total duration):
                FULL STOP via PID_Stop() -- PFM output off entirely on
                every channel -- per direct instruction, not
                hold-at-floor. An operator must send
                PID:PROFILE:START again for another shot; nothing
                resumes on its own.
              * Per-channel open/closed-loop mode
                (PID_SetLoopMode()/PID_GetLoopMode(), default
                closed-loop): open-loop writes the profile/setpoint
                value straight to HRTIM every tick with NO PID error
                correction at all; feedback is still consumed and
                still recorded (lastMeasuredHz, the waveform log) for
                reporting/comparison, it just never influences that
                channel's output. Raised as a good idea during design
                and confirmed wanted, per direct instruction.
              * Zero-samples-this-window handling (a channel's
                PfmInput_ConsumeAveragePeriod() returns no fresh data):
                per direct instruction, hold the previous output and
                skip that channel's update for the tick -- there can
                be real millisecond-scale delay in the Transrex
                supply's own response to a demand change, so this is a
                legitimate operating condition, not necessarily a
                fault.
              * PID_Update() restructured around all of the above: a
                once-per-tick shared-clock completion check, then per
                channel -- profile-or-ramp setpoint, windowed-average
                feedback consume, open-loop branch (write straight
                through) OR closed-loop branch (existing PID math,
                unchanged, including the 2026-09-09 directional
                anti-windup fix) gated on fresh feedback.

          - Core/Src/commands.c, Core/Inc/commands.h, Core/Src/
            cmd_parser.c: four new wire commands -- PID:LOOPMODE <ch>
            <0|1>, PID:PROFile:TIMing <rampTimeS> <flatTopTimeS>
            (operator-facing unit is seconds, matching the real
            5-15s/1-15s nominal ranges; converted to PID_Update()'s own
            millisecond/tick units internally), PID:PROFile:CURRent
            <ch> <demandCurrentA>, PID:PROFile:STARt. Error codes 11
            (invalid channel)/12 (invalid argument), matching every
            existing PID:* command's convention.

        CONFIRMED on real hardware (bench, fiber-link feedback on one
        channel, none on another): armed PID:LOOPMODE 1 0 (ch1
        open-loop) / PID:PROFILE:TIMING 1 0.5 (1s ramp, 0.5s flat-top,
        2.5s total) / PID:PROFILE:CURRENT on two channels, then
        PID:PROFILE:START and polled PID:STATus? through the whole
        shot. Channel 1's setpoint/output climbed through the up-ramp
        (~38.5kHz), held near the top through flat-top (~51.5kHz),
        descended through the down-ramp (~12.8kHz), and landed at
        PFM_TURNON_FREQ_HZ (5047, i.e. the trapezoid's 0A floor) right
        as the shot ended -- exactly the commanded shape. Channel 2 (no
        feedback physically connected on this channel this bench run)
        correctly held its output at the closed-loop "no fresh
        feedback" default (never wrote a first sample) rather than
        diverging or erroring, confirming the zero-samples handling.
        `running` (PID_IsRunning(), reported by PID:STATus?) flipped
        1 -> 0 automatically right around the shot's own 2.5s total
        duration, with no PID:STOP sent -- confirms the shared clock
        and auto-stop-at-completion both work as designed.

        A real process gotcha, not a firmware bug: Debug/
        WHAM-XREX-PFMG474.bin is NOT produced by `make all` (grep of
        Makefile/objects.mk/sources.mk turns up no .bin rule at all --
        only the .elf/.list) -- it has to be regenerated by hand
        (arm-none-eabi-objcopy -O binary WHAM-XREX-PFMG474.elf
        WHAM-XREX-PFMG474.bin) after every rebuild before
        wham_serial_flash.py's default --bin path is valid. The very
        first flash attempt this round silently flashed a STALE .bin
        (from before this round's source changes) that verified fine
        (its own bytes matched what was written) while every new
        PID:LOOPMODE/PID:PROFile:* command still reported "ERR 1
        Unknown command" -- confusing at first because the flash
        itself reported success. Caught by comparing the .bin's
        mtime against the freshly-rebuilt .o files' mtimes (.bin was
        over an hour older); fixed by regenerating it and reflashing,
        confirmed by the command tests above then passing. Worth a
        real fix (a Makefile post-build rule, or teaching
        wham_serial_flash.py to invoke objcopy itself) -- not done in
        this round, flagged for follow-up.

2026-09-09  —  Closed-loop PID controller implemented, a real
               anti-windup bug found and fixed, CONFIRMED converging
               on real hardware
-----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Change:  Implemented the architecture designed in the entry below --
        Possibility 3 + fixed-rate Master heartbeat, 4 independent
        channels. New/changed files:

          - Core/Inc/pid.h, Core/Src/pid.c (new): the control loop
            itself. Per-channel {setpoint, Kp/Ki/Kd, integral,
            derivative history} state, fully independent across
            channels. PID_Update() -- called once per Master heartbeat
            from HRTIM1_Master_IRQHandler() -- reads each channel's
            latest measured period (pfm_input.c), converts to Hz,
            runs that channel's PID with a fixed Delta-t, clamps to
            [PID_OUTPUT_MIN_HZ, PID_OUTPUT_MAX_HZ] (ctrlr_config.h,
            3000-150000 Hz -- the floor is a real HRTIM 16-bit-PER
            hardware limit, not a tuning choice), writes the result via
            hrtim.c's new HRTIM1_SetChannelPeriod(). PID_Start()/
            PID_Stop() begin/end closed-loop operation (PFM_Input
            continuous capture + HRTIM output together); PID:START/
            STOP/SETPOINT/GAINS/STATus? (commands.c/.h, cmd_parser.c)
            are the wire interface, ERR 11/12 added.

          - Core/Src/hrtim.c, Core/Inc/hrtim.h: HRTIM1_FullInit()'s
            per-channel timer config changed to Possibility 3 for every
            active channel (ResetTrigger=NONE, UpdateTrigger=NONE,
            ResetUpdate=ENABLED -- shadow->active promotion on that
            channel's own roll-over, no Master involvement, no
            relationship between channels). Master's own timebase
            repurposed from "shared PWM carrier" to "PID heartbeat"
            (HRTIM_MASTER_PID_PERIOD/_PRESCALE, derived from
            ctrlr_config.h's new PID_LOOP_RATE_HZ). The old
            HRTIM1_ApplyPfmStep() (one shared `per` + a Master-CMPk
            phase-offset array) replaced by HRTIM1_SetChannelPeriod()
            (one channel, one `per`, CMP1 always recomputed as per/2 --
            see that function's own comment for why a fixed
            *proportional* 50% duty, not a fixed tick count, is the
            right default for a V-to-F tone). The old
            HRTIM1_WaitForPhaseAndConnect()/multi-step cold-start phase
            dance in HRTIM1_PWM_Start() -- needed only to bring up a
            phase-LOCKED carrier cleanly -- removed entirely; the 4
            independent channels have no phase relationship to get
            right at cold start, so there's nothing left to wait for.
            HRTIM1_ApplyPfmStep() kept as a thin compatibility shim
            (ignores cmp[]/phase[], just calls HRTIM1_SetChannelPeriod()
            per channel) so pfm.c's inherited TABLE:STEP/FIRE path
            still links and does something sane, though it's no longer
            this project's real output path.

          - Core/Src/pfm_input.c, Core/Inc/pfm_input.h: new continuous/
            free-running capture mode (PfmInput_StartContinuous()/
            StopContinuous()/GetLatestPeriod()) alongside the existing
            bounded bench-capture path (PFMIN:CAPTURE/STATus?/DATA?,
            unchanged) -- no target count, no array accumulation
            (can't overflow PFM_INPUT_MAX_PERIODS), just one
            continuously-overwritten latest-period scalar per channel,
            for a live consumer instead of a bench snapshot. A
            `running` flag shared between both modes keeps them from
            colliding on the same channel's DMA/timer resource.

          - ctrlr_config.h: HRTIM_NUM_CHANNELS raised 3 -> 4 (repurposed
            meaning: independent PID channels, not phase-locked ones --
            see that file's own updated comment). New PID_LOOP_RATE_HZ
            (1000, a first conservative guess, NOT yet tuned against
            real Transrex/magnet electrical time constants) and
            PID_OUTPUT_MIN_HZ/MAX_HZ (3000/150000).

        REAL BUG, found and fixed via real-hardware bench testing, not
        just code review: the first working build's anti-windup logic
        (`if (clampedHzF == outputHzF) { integrate }` -- freeze the
        integrator any time output is clamped, regardless of direction)
        deadlocked in exactly the common starting condition -- setpoint
        well above PID_OUTPUT_MIN_HZ, output computes small before the
        integral has built up, gets clamped UP to the floor, and the
        old check then refused to integrate because output was
        "clamped" -- even though integrating was exactly what would
        have escaped the clamp. Confirmed on real hardware: channel 1's
        output AND measured feedback (a real loopback wire from earlier
        WHAM-PFMG474-V4 bench testing, PFM_Input_01<->HRTIM channel 0,
        still connected) both sat frozen at exactly 3000 Hz indefinitely
        with setpoint=20000, Kp=0.02, Ki=0.5 -- error stayed large and
        positive, integral never moved. Fixed: only block integration
        when it would make the SAME saturation worse (clamped-high with
        still-positive error, or clamped-low with still-negative
        error); otherwise integrating is what should happen to escape
        the rail. See pid.c's own comment on this fix for the full
        writeup -- a genuinely easy mistake worth remembering.

        CONFIRMED on real hardware after the fix, same setpoint/gains,
        channel 1 (the one channel with a real loopback wired):
        measured/output climbed smoothly and monotonically from
        ~4100 Hz toward the 20000 Hz setpoint over 3 seconds (4098 ->
        7317 -> 9883 -> 11928 -> 13544 -> 14851 -> 15896 -> 16727 ->
        17384 -> 17911 -> 18332 -> 18671, at 0.25 s intervals), output
        tracking measured almost exactly throughout (confirming the
        loopback is real and the whole chain -- PFM_Input capture ->
        Hz conversion -> PID -> HRTIM per-channel write -> real output
        -> real feedback -- is genuinely closed). A classic smooth
        asymptotic approach, exactly the expected shape for these
        gains against whatever this loopback's own effective time
        constant is (not a real magnet -- see "explicitly NOT yet
        resolved" below).

        Explicitly NOT yet resolved (real open items, not implementation
        detail):

          - Kp/Ki/Kd used above (0.02/0.5/0) were arbitrary bench-test
            values against a wire loopback, not tuned against any real
            Transrex/magnet plant -- expect this to need real retuning
            once real hardware is in the loop.
          - Only channel 1 has ever been verified end-to-end (the one
            channel with a physical loopback on this bench right now).
            Channels 2-4 build and report status correctly but are
            functionally untested -- nothing physically wired to their
            PFM_Input feedback pins yet.
          - PID_LOOP_RATE_HZ (1000 Hz) is a first guess, not validated
            against real magnet-supply control-bandwidth requirements.
          - A latched hardware fault (PID_Update()'s own check) calls
            PID_Stop() but there is no command yet to explicitly
            re-arm/restart PID after a FAULT:CLEAR -- an operator must
            send PID:START again manually once real fault-recovery
            behavior is decided.
          - Everything from the previous entry's own "explicitly NOT
            yet resolved" list that this pass didn't touch (FDM fault
            decode, COMMS/Ethernet, fiber-optic I/O, E-stop hardware
            independence) is still fully out of scope, per direct
            instruction.

  Files:  Core/Inc/pid.h (new), Core/Src/pid.c (new), Core/Src/hrtim.c,
        Core/Inc/hrtim.h, Core/Src/pfm_input.c, Core/Inc/pfm_input.h,
        Core/Inc/ctrlr_config.h, Core/Src/commands.c, Core/Inc/commands.h,
        Core/Src/cmd_parser.c, Core/Src/stm32g4xx_it.c, Core/Src/main.c,
        Debug/Core/Src/subdir.mk, Debug/objects.list (STM32CubeIDE's
        generated-Makefile gotcha, same as every previous Core/Src
        addition -- see AGENTS.md)

2026-09-09  —  Project start: reseeded from WHAM-PFMG474-V4, closed-loop
               PID architecture for 4 independent Transrex channels
               designed (no code yet)
-----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Change:  New project. Two documents (docs/Transrex/) define the goal:
        Transrex_Controls_Upgrade (1).pdf specifies the Transrex ISR-2126
        magnet-supply upgrade this controller serves -- in particular its
        Control Unit I/O table (Sec. 7.1), a 4-independent-unit interface
        each with its own PFM DEMAND output (0-100 kHz, V-to-F encoded
        firing-angle command) and PFM UNIT_CURRENT feedback input (same
        encoding, from a V/F current sense). hrtim_divergent_period_timing.pdf
        works out how HRTIM behaves when independent channels need
        arbitrary, unrelated frequencies -- its "Possibility 3" (per-timer
        ResetUpdate=TIMUPDATEONRESET_ENABLED, ResetTrigger=NONE,
        UpdateTrigger=NONE, so each channel's shadow->active transfer
        happens on ITS OWN roll-over, never mid-cycle, with no
        relationship required between channels) is the architecture this
        project is built around.

        Scope, per direct confirmation: closed-loop PID control only, on
        THIS EXACT HARDWARE (same STM32G474QET6, same pin mapping this
        project's base already uses, same board already on the bench) --
        every other requirement in the Transrex spec (fiber-optic I/O,
        FDM fault decode, the LabVIEW Ethernet COMMS link, digitizers,
        E-stop hardware-independence, the other 3 non-controller
        subsystems) is explicitly OUT of scope for now.

        Reseeded (not a GitHub fork -- see AGENTS.md's "Provenance") from
        WHAM-PFMG474-V4 at commit de83324, full working snapshot, fresh
        git history. Board/firmware identity (HW_BOARD_NAME,
        FW_VERSION_STRING, the .cproject/.project/.ioc/.launch project
        name, the Debug/ build artifact name) renamed throughout;
        HW_BOARD_REV left alone (same physical PCB). Historical entries
        below this one predate the fork and describe WHAM-PFMG474-V4's
        own development -- kept verbatim, since the code they explain
        (hrtim.c's NVIC priority scheme, pfm_input.c's DMA capture
        design, etc.) is still sitting in this repo unchanged so far.
        docs/memory_history.csv / docs/memory_report.html were NOT
        carried over (they reference WHAM-PFMG474-V4's own commit SHAs,
        meaningless here) -- python/memory_report.py itself was kept and
        will build fresh history from this repo's own commits.

        Architecture, as designed in conversation (not yet implemented):

          - NO shared PFM_Step_t table for this mode. Each of the 4
            channels is an independent {setpoint, PID state (Kp/Ki/Kd,
            integral accumulator), feedback source, output timer} tuple
            -- there is no pre-computed trajectory to walk, every output
            value is computed live.

          - Master-repetition interrupt (HRTIM1_Master_IRQn) is KEPT,
            deliberately, as a FIXED-RATE heartbeat -- this was
            explicitly reconsidered mid-design and is worth not
            re-litigating without rereading this entry. The first-draft
            idea (each channel's own roll-over triggers ITS OWN PID
            recompute, fully self-clocked, no Master involvement) was
            rejected: it ties each channel's control-loop sample
            interval to that channel's own instantaneous carrier
            frequency, which is itself what the PID is adjusting --
            variable sample-time PID is materially harder to tune/
            guarantee-stable than fixed sample-time PID, and conflates
            "how fast the V-to-F encoding happens to cycle" with "how
            fast the control loop should run" (the latter should track
            the Transrex/magnet's own electrical time constant, which
            has nothing to do with the demand signal's encoding rate).
            Master's role in Possibility 3 was never really "step a
            shared table" (that's just what the reference document's
            own example was doing with it) -- its real, general role is
            "a fixed-cadence, always-safe moment to WRITE shadow
            registers," which is exactly what a fixed-rate PID heartbeat
            needs, and keeping it doesn't reintroduce any of the mid-
            cycle hazard Possibilities 1/2 had, because the actual
            hardware APPLY still happens at each channel's own
            roll-over, per Possibility 3, regardless of what wrote the
            shadow or when.

          - Per Master tick, loop over the 4 channels: read PFM_Input's
            LATEST captured period for that channel's feedback (a new,
            simpler capture mode than today's PFMIN:CAPTURE <M>/STATus?/
            DATA? -- free-running, no target count, "give me whatever
            you last measured" rather than "capture exactly M periods,
            then stop"), convert period -> frequency (a real division,
            not optional -- PID error must be linear in frequency, since
            that's what's linear in the V-to-F encoding; period is not
            linear in the encoded value), run that channel's PID with a
            FIXED, known Delta-t (= the Master period), clamp, write the
            new PER into that channel's shadow register. CMP is very
            likely fixed/constant per channel (single-ended V/F tone,
            not a variable-duty PWM leg) -- simplifies away most of the
            PER/CMP-coherency hazard the reference document flags for
            the general case.

          - Master's own rate is a free, deliberately-chosen parameter
            now representing CONTROL BANDWIDTH, not carrier rate --
            expected to land far below the ~100 kHz carrier ceiling
            (tens of Hz to low kHz, matching real magnet-supply loop
            dynamics), which is what makes it safe to run 4x PID compute
            inside one interrupt without threatening PFM_Input's DMA
            margins (same headroom analysis as the priority-inversion
            fix below, ~8 periods/~80-160us before circular-buffer
            wraparound) -- should be validated empirically with
            PFM_GetDiagGapLog() (pfm.c, PFM:GAPLOG?) once implemented,
            not just assumed safe from the arithmetic alone.

        Explicitly NOT yet resolved (real open items, not implementation
        detail):

          - Exact HRTIM per-timer IRQn lines are NOT needed for this
            design (Master-driven, not per-channel-IRQ-driven, per the
            correction above) -- this removes what was originally
            flagged as an open risk.
          - HRTIM_NUM_CHANNELS is currently 3 (ctrlr_config.h); this mode
            needs 4 independent channels with NO phase/interleave
            relationship between them, which is a structurally different
            use of HRTIM_NUM_CHANNELS / PFM_PhaseForChannel() than
            today's phase-locked-bridge meaning -- needs its own
            abstraction, not a bigger N on the existing one.
          - Setpoint/gain wire interface (new serial commands) not yet
            designed.
          - Real magnet-supply operating frequency range, for sizing the
            worst-case Master-heartbeat-rate/ISR-load budget with actual
            numbers instead of the "~100 kHz ceiling" spec value (which
            is the Transrex document's encoding range, not necessarily
            this system's typical operating point).

  Files:  none yet -- design only, this entry documents the decision
        record before implementation starts.

2026-09-09  —  Memory footprint tracking: python/memory_report.py, tracked
               per commit since project start
-----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Change:  Requested: visualize flash/RAM usage, what it's for, and what
        remains, tracked over the project's lifetime and kept current in
        future commits. New python/memory_report.py, two pieces:

          1. `history` -- reads a built .elf with arm-none-eabi-size (total
             flash/RAM) and arm-none-eabi-nm --print-size -l (per-symbol,
             with DWARF file:line from this project's -g3 builds) and
             appends one row to docs/memory_history.csv: totals plus a
             byte count per source-file "module" bucket (PFM table engine,
             PFM_Input capture, HRTIM driver, ST HAL library, etc.), split
             into flash and RAM. `--backfill` rebuilds the whole CSV in one
             pass from every past commit that has a Debug/WHAM-PFMG474-V4.elf
             checked in (this project tracks Debug/ build output in git,
             confirmed useful here beyond its original purpose) -- no
             rebuilding needed, `git show <rev>:Debug/WHAM-PFMG474-V4.elf`
             pulls each historical binary directly. Backfilled all 5
             commits with a tracked elf, back to the initial commit
             (2026-09-04).

          2. `report` -- renders docs/memory_report.html from that CSV: a
             self-contained dashboard (Google Fonts is the only external
             request; everything else inline, no build step) with a
             utilization-over-lifetime trend chart (Flash % / RAM % per
             commit, hand-rolled SVG with a crosshair tooltip), two stat
             tiles (used/remaining + sparkline), and two current-breakdown
             bar charts (Flash by module, RAM by module, hover tooltips).
             Both light and dark themes defined as CSS custom properties
             (verified via computed-style, not just eyeballed, since a
             local-file preview forces light rendering regardless of CSS).

        Design decision, the one non-obvious part: per-module attribution
        reads the LINKED elf's own retained symbol table, not a per-.o
        `size` sum. This project's Makefile builds with
        -ffunction-sections/-fdata-sections and links with --gc-sections;
        a raw .o's size includes every function the compiler emitted for
        that translation unit, even ones the linker later discards as
        unreachable -- checked directly: summing every HAL driver .o in
        this build totals ~94 KB of .text for HAL alone, while the FINAL
        linked .text is only ~48.5 KB for the ENTIRE firmware. Reading
        attribution off the linked elf's nm output avoids this: every
        symbol counted is genuinely in the binary. One real wrinkle
        handled explicitly: ~84 unimplemented IRQ vector names are weak
        aliases of one shared Default_Handler (same address) -- deduped by
        (address, size, type) before summing, or that alone would have
        been counted ~84 times over. The nm-vs-`size` gap that remains
        (alignment padding, the raw .isr_vector table, a few anonymous/
        mergeable symbols -- ~1.7 KB flash, ~1.6 KB RAM at the current
        commit) is rolled into an explicit "Padding / unattributed" bucket
        rather than silently dropped, so a breakdown chart's total always
        equals `size`'s own authoritative number.

        Current snapshot (a764a01, this commit): flash 51,312 / 524,288 B
        (9.8%), RAM 55,368 / 131,072 B (42.2%) -- RAM dominated by
        g_pfmTable (PFM table engine, ~39.9 KB, PFM_TABLE_SIZE=5000 *
        (2+2*HRTIM_NUM_CHANNELS) bytes/entry) and PFM_Input's per-channel
        capture buffers (~6.4 KB); flash dominated by the ST HAL library
        (~27.4 KB, mostly stm32g4xx_hal_tim.c/_uart.c/_hrtim.c).

        Standing instruction added (AGENTS.md, and this session's own
        memory) to regenerate both files after any commit that changes
        flash/RAM usage -- same "proactive, not just when asked"
        convention as this changelog itself.

  Files:  python/memory_report.py (new), python/memory_report_template.html
        (new), docs/memory_history.csv (new), docs/memory_report.html (new,
        generated -- do not hand-edit), AGENTS.md

2026-09-09  —  Real PFM output falls ~77 periods behind on a long table
               hold: root-caused to a stale NVIC priority inversion,
               FIXED and CONFIRMED on real hardware
-----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Change:  Found while stress-testing the DMA-based PFM_Input capture
        below with progressively less trivial waveforms (an extreme
        zigzag, then a slower ramp/hold(100 periods)/ramp profile). The
        ramp/hold/ramp shot's real HRTIM output was confirmed, via a
        DSLogic ground-truth capture (all 6 channels agreeing, U vs UN
        median error 0.0), to genuinely stall on the wire: it holds one
        frequency for ~187 real periods instead of the table's
        programmed 100, then cleanly resumes the correct sequence ~77
        periods late. PFM:DIAG?'s existing single max-gap diagnostic
        (added the same day for a different, now-closed dead end) could
        not distinguish "one big stall" from "many small repeated
        delays," so a new diagnostic was added: PFM_GetDiagGapLog()
        (pfm.c/pfm.h) logs the raw DWT-cycle gap between every single
        PFM_CycleBoundaryHandler() call for the current shot (up to
        PFM_INPUT_MAX_PERIODS of them), exposed over serial as
        PFM:GAPLOG? (commands.c/commands.h/cmd_parser.c) -- TEMPORARY,
        same debug-command convention as PFMIN:DMASTAT?, meant to be
        removed once this investigation closed.

        The raw gap log immediately showed the real mechanism: not one
        stall, but a spike of ~15000-18500 cycles (~88-109 us) recurring
        roughly every 8-10 table entries, each followed by an
        anomalously short next gap (the classic signature of a single
        delayed ISR entry, not an accumulating one). The recurrence
        interval (~8 periods) matches PFM_Input's DMA capture design
        exactly: a 32-entry circular buffer with a half/full-transfer
        interrupt every 16 captured edges, i.e. every 8 periods at 2
        edges/period.

        Root cause: PFM_Input's DMA1_ChannelN_IRQn (the interrupt whose
        callback batch-processes those 16 edges, pfm_input.c) was left
        at NVIC priority 1 -- a decision inherited from the OLD, now-
        superseded per-edge interrupt-driven capture design, where
        TIMx capture interrupts genuinely needed to preempt
        HRTIM1_Master_IRQn (demoted to priority 2 on 2026-09-08) to
        avoid a real overcapture race. That reasoning stopped applying
        the moment capture became DMA-driven (2026-09-09, entry below):
        the DMA controller grabs each CCRx value in hardware with zero
        CPU/IRQ involvement, so nothing about capture fidelity depends
        on IRQ priority anymore -- only the periodic 16-edge batch-
        processing ISR still runs on the CPU, and leaving IT above
        HRTIM1_Master let its own real (non-trivial) processing time
        repeatedly steal cycles from the table-advance ISR, which is
        what a long run of identical hold entries made visible as a
        large, compounding real-time lag.

        Fix: HRTIM1_Master_IRQn restored to priority 1 (hrtim.c,
        HRTIM1_EnableMasterInterrupt()); PFM_Input's DMA1_ChannelN_IRQn
        demoted to priority 3 (pfm_input.c, PfmInput_Init()), below
        both HRTIM1_Master and the GateDriverStatus EXTI/TIMx_IRQn tier
        (priority 2). Safe because the ~8-period window before the
        circular buffer would actually wrap and lose data is orders of
        magnitude larger than HRTIM1_Master's own ISR execution time.

        CONFIRMED on real hardware, same ramp/hold/ramp shot, before/
        after:
          PFM:DIAG? max gap    18547 cycles (109 us)  ->  5152 cycles
                                                            (30 us -- this
                                                            IS the table's
                                                            own longest
                                                            programmed
                                                            period, not a
                                                            stall)
          PFM:GAPLOG? (full)   repeating ~15-18k-cycle spikes every
                                8-10 entries  ->  every single one of
                                200 entries within normal jitter of its
                                own programmed period, zero anomalies
          PFMIN:DATA? (all 3   198-199/200 captured, OVERCAP=0 -- capture
          active channels)     fidelity unaffected by the IRQ demotion

        Also explains the "max gap grows roughly linearly with channel
        count" note in the DMA capture entry below (1 ch ~17-30 us ...
        6 ch ~100 us) -- that WAS this same priority inversion the whole
        time, just below the threshold to visibly stall output until a
        long-enough run of identical table entries made the accumulated
        effect obvious.

  Files:  Core/Src/hrtim.c, Core/Src/pfm_input.c, Core/Src/pfm.c,
        Core/Inc/pfm.h, Core/Src/commands.c, Core/Inc/commands.h,
