        profiles used to find this)

2026-09-09  —  DMA-based PFM_Input capture: implemented, two real bugs
               found and fixed, CONFIRMED on real hardware at all 6
               channels simultaneously (the exact config that hard-
               locked the board under the old design)
-----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Change:  Implemented the DMA-based capture redesign planned in the
        entry below. Each active channel's TIMx now DMAs its captured
        CCRx value directly into a small RAM ring buffer (g_dmaBuf[],
        32 entries/channel) with NO per-edge ISR at all -- the CPU only
        wakes on a DMA half/full-transfer interrupt
        (HAL_TIM_IC_CaptureHalfCpltCallback()/HAL_TIM_IC_CaptureCallback(),
        pfm_input.c), once per 16 edges instead of once per edge.
        Everything downstream (period computation, the 16-bit
        wraparound mask, target-count stopping) moved into
        ProcessDmaChunk(), called once per batch. Wire protocol
        (PFMIN:CAPTURE/STATus?/DATA?) unchanged -- purely an internal
        capture-mechanism swap. One DMA channel per PFM_Input channel
        (DMA1_Channel1..6, DMAMUX request IDs from stm32g4xx_hal_dma.h
        -- DMA_REQUEST_TIM2_CH1/CH2, _TIM3_CH1, _TIM4_CH1,
        _TIM5_CH1/CH2), all newly used -- nothing else in this project
        used any DMA channel before this (confirmed via grep before
        choosing them).

        Two real bugs found and fixed during real-hardware bring-up,
        neither existing before this redesign:
          1. DMAMUX1's own peripheral clock was never enabled (only
             DMA1's was) -- HAL_TIM_IC_Start_DMA() reported HAL_OK
             (confirmed via a temporary PFMIN:DMASTAT? debug command)
             but no request ever actually reached the DMA controller,
             so capture silently reported 0 periods with no error
             anywhere. Fixed: __HAL_RCC_DMAMUX1_CLK_ENABLE() added
             alongside the existing DMA1 clock enable.
          2. A shot ending mid-chunk (before the next half/full DMA
             interrupt) silently lost up to 15 real edges already
             sitting in the buffer but never processed -- confirmed on
             real hardware, a 200-period capture reaching only 191.
             Fixed: PfmInput_OnShotEnd() now reads the DMA channel's
             CNDTR register to compute exactly how much unprocessed
             data is sitting in the buffer and flushes it via
             ProcessDmaChunk() before actually stopping DMA.

        CONFIRMED on real hardware, incremental channel-count test
        (same philosophy as the interrupt-driven design's own
        incremental re-test, but this time scaling all the way up):
        1, 2, 3, 4, 5, then all 6 channels, each with a full 100->60
        kHz decreasing sweep, checked for lockup/fault/PFM:DIAG? max
        gap at every step:

          channels   max gap (PFM:DIAG?)   result
          1          ~17-30 us              clean, 197-200/200
          2          ~42 us                 clean, 200/200 both
          3          ~57 us                 clean, 200/200 all 3
          4          ~74 us                 clean, 200/200 all 4
          5          ~91 us                 clean, 200/200 all 5
          6 (all)    ~100 us                clean, 200/200 all 6

        Max gap grows gently and roughly linearly with channel count,
        nowhere near the ~734 us starvation threshold the old
        interrupt-driven design hit at just 2 channels -- and the final
        6-channel run is the EXACT configuration that caused a real
        board lockup requiring a power cycle under that old design.
        No lockup, no fault, board fully responsive throughout and
        after, at every step. This appears to be a complete, real fix
        for the interrupt-starvation root cause, not another
        mitigation.

        PFM_INPUT_ACTIVE_CHANNEL_MASK left at 0x3F (all 6) as of this
        entry, reflecting the confirmed-working end state.

        Added python/pfm_input_plot.py usage throughout this test
        (--channel 1,2,3,4,5,6 / all) to visually confirm each step,
        not just trust the numeric OK/count replies.

  Files:   Core/Src/pfm_input.c, Core/Inc/pfm_input.h,
        Core/Src/commands.c, Core/Inc/commands.h, Core/Src/cmd_parser.c,
        Core/Src/stm32g4xx_it.c

2026-09-09  —  PFM:DIAG? diagnostic CONFIRMS interrupt starvation as
               the table-advance stall's root cause (734us max gap,
               ~67x a normal period, with just 2 capture channels
               active) -- DMA-based capture redesign now planned
-----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Change:  Added PFM:DIAG? (commands.c's cmd_pfm_diag(), pfm.c's
        PFM_GetDiagCounters()) to test the interrupt-starvation theory
        for the table-advance stall found in the entries just below --
        NOT a plain call counter (a completed shot's call count is
        always exactly the table's entry count regardless of whether
        stalling happened along the way, since PFM_CycleBoundaryHandler()
        only stops once g_pfmIndex reaches it -- that number alone
        can't distinguish "ran on schedule" from "was starved, then
        caught up"). Instead tracks the largest REAL TIME gap between
        two consecutive calls, using the Cortex-M4 DWT free-running
        cycle counter (170 MHz, confirmed same clock as
        HRTIM_TIMER_CLK_HZ) -- reset at every PFM_Restart(), read back
        as "OK <callCount> <maxGapCycles> <maxGapUs>".

        Result, same 100->60kHz decreasing sweep, only PFM_Input_01+02
        active (2 real channels, both genuinely wired): PFM:DIAG? ->
        "OK 200 124818 734" -- 200 calls (as expected either way), but
        a MAXIMUM GAP of 734 us between two consecutive calls. Normal
        gap at ~91-95 kHz should be ~11 us (one real period) -- 734 us
        is ~67x that. This is direct, quantitative proof that
        HRTIM1_Master_IRQHandler() (which runs PFM_CycleBoundaryHandler(),
        priority 2 -- deliberately BELOW every TIM2-5 capture channel's
        priority 1, so real edges are never missed) was starved for
        roughly 67 real periods straight, with just 2 real capture
        channels active -- not a logic bug in the table-advance code,
        and not something that requires many/floating channels to
        trigger.

        DECISION: since capture-channel interrupt load is confirmed as
        the actual mechanism, the planned fix is a DMA-based capture
        redesign -- configure each TIMx to auto-transfer its captured
        CCRx value into a RAM ring buffer via DMA on every edge, with
        ZERO CPU/ISR involvement per edge (versus one full ISR entry/
        exit + HAL dispatch + capture logic per edge today). The CPU
        would only wake up occasionally (a DMA half/full-buffer
        interrupt) to process a BATCH of already-timestamped edges,
        removing capture-channel interrupt PRIORITY/LOAD from
        competing with HRTIM1_Master almost entirely. Not yet
        implemented -- this entry documents the confirmed root cause
        and the planned direction, not a landed fix.

  Files:   Core/Src/pfm.c, Core/Inc/pfm.h, Core/Src/commands.c,
        Core/Inc/commands.h, Core/Src/cmd_parser.c

2026-09-09  —  Added python/pfm_input_plot.py; all-6-channel test finds
               a NEW real issue: floating capture channels can starve
               the PFM table-advance ISR itself (output side, not just
               capture)
-----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Change:  Added python/pfm_input_plot.py -- pulls PFMIN:DATA? for one
        or more channels (--channel 1 / 1,3,5 / all) and plots period
        (raw ticks) + derived frequency (kHz) on one figure per
        request, multiple channels overlaid with a shared, OUTLIER-
        ROBUST y-axis (90th-percentile-based, not plain autoscale) so
        one noisy channel's spikes don't flatten a real channel's data
        out of view -- see the script's own header comment. Also
        --csv and --save (headless) options.

        Used it for a real-hardware "full test" requested directly:
        temporarily re-enabled ALL 6 PFM_Input channels
        (PFM_INPUT_ACTIVE_CHANNEL_MASK = 0x3F, pfm_input.h) and
        repeated the 100->60 kHz decreasing sweep. Only PFM_Input_01
        (phase U) is physically wired to anything on this bench; the
        other 5 are floating. Result revealed something NEW and real,
        not just "floating pins read garbage" (expected) but a
        genuine OUTPUT-side side effect:

        Channel 1's own captured sequence got stuck: 1699x12, 1736x13,
        1774x11, then 1814 repeated for 160+ consecutive periods
        straight through to the end of its 200-period capture (should
        have been ~10 periods/step, cleanly advancing through all 20
        steps to 2832). This is NOT a pfm_input.c capture bug -- it
        faithfully reported a REAL signal that itself got stuck.
        Mechanism: HRTIM1_Master_IRQHandler() (calls
        PFM_CycleBoundaryHandler(), which advances the table and
        writes the next per/cmp registers) runs at NVIC priority 2,
        deliberately BELOW all 4 TIM2-5 capture-channel IRQs' priority
        1 (that's what makes capture always preempt it and never miss
        a real edge -- see hrtim.c's HRTIM1_EnableMasterInterrupt()).
        With 5 channels now floating and picking up electrical noise
        as spurious rising edges at a high, UNCONTROLLED rate
        (PFM_Input_06 alone logged OVERCAP=66 out of 200 -- edges
        arriving faster than even ITS OWN ISR could keep up), those
        higher-priority capture ISRs can consume enough CPU time to
        genuinely starve the lower-priority table-advance ISR --
        stalling the REAL PWM output's progression through the table,
        not just corrupting capture data for the floating channels.

        No fault, no hang, no crash -- board stayed fully responsive
        (FAULT? OK 0, *IDN? normal) throughout; this is a live timing
        degradation, not a stability failure.

        Reverted PFM_INPUT_ACTIVE_CHANNEL_MASK back to 0x01 (channel 1
        only) -- this real risk (an unconnected/floating capture
        channel can degrade PFM OUTPUT timing, not just its own
        capture data) is worth keeping in mind for any future work
        that wires up and enables channels 2-6: a genuinely floating
        pin should stay disabled in this mask until it's actually
        connected to something, not just left enabled "in case."

  Files:   python/pfm_input_plot.py, Core/Inc/pfm_input.h

2026-09-09  —  HRTIM SET/RESET collision fix EXTENDED to channel 0 (U)
               after real-hardware re-test showed it wasn't actually
               immune -- cold-start settling logic updated to match
-----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Change:  The V/W-only version of the CMP3 SET fix (entry just below)
        was reflashed and re-tested with a full 6-channel (U/UN/V/VN/
        W/WN) DSLogic capture of the same 60-100 kHz ramp. Result:
        V/VN/W/WN -- 0 anomalies, fix fully confirmed. But U/UN newly
        showed 6 anomalies each, same exact "measured = old + new"
        merged-period signature V/W had -- and at almost the identical
        per-value transitions (steps 12-16, ~1993-1814 ticks, plus the
        final 100 kHz step) V/W used to fail at before their fix. This
        means channel 0 was never actually immune to whatever the
        underlying HRTIM hazard is -- it was just rarer/narrower than
        V/W's own additional SET/RESET-crossbar vulnerability, and
        probably got statistically masked by V/W's much-more-frequent
        failures in every earlier test.

        Extended fix: apply the same CMP3-based SET (see the entry
        below for the full ST-community-sourced mechanism) to channel
        0 too, not just channels 1..N-1. Its ResetTrigger stays
        MASTER_PER (counter still resets exactly on the Master
        boundary); only the output's SET SOURCE moves off TIMPER onto
        CMP3, same 16-tick fixed offset as V/W.

        Caught before testing, not after: this broke an existing
        assumption in HRTIM1_WaitForPhaseAndConnect()'s cold-start
        settling logic (HRTIM1_PWM_Start()), which explicitly skipped
        the "reforceActive" output re-assert for channel 0 specifically
        BECAUSE its SET source used to BE its own reset event (TIMPER
        = MASTER_PER, the same instant). With SET now on a separate
        CMP3 event, channel 0 needs the same reforceActive=1 treatment
        channels 1..N-1 already get, or the very first pulse of every
        shot risks the same garbled-cold-start failure mode this
        function was built to prevent back on 2026-09-04. Fixed by
        passing reforceActive=1 for channel 0's call too, with both
        call sites' comments updated to match.

        CONFIRMED FIXED, real hardware, same day: reflashed, repeated
        the 60-100 kHz ramp with a full 6-channel DSLogic capture
        (/Users/everettpenne/DSLogic U3Pro32-la-260909-101706.dsl).
        Result: U/UN/V/VN/W/WN -- ALL SIX channels, 0 anomalies. Cold
        start (each channel's very first pulse) also checked directly
        and reads a normal-length period, not garbled -- the
        reforceActive=1 fix for channel 0 holds. This appears to be the
        actual root-cause fix for the frequency-ramp glitch investigated
        across this whole 2026-09-09 session, not another mitigation.

        Generalization check (requested directly, not assumed):
        confirmed every place this fix lives -- the CMP3 compare-
        register config loop, the per-channel output SetSource
        selector, and HRTIM1_PWM_Start()'s cold-start reforceActive
        handling -- is keyed off HRTIM_NUM_CHANNELS (ctrlr_config.h),
        not a hardcoded channel count, so raising N in the future
        automatically brings any newly-active channel under this fix
        with no further code changes. Also confirmed no register
        conflict: each channel's own local CMP3 (used here) is a
        separate hardware resource from the Master's MCMP3R/MCMP4R
        (already used for channels 3/4's phase offsets) -- same name,
        different registers. Documented directly on
        HRTIM_NUM_CHANNELS's own comment in ctrlr_config.h so this is
        discoverable, not just implicit.

  Files:   Core/Src/hrtim.c, Core/Inc/ctrlr_config.h

2026-09-09  —  HRTIM SET/RESET collision fix for phase-shifted channels
               (V/W) -- root cause found, real fix applied, pending
               hardware re-verification
-----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Change:  Root-caused the frequency-ramp glitch from the entries just
        below: a real-hardware sweep with PFM_Input_01 restricted to
        phase U (clean, 0 anomalies) alongside a full 6-channel DSLogic
        capture (U/UN/V/VN/W/WN) found V and W BOTH glitching --
        starting as low as ~68-69 kHz, well inside the range U alone
        had suggested was safe -- while U/UN stayed perfectly clean.
        Every glitch's measured duration matched (old period + new
        period) almost exactly (ratio 0.988-1.005 across all 16
        glitches checked), on BOTH V and W -- i.e. one complete real
        SET/rising-edge event is being silently dropped, not a garbled
        or partial one.

        Found the exact documented mechanism via ST's own community
        engineering forum (search "HRTIM PWM transients greater than a
        period"): when a timer's output SET and RESET events fall
        within ~3 HRTIM clock cycles of each other, RESET always wins
        and SET is silently dropped, leaving the output low for one
        extra full cycle. U's output SET is tied to TIMPER = MASTER_PER
        (its ResetTrigger), always far from its own mid-cycle CMP1
        (duty, its ResetSource) -- no collision possible. V/W's SET is
        ALSO tied to TIMPER, but for them TIMPER = MASTER_CMP1/CMP2
        (their own ResetTrigger) -- a point that itself shifts every
        table entry as per changes (phase = k*(per+1)/N), and during a
        fast ramp can land within a few clocks of the PREVIOUS cycle's
        own CMP1 (duty) event, exactly the documented collision.

        FIX (the same one documented in that thread): V/W's output SET
        moved off the un-prioritized TIMPER event onto a dedicated
        CMP3 compare (HRTIM's collision rule favors the higher-numbered
        compare register, so SET now wins ties against RESET's CMP1
        instead of losing). CMP3xR set once at init to a small fixed
        16-tick offset (not duty-dependent, no per-cycle updates
        needed) -- negligible against real period lengths (1200+
        ticks). Deliberately narrow: only channels 1..HRTIM_NUM_
        CHANNELS-1 change; channel 0 and the unused channels beyond
        HRTIM_NUM_CHANNELS (free-running, no MASTER_CMPk race possible)
        keep SetSource = TIMPER.

        Rebuilt clean (zero warnings, text 47140 -> 47252 bytes).
        NOT YET RE-VERIFIED ON REAL HARDWARE as of this entry -- next
        step is reflashing and repeating the 6-channel DSLogic capture
        to confirm V/W actually stop glitching. Be prepared to revert
        this specific change (the SetSource/CMP3 edit in
        HRTIM1_FullInit(), hrtim.c) if it doesn't hold up or causes any
        new real-hardware issue -- everything else in this session
        stays regardless of this fix's outcome.

  Files:   Core/Src/hrtim.c

2026-09-09  —  100 kHz hard carrier-frequency limit; PFM_Input reduced
               to the one wired channel; HRTIM shadow/preload
               architecture reviewed (correct, root cause still open)
-----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Change:  Direct response to the frequency-ramp findings just above/
        below this entry (real, intermittent missing-pulse glitches on
        the wire above ~85 kHz on a frequency-CHANGING table):

        1. ctrlr_config.h: new PFM_MAX_CARRIER_FREQ_HZ (100000) --
           hard compile-time ceiling. Enforced in commands.c's
           cmd_table_step() (new TABLE_STEP_MIN_PER bound, same
           per_from_freq() formula as python/pfm_table_upload.py) --
           any TABLE:STEP whose `per` implies a faster carrier is
           rejected with new ERR 10, not silently accepted. 100 kHz is
           the documented, tested-clean boundary, not a guess -- every
           constant-100kHz test this project has run has been clean,
           and the ramp's own first ~5 steps (all below 85 kHz) never
           glitched either. python/pfm_table_upload.py's PROFILE 6
           FREQ_RAMP_END_HZ lowered from 140_000 to 100_000 to match
           (would otherwise immediately hit ERR 10 on re-upload).

        2. pfm_input.h: new PFM_INPUT_ACTIVE_CHANNEL_MASK (0x01 --
           PFM_Input_01 only), the one channel actually wired to a
           real signal (phase U) on this bench. pfm_input.c reworked
           so a timer with no active channel is left COMPLETELY
           untouched by PfmInput_Init() -- no HAL_TIM_IC_Init(), no
           clock enable, no NVIC IRQ enable, no GPIO AF config -- not
           merely armed with M=0. TIM3/TIM4/TIM5 and TIM2_CH2 are now
           fully idle. Motivation: cut CPU/interrupt load while the
           frequency-transition timing investigation is open (load is
           exactly what's under suspicion), and shrink the variables
           in play. Re-enable a channel by setting its bit in the
           mask; nothing else needs to change.

        3. Investigated hrtim.c's HRTIM shadow/preload register
           architecture (HRTIM1_FullInit(), HRTIM1_ApplyPfmStep(),
           HRTIM1_SoftwareUpdate(), PFM_ApplyCurrentStep()) end to end
           looking for a misconfiguration that could explain the
           glitch. Found it CORRECTLY implements ST's own reference
           multiphase design (STM32CubeF3 HRTIM_Multiphase): PreloadEnable
           = ENABLED on both the Master and every active timer;
           UpdateTrigger = MASTER for every phase-locked channel (ties
           every channel's shadow->active promotion to one single,
           coherent Master update event, not per-timer independent
           events); RepetitionCounter = 0 (updates apply every single
           period, not gated to every Nth); PFM_ApplyCurrentStep()
           writes next-period values into shadow registers during the
           CURRENT period (a full period's worth of margin, not a
           last-instant race), relying on the natural hardware update
           event to promote them -- NOT an explicit software-forced
           update mid-period (that WOULD be capable of glitching; this
           design correctly avoids it, using HRTIM1_SoftwareUpdate()
           only once, for the very first period of a shot in
           PFM_Restart(), before the counters are even running).
           CONCLUSION: this is not a "forgot to use preload" bug --
           the architecture is sound on paper. Root cause remains
           OPEN. Refined clue, not yet confirmed: the glitched value
           each time measures almost exactly 2x the NEW (post-
           transition) period, not old+new -- i.e. the counter really
           is running at the new, faster rate by the time of the
           glitch, but ONE real toggle/edge at the exact transition
           instant is being suppressed for one cycle. This may be
           specific to channel 0 (phase U / PFM_Input_01), which
           resets on MASTER_PER directly (the same event that also
           promotes the Master's own shadow PER) rather than a
           phase-shifted MASTER_CMPk trigger the other channels use --
           unconfirmed. Resolving this further needs either STM32G4
           reference-manual-level detail on Master PER shadow-register
           promotion relative to its own counter-reset event, or live
           timing instrumentation -- deliberately NOT attempted this
           session given this project's history of real board hangs
           chasing this exact flavor of timing bug aggressively.

  Files:   Core/Inc/ctrlr_config.h, Core/Src/commands.c,
        Core/Inc/commands.h, Core/Inc/pfm_input.h, Core/Src/pfm_input.c,
        python/pfm_table_upload.py

2026-09-09  —  PFM_Input: frequency-ramp test finds two real bugs --
               one fixed (buffer overflow), one open (dropped edges
               at fast transitions)
-----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Change:  Added PROFILE 6 to python/pfm_table_upload.py: a 200-entry,
        20-step frequency ramp (60 kHz -> 140 kHz, constant 50% duty,
        10 periods/step -- sized to exactly match PFM_INPUT_MAX_PERIODS
        so a single PFMIN:CAPTURE 200 covers the whole shot), to verify
        pfm_input.c's rising-edge capture tracks a CHANGING period, not
        just the constant 100 kHz carrier every prior real-hardware
        test used.

        Real-hardware result: fired 3 times, cross-checked against two
        DSLogic captures (a narrow one covering the first 3 steps, then
        a widened ~5 ms window covering the full 200-period shot:
        /Users/everettpenne/DSLogic U3Pro32-la-260909-091458.dsl).
        DSLogic's ground truth is CLEAN across all 200 real periods --
        every one of the 20 programmed frequency steps lands correctly
        on the real PA15 wire, no glitches, no doubled pulses anywhere.

        But the firmware's own PFMIN:DATA? reply does NOT match this
        cleanly: 9 of its 200 entries come back as almost exactly 2x
        the correct value for that point in the ramp (e.g. one entry
        read 3986, exactly 2x the real 1993-tick period at that step).
        Cross-referencing index-for-index against the DSLogic ground
        truth (accounting for a fixed, well-understood 1-index offset
        between the two data sets -- firmware's period[0] is the
        interval AFTER its reference-only first rise, so it's one
        real edge ahead of DSLogic's own rise[0]) confirms: this is a
        REAL missed capture, not a real signal artifact -- the
        firmware's ISR is silently failing to register one specific
        rising edge, always right at a frequency-transition boundary,
        and increasingly often as the ramp's periods shorten (none in
        the first ~5 steps at 2000+ ticks/period, then at nearly every
        transition once periods drop below ~1700 ticks). NOT YET
        ROOT-CAUSED. Leading hypothesis, unconfirmed: an ISR
        service-time margin issue that only bites when the capture
        callback's own turnaround time becomes comparable to the
        (now much shorter) real period, possibly worsened by
        contention with HRTIM1_Master_IRQHandler/
        PFM_CycleBoundaryHandler firing at the same moment to load the
        new per/cmp values for the transition. This is a DIFFERENT
        bug from the 2026-09-08 duty-cycle one (which corrupted every
        single measurement, not just transitions, and is confirmed
        gone -- see below) -- it only affects period tracking of a
        FAST-CHANGING signal, not a constant one.

        Separately, and unrelated: PFMIN:DATA? 1 was observed to
        report 201 entries against an armed target of 200, with the
        201st value being pure garbage (64250 = 0xFAFA). Root cause:
        HAL_TIM_IC_Stop_IT() does not take effect instantaneously: a
        capture already in flight when capturedCount reaches
        targetCount can still race it and reach HAL_TIM_IC_
        CaptureCallback() one more time, writing period[targetCount]
        -- one past this channel's array -- which silently overflows
        into the START of the NEXT channel's PfmInputState_t in
        g_state[] (g_state[] is a plain contiguous array). FIXED:
        the callback now bails out immediately if
        capturedCount >= targetCount, before touching anything else,
        making "already finished" a true no-op regardless of how the
        stop race lands. Rebuilt clean (zero warnings); NOT yet
        re-verified on hardware.

  Files:   Core/Src/pfm_input.c, python/pfm_table_upload.py

2026-09-08  —  PFM_Input: duty-cycle measurement dropped, rising-edge
               period-only capture (bug sidestepped, not root-caused)
-----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Change:  Two independent duty-cycle capture designs (BOTHEDGE +
        live-GPIO disambiguation, then an alternating-single-polarity
        redesign with a disassembly-confirmed CCxE-toggle fix) both
        gave a real signal wired to PFM_Input_01 consistently ONE FULL
        REAL PERIOD LATE readings (reported high == real period + real
        high; reported period == 2x real period, 20/20 captures both
        times) -- see the two entries below for the full diagnostic
        history. A follow-up PWM-Input-Mode isolation test caused two
        real board hangs (recovered via physical power-cycle both
        times) and was abandoned before producing data; that
        diagnostic code was written, tested, and then fully removed
        again in the same session.

        Decision: duty-cycle (time-high) measurement is dropped
        entirely rather than continuing to chase an unexplained bug.
        pfm_input.c now measures period only, via plain
        TIM_ICPOLARITY_RISING armed once at PfmInput_Init() and never
        touched again -- ArmPolarity()/the WAIT_RISE/WAIT_FALL phase
        state machine are gone. The one thing every failed duty-cycle
        attempt had in common was an in-ISR polarity change; rising-
        edge-only capture has none, which should sidestep the bug
        entirely rather than fix it -- NOT yet re-verified on real
        hardware (next step: repeat the phase-U-into-PFM_Input_01
        test from the entry below and confirm period reads correctly
        this time).

        Wire-format change: PFMIN:DATA? <ch> now replies
        "OK <count> OVERCAP=<n> <per1> <per2> ..." (dropped the
        per-period "<hi>" field). PfmInput_GetHighTicks() removed from
        pfm_input.h's API.

        Core/Src/commands.c, Core/Inc/commands.h, AGENTS.md

  CONFIRMED FIXED, real hardware, same day: reflashed, uploaded the
        identical 100-entry/100 kHz/40%-duty table used in the earlier
        broken test (per=1699, cmp=680, all 3 channels), phase U wired
        to PFM_Input_01 exactly as before, PFMIN:CAPTURE 20, FIRE.
        PFMIN:DATA? 1 -> "OK 20 OVERCAP=0" followed by 1699 twenty
        times, no exceptions -- the exact real period, not 3398.
        Independently confirmed against a fresh DSLogic capture of the
        same shot (/Users/everettpenne/DSLogic
        U3Pro32-la-260909-090119.dsl): 50 real rising edges captured,
        and (excluding one cold-start-settling first period, already
        documented elsewhere in this project) the other 48 average
        1697.1 ticks at 170 MHz (range 1695.9-1698.0, sigma ~0.55) --
        matching the firmware's 1699 within ordinary 250 MHz-sample-
        into-170-MHz-tick quantization/clock-tolerance noise, nothing
        like the old bug's clean, exact 2x signature. Root cause of
        the original bug is still not explained -- it was sidestepped,

2026-09-08  —  PFM_Input real-hardware test: data corruption found,
               root cause NOT yet found -- diagnostic writeup
-----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Change:  First real-signal test of pfm_input.c (see the entry below
        for the feature itself): phase U's HRTIM output (per=1699,
        cmp=680 -- 100 kHz, 40% duty, this exact per/cmp pair already
        hardware-verified via DSLogic in earlier sessions) wired
        directly to PFM_Input_01 (PA15), a 100-entry constant table
        fired, PFMIN:CAPTURE 20 armed beforehand.

        Result: the capture mechanism itself works exactly as designed
        -- channel 1 reliably reached its armed count of 20 promptly
        after FIRE, confirming the shot-synchronized arm/start/stop
        plumbing end to end on real hardware, not just in isolation.
        But the MEASURED VALUES are wrong: high-time (duty) values
        consistently read correctly (~664 ticks, matching the
        expected ~40% duty minus the same ~90-100 ns dead-time
        shortfall already characterized in this project's earlier
        DSLogic work), but period values consistently read as
        roughly 2-3x the expected single-period tick count (~1700) --
        i.e. real periods are being merged/undercounted.

        Diagnosed with real instrumentation added to pfm_input.c
        specifically for this (PfmInput_GetOvercaptureCount(),
        PfmInput_DebugGetUnexpectedRise()/Fall(), all reported via a
        temporary OVERCAP=/URISE=/UFALL= prefix on PFMIN:DATA?'s
        reply -- not yet in docs/command_reference.md, this is
        diagnostic-only for now), not guessed:
          - Hardware overcapture (CCxOF) stayed near zero (0-1 across
            20 captured periods) -- rules out simple CPU/ISR
            starvation silently overwriting CCR values before the ISR
            could read them.
          - The other 5 armed-but-unconnected channels showed ZERO
            capture activity (0 overcaptures, 0 captured periods each)
            -- rules out floating-pin noise on the other channels
            (e.g. PD4, sharing TIM2 with the pin under test)
            interfering with channel 1's own servicing.
          - UFALL (WAIT_CLOSING_RISE seeing an unexpected extra
            falling-type capture) was consistently high (34-36 across
            20 periods) -- the state machine IS reliably being handed
            genuine extra capture events while waiting for the single
            expected closing rise.
          - Raising ICFilter (the timer's built-in digital
            deglitch/noise filter, left at 0 in the first version) to
            0x3 then 0xF did NOT converge toward correct -- 0x3 showed
            no measurable change from 0, and 0xF made the corruption
            categorically worse (high-time readings jumped to ~1700,
            nearly a full period, instead of the correct ~664). This
            is inconsistent with simple debounce-able signal ringing
            (which more filtering should improve, not worsen) and is
            more consistent with the filter's own sampling delay
            desynchronizing HAL_TIM_ReadCapturedValue() (which goes
            through the filter) from the separate, always-instantaneous
            HAL_GPIO_ReadPin() this module uses to disambiguate BOTHEDGE
            captures (which does not) -- a real mechanism, but one that
            doesn't explain the ORIGINAL filter=0 corruption, which
            has no filter delay to desync against in the first place.
          - ICFilter reverted to 0 (neither 0x3 nor 0xF showed a real
            improvement, and higher values add the GPIO-read/filter-
            delay mismatch risk above for no confirmed benefit).

        Root cause NOT found. What remains genuinely unknown, flagged
        honestly rather than guessed: whether this is a real electrical
        signal-integrity problem on the physical U-to-PFM_Input_01
        connection (this session has no scope/logic analyzer access to
        see the actual waveform) or a logic bug in pfm_input.c's
        BOTHEDGE-plus-GPIO-level-read disambiguation approach itself
        (e.g. a genuine race between an edge landing in CCR and this
        module's separate GPIO read of the "current" pin state, if the
        real signal is doing something other than this module's
        two-edges-per-period assumption). Next step needs the bench:
        a scope or DSLogic capture of the actual PA15/U signal during
        a shot, to see the real waveform directly rather than infer it
        indirectly through capture behavior.

        Diagnostic instrumentation (PfmInput_GetOvercaptureCount() in
        pfm_input.h; PfmInput_DebugGetUnexpectedRise()/Fall(), not in
        the header, reached via local externs in commands.c) left in
        place, not reverted -- the investigation isn't finished. Board
        left flashed with ICFilter=0 (the original, unmodified value)
        plus this diagnostic instrumentation.

2026-09-08  —  PFM_Input period/duty capture (pfm_input.c/.h),
               synchronized to PFM shot lifetime
-----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Change:  New module measuring period + duty (time-high) per period,
        for a runtime-chosen count M of periods, independently on each
        of the 6 PFM_Input_01..06 pins (docs/pin_mapping_v4.csv --
        PA15/TIM2_CH1, PD4/TIM2_CH2, PB2/TIM5_CH1, PC12/TIM5_CH2,
        PB4/TIM3_CH1, PD12/TIM4_CH1). Requested as a plan first
        (plan mode used directly); the plan's first draft had
        PFMIN:CAPTURE both arm AND immediately start-and-block on its
        own, with no tie to FIRE at all -- corrected per direct
        follow-up request before implementation: capture must begin
        exactly when a PFM output shot begins, not at some arbitrary
        later moment.

        Design, as corrected: PFMIN:CAPTURE <M> now only ARMS a target
        period count per channel (PfmInput_Arm(), pfm_input.c) -- pure
        bookkeeping, touches no TIMx hardware, returns OK immediately.
        The actual HAL_TIM_IC_Start_IT() calls happen inside
        PFM_Restart() (pfm.c), the same function that calls
        HRTIM1_PWM_Start() for the shot itself, via a new
        PfmInput_OnShotStart() call added right alongside it -- so
        input capture and PWM output begin at the literal same call,
        not a separately-timed command. A capture also can never run
        longer than the shot that started it: PfmInput_OnShotEnd() is
        called from every place pfm.c stops a shot (PFM_ForceStop(),
        shared with both fault sources, AND the normal
        end-of-table-exhaustion branch in PFM_CycleBoundaryHandler(),
        which stops inline rather than via PFM_ForceStop() and so
        needed its own explicit call) -- stops any channel still
        running, leaving its partial count intact. This means there is
        no separate, arbitrarily-chosen capture timeout to invent: the
        shot's own already-deterministic duration is the bound.
        New PFMIN:STATus? (current captured-period count per channel,
        the way to know when an armed capture has finished without
        PFMIN:CAPTURE itself blocking) and PFMIN:DATA? <ch> (raw
        per-period ticks, self-describing its own count rather than
        assuming the armed M was reached, matching TABLE:END's own
        `OK <count>` precedent). New ERR 8/9.

        Channel/pin/AF mapping verified before writing any code, not
        assumed: docs/pin_mapping_v4.csv + the same datasheet
        column-position method already trusted in this project (Table
        13), cross-checked against known-good reference points on the
        same rows (TIM2_CH1 on PA15 -> AF1, matching the universally-
        known STM32 TIM1/TIM2=AF1 convention; SPI4/USART3/HRTIM1_FLTx
        landing on their own already-expected AF numbers on nearby
        rows). All 6: PA15/TIM2_CH1=AF1, PD4/TIM2_CH2=AF2,
        PB2/TIM5_CH1=AF2, PC12/TIM5_CH2=AF1, PB4/TIM3_CH1=AF2,
        PD12/TIM4_CH1=AF2. PA15 and PB4 both default to JTAG-debug-port
        pins at reset (JTDI, JTRST) -- reclaiming them as plain
        AF-muxed GPIO is standard given this project's SWD-only debug
        link, not a real risk, just noted.

        Key hardware constraint confirmed before designing the capture
        mechanism: TIM2 hosts BOTH PFM_Input_01 (CH1) and PFM_Input_02
        (CH2); TIM5 hosts BOTH PFM_Input_03 (CH1) and PFM_Input_04
        (CH2) -- a fixed PCB trace-routing fact, not a software choice.
        This rules out STM32's classic "PWM Input Mode" (which
        cross-connects a timer's CH1+CH2 to measure ONE input's
        rising+falling edges together) for those two timers, since each
        channel here must independently measure its own, possibly
        unrelated signal. Used TIM_ICPOLARITY_BOTHEDGE independently on
        every one of the 6 channels instead, with the live GPIO pin
        level read at each capture interrupt to disambiguate rising
        from falling -- applied uniformly across all 6 channels
        (including the two solo ones on TIM3/TIM4) for one consistent
        mechanism rather than mixing PWM Input Mode in where it would
        happen to fit.

        TIM2 and TIM5 are 32-bit counters; TIM3 and TIM4 are 16-bit
        (confirmed via stm32g474xx.h's IS_TIM_32B_COUNTER_INSTANCE
        macro). At this board's confirmed ~100 kHz-ish PFM_Input signal
        range and the 170 MHz timer kernel clock (APB1 undivided, same
        derivation already documented for HRTIM), no prescaler is used
        on any of the 4 counters -- full resolution (~1700 ticks/period
        at 100 kHz), and even the 16-bit counters' ~385 us/~2.6 kHz
        overflow floor is comfortably below this signal range. A real
        ceiling, not assumed to hold forever if actual signals turn out
        slower than expected -- flagged in pfm_input.c.

        stm32g4xx_hal_tim.c/.h (+ _tim_ex) did not exist in this
        project's Drivers/ tree at all (HAL_TIM_MODULE_ENABLED was
        commented out) -- copied from STM32Cube_FW_G4_V1.6.3, same
        exact-HAL-version-match reasoning as the QSPI driver copy last
        session (HAL Driver V1.2.7). Debug/Drivers/.../subdir.mk and
        Debug/objects.list hand-patched again (Core/Src/pfm_input.c,
        the two new Drivers/ source files).

        HAL_TIM_IC_MspInit()/MspDeInit() live in pfm_input.c itself,
        not stm32g4xx_hal_msp.c, matching hrtim.c/qspi_test.c's
        precedent for hand-added (non-CubeMX) peripherals.
        stm32g4xx_it.c's TIM2/TIM3/TIM4/TIM5_IRQHandler()s use the
        generic HAL_TIM_IRQHandler() + overridden
        HAL_TIM_IC_CaptureCallback() dispatch, the same style already
        used for USART2 (HAL_UART_IRQHandler()/RxCpltCallback(),
        uart.c) -- a better fit than HRTIM1_Master_IRQHandler()'s
        hand-rolled raw-flag style for 4 instances x up to 2 channels
        each. NVIC priority tied with HRTIM1_Master_IRQn and the
        GateDriverStatus EXTI interrupts (1,0), above USART2 (2,0).

        Self-contained and removable (PFM_INPUT_FEATURE_ENABLED,
        pfm_input.h), matching boot_jump.h/qspi_test.h's established
        pattern -- including a real gap caught and fixed before it ever
        shipped: the first version unconditionally declared
        `extern TIM_HandleTypeDef htim2..htim5` in the header and
        referenced them unconditionally in stm32g4xx_it.c's 4 new
        IRQHandler()s, but pfm_input.c only DEFINES them inside its own
        `#if PFM_INPUT_FEATURE_ENABLED` block -- building with the
        feature disabled would have failed to link (undefined
        references) despite compiling cleanly, a real hole the "does it
        actually still build with everything else here" habit this
        project already has caught before it needed a bug report. Fixed
        by guarding both the extern declarations and the 4 IRQHandler()
        definitions on the same macro -- confirmed by actually building
        both ways (see Verified below), not just re-reading the fix.

        Buffer sizing note: PFMIN:DATA?'s reply can be large (up to
        PFM_INPUT_MAX_PERIODS=200 entries, ~4.4 KB of text) -- this
        project's linker script reserves only 1 KB (_Min_Stack_Size,
        STM32G474QETX_FLASH.ld) for the stack, so that buffer is
        `static` in commands.c, not a stack-local array, even though
        the actual runtime stack has much more room in practice (see
        AGENTS.md's own already-flagged, still-deferred note on stack
        sizing) -- not worth adding a multi-KB stack frame to a
        codebase that has already flagged this as an open question
        rather than a settled one.

        Verified on real hardware: clean rebuild (zero warnings) with
        PFM_INPUT_FEATURE_ENABLED both 1 and 0 (confirms genuine
        removability -- disabling drops 14704 bytes of .bss, and fixes
        the extern/IRQHandler linkage gap described above, confirmed by
        the disabled build actually linking cleanly). With it enabled:
        reflashed, PFMIN:CAPTURE 20 with no FIRE yet -> PFMIN:STATUS?
        correctly reads all zeros (arming alone touches no hardware);
        M=0 and M=9999 both correctly rejected with ERR 9; channel 0
        and 7 both correctly rejected with ERR 8 on PFMIN:DATA?; a
        normal TABLE upload + FIRE with an armed-but-unconnected
        capture completed normally (FIRE still replies OK immediately,
        no blocking) and PFMIN:STATUS?/DATA? afterward correctly showed
        all-zero counts (no signal present on any PFM_Input pin on this
        bench right now, the expected result, not a hang). NOT yet
        verified on real hardware: an actual signal's period/duty
        values coming back correctly, the TIM2/TIM5 shared-channel
        independence claim, and a fault-triggered stop mid-capture --
        all need a real signal source (function generator, or an HRTIM
        output looped back to a PFM_Input pin) and are the bench's own
        work, not something this session could do standalone.
        docs/command_reference.md not yet updated with
        PFMIN:CAPTURE/STATus?/DATA?/ERR 8/9 -- deferred until that
        bench verification lands, so the write-up reflects fully
        confirmed behavior, not just the command-layer shape of it.

2026-09-08  —  QUADSPI test module (qspi_test.c/.h) -- W25Q128JVS
               connectivity confirmed on real hardware
-----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Change:  Requested as an easily removable module, following
        boot_jump.h's own "self-contained and removable" pattern
        exactly: QSPI_TEST_FEATURE_ENABLED (qspi_test.h) is the single
        point of control. Scope, per direct confirmation: connectivity
        only -- one command, the W25Q128JVS's standard JEDEC Read ID
        instruction (0x9F, plain 1-line mode, no Quad Enable required)
        -- not a general QUADSPI driver, not program/erase, not
        memory-mapped access.

        HAL enablement: stm32g4xx_hal_qspi.c/.h did not exist in this
        project's Drivers/ tree at all (QUADSPI was never enabled via
        CubeMX here). Copied from STM32Cube_FW_G4_V1.6.3's package,
        chosen specifically because it bundles HAL Driver V1.2.7 -- an
        exact version match against every other driver file already in
        this project (confirmed via each file's own version banner
        before copying, not assumed) -- to avoid any HAL-version API
        mismatch. HAL_QSPI_MODULE_ENABLED uncommented in
        stm32g4xx_hal_conf.h. Both Debug/Drivers/.../subdir.mk and
        Debug/objects.list needed the same hand-patching gotcha
        AGENTS.md already documents for Core/Src additions -- this is
        the first time it applied to Drivers/ instead.

        Pin/AF mapping verified before writing any code, not assumed
        from the HAL header's existence: docs/pin_mapping_v4.csv lists
        PE12-PE15 (BK1_IO0-3) and PB10/PB11 (CLK/BK1_NCS). The HAL only
        exposes two QUADSPI-capable AF constants (AF3, AF10) and which
        one applies is pin-specific -- confirmed via the same datasheet
        column-position method already trusted in this project (Table
        13), calibrated against three independent already-known-good
        reference points on the same table rows (TIM1_CH3N->AF2,
        SPI4_SCK->AF5, FMC_D9->AF12 on the PE12 row; USART3_TX->AF7,
        HRTIM1_FLT3->AF13 on the PB10 row -- that last one an exact
        cross-check against this session's own already-hardware-verified
        PC10/HRTIM1_FLT6->AF13 result). All 6 pins landed on AF10
        (GPIO_AF10_QUADSPI) -- confirmed, not guessed.

        qspi_test.c: HAL_QSPI_MspInit()/MspDeInit() live here, not in
        stm32g4xx_hal_msp.c, matching hrtim.c's own precedent for
        hand-added (non-CubeMX-generated) peripheral pin config -- see
        AGENTS.md's "NOT REGEN-SAFE" HRTIM/CubeMX collision history for
        why. QUADSPI kernel clock explicitly set to SYSCLK
        (HAL_RCCEx_PeriphCLKConfig(), RCC_QSPICLKSOURCE_SYSCLK) rather
        than relying on the POR default being correct. ClockPrescaler
        chosen for a conservative 5 MHz first-bring-up rate (170 MHz
        SYSCLK / 34). NCS pin gets GPIO_PULLUP (flagged assumption, no
        external pull confirmed -- idle-deselected-by-default before
        the peripheral drives it); CLK/IO0-3 get GPIO_NOPULL.

        New QSPI:ID? command (commands.c/commands.h, cmd_parser.c),
        gated on QSPI_TEST_FEATURE_ENABLED the same way BOOT is gated
        on BOOT_JUMP_FEATURE_ENABLED -- entirely absent from the
        command table when disabled. Reports the 3 raw ID bytes as hex
        ("OK EF 40 18"), no hardcoded expected value asserted -- the
        command reports what the chip says, doesn't claim to validate
        it. New ERR 7 (QUADSPI command/receive failure or timeout).

        BUGFIX, found and fixed via real-hardware testing, not
        docs/guesswork: the first QSPI:ID? attempt against actual
        silicon returned a stable, repeatable "DE 80 30" instead of the
        expected Winbond "EF 40 18". Confirmed this was not noise or a
        wiring fault: EF4018 left-shifted by exactly 1 bit (as one
        continuous 24-bit stream) == DE8030, an exact match. Root cause:
        QSPI_InitTypeDef.SampleShifting was QSPI_SAMPLE_SHIFTING_HALFCYCLE
        (the usually-recommended default, meant to compensate for
        signal propagation delay) -- switching to
        QSPI_SAMPLE_SHIFTING_NONE fixed it immediately and repeatably.
        Plausible explanation (not chased further): at this
        deliberately conservative 5 MHz and whatever this board's
        QUADSPI trace length actually is, the extra half-cycle of
        sample delay overshoots past the valid data window instead of
        centering in it. Documented inline in qspi_test.c; worth
        revisiting if the clock rate is ever raised well past 5 MHz.

        Verified on real hardware, end to end: clean rebuild (zero
        warnings) both with the module enabled and with
        QSPI_TEST_FEATURE_ENABLED forced to 0 (confirms genuine
        removability, not just a claim -- disabling drops 2308 bytes of
        .text and the command disappears from the table). With it
        enabled and the SampleShifting fix applied: QSPI:ID? returned
        "OK EF 40 18" -- correct Winbond manufacturer ID, correct
        W25Q128 memory type/capacity bytes -- repeatably (3x) after
        reflashing. This confirms the new QUADSPI hardware (pins,
        traces, the W25Q128JVS itself) is wired and responding
        correctly, beyond just the firmware compiling.

        docs/command_reference.md updated with QSPI:ID?/ERR 7 -- and,
        since this session's real-hardware verification of the
        GateDriverStatus fault path (see the entry below) was already
        thorough enough to stand on its own, FAULT?/FAULT:CLEAR/ERR 6
        got written up in the same pass rather than staying deferred
        any longer, with the PC10-specific low-drive bench trip
        clearly flagged there as the one still-open item.

2026-09-08  —  GateDriverStatus fault interrupt (PE0-PE11) + version.h/
               ctrlr_config.h re-condensed
-----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Change:  Two changes requested together, both in ctrlr_config.h:

        1. version.h condensed back into ctrlr_config.h. Briefly split
        earlier the same day (identity in version.h, behavior in
        ctrlr_config.h) on the reasoning that identity and behavior are
        different kinds of thing -- re-condensed per direct request,
        back to one config file, matching the sibling PFM-STM32G474
        project's single supply_config.h. version.h deleted;
        HW_BOARD_NAME/REV, FW_VERSION_STRING now live in
        ctrlr_config.h. Only one includer existed (commands.c); updated,
        plus doc-comment references in commands.h/commands.c/AGENTS.md.

        2. GateDriverStatus_01..12 (PE0-PE11) promoted from GDS?'s
        read-only diagnostic (see the entry below) to a real fault
        interrupt that can stop PWM output on its own, independent of
        PC10/HRTIM1_FLT6. New GDS_FAULT_POLARITY (ctrlr_config.h,
        NORMALLY_HIGH/NORMALLY_LOW, naming matches the sibling
        PFM-STM32G474 project's supply_config.h exactly) -- set to
        NORMALLY_LOW after direct confirmation against this board's
        actual gate driver ICs. Single source of truth in one file,
        avoiding the sibling project's own documented history of this
        same setting once being defined twice (supply_config.h and
        gate_driver.h, with gate_driver.h's #ifndef always silently
        winning) -- not a risk here structurally, since gate_driver.h
        includes ctrlr_config.h rather than defining anything itself.

        main.c: PE0-PE11 upgraded from GPIO_MODE_INPUT to
        GPIO_MODE_IT_RISING_FALLING (both edges -- the fault/healthy
        determination happens in software against GDS_FAULT_POLARITY,
        not by picking a trigger edge), plus __HAL_RCC_SYSCFG_CLK_ENABLE()
        (required for HAL_GPIO_Init() to actually route EXTI lines) and
        NVIC priority/enable for all 7 vectors PE0-PE11 spans
        (EXTI0-EXTI4 individually, EXTI9_5, EXTI15_10 -- tied with
        HRTIM1_Master_IRQn's priority, above USART2's).

        stm32g4xx_it.c: 7 new IRQ handlers, each clearing only its own
        pin's pending bit then calling gate_driver.c's
        GateDriver_CheckFault() -- which re-reads and evaluates ALL 12
        pins itself regardless of which specific edge woke the CPU, per
        the literal ask ("bitwise read PE0 through PE11" -- see below
        for why PE11, not PE12 as originally asked; confirmed with the
        user before implementing).

        gate_driver.c/.h: new GateDriver_CheckFault() (bitwise mask
        against GDS_FAULT_POLARITY: NORMALLY_LOW faults on any HIGH bit,
        NORMALLY_HIGH faults on any LOW bit; on a fault, immediately
        PFM_ForceStop() + latch) and GateDriver_FaultIsLatched()/
        GateDriver_FaultClear(). GateDriver_FaultClear() clears the
        latch then immediately re-validates by calling
        GateDriver_CheckFault() again -- deliberate, and load-bearing:
        unlike PC10/HRTIM1_FLT6's hardware latch (which re-trips on its
        own if the condition is still physically present),
        GateDriver_CheckFault() only ever runs from an EXTI edge (or the
        one explicit boot-time call, next paragraph) -- without the
        recheck, clearing a still-bad pin would silently report FAULT?
        OK 0 until the next incidental toggle.

        main.c also gained one explicit GateDriver_CheckFault() call at
        boot, before uart_init(). EXTI is edge-triggered: a pin already
        in its fault state at the moment PE0-PE11 are configured for
        interrupt mode produces no edge of its own and would otherwise
        go undetected until something happened to toggle it later. This
        was not a hypothetical concern -- see Verified below.

        pfm.h/pfm.c: new PFM_ForceStop() (HRTIM1_PWM_Stop() +
        g_pfmState = STOPPED), extracted from the two places
        PFM_CycleBoundaryHandler() already did exactly this inline (the
        PC10-fault and empty-table branches), so GateDriver_CheckFault()
        -- running from EXTI context, independent of the HRTIM
        master-repetition ISR -- can force the same clean stop without
        leaving g_pfmState stale at RUNNING.

        commands.c: FAULT?/FAULT:CLEAR/FIRE's ERR 6 check now consider
        BOTH fault sources via a new AnyFaultLatched() helper
        (HRTIM1_FaultIsTripped() || GateDriver_FaultIsLatched()) --
        combined only at this command layer; the two detection
        mechanisms stay structurally independent underneath. FAULT:CLEAR
        clears both latches unconditionally.

        Pin range clarified before implementing, not assumed: the
        request said "PE0 through PE12," but docs/pin_mapping_v4.csv
        shows PE12 is unconnected on this board (QUADSPI_BK1_IO0, "No
        connection") while PE0-PE11 are exactly the 12 GateDriverStatus
        pins already confirmed by GDS?. Confirmed PE0-PE11 (12 pins) was
        intended before writing any pin-mask/EXTI code -- including an
        unconnected, floating PE12 in the fault-OR logic would have
        risked either a spurious permanent fault-lock or, depending on
        how it happened to float, silently masking a real fault.

        Verified on real hardware, end-to-end: clean rebuild (zero
        warnings), reflash. GDS? read GateDriverStatus_03 (PE2) already
        HIGH -- under the confirmed NORMALLY_LOW polarity, that's a real
        fault condition, present before this change ever existed to
        detect it. Immediately after flashing: FAULT? read OK 1 (the
        boot-time explicit check caught it, confirming that fix was not
        just theoretical), FIRE was correctly rejected with ERR 6, and
        FAULT:CLEAR correctly re-latched (FAULT? back to OK 1 right
        after) rather than clearing a fault that was still physically
        present -- confirming the recheck-on-clear behavior end to end,
        not just by inspection.

2026-09-08  —  GateDriverStatus diagnostic (GDS?, PE0-PE11)
-----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Change:  Added a raw HIGH/LOW readback of the 12 GateDriverStatus_01
        through _12 pins (PE0-PE11, GPIOE -- docs/pin_mapping_v4.csv),
        requested while investigating why a fault wasn't being
        registered. New Core/Src/Inc/gate_driver.c/.h -- deliberately
        narrow (unlike the sibling PFM-STM32G474 project's
        gate_driver.c/fault_pins.c): just GateDriver_Read(), a direct
        GPIOE->IDR read with no debounce, no fault-latching, no per-pin
        polarity/callback table. These 12 pins are gate-driver-IC status
        outputs and were never wired into HRTIM's native fault input
        (HRTIM1_FLT6/PC10, see the entry below) -- this command cannot
        stop PWM by itself; it's read-only visibility.

        These pins had never been configured as GPIO inputs at all
        before this change -- MX_GPIO_Init() (main.c) only enabled
        GPIOA. Added GPIOE clock enable + GPIO_MODE_INPUT/GPIO_NOPULL
        config for PE0-PE11 there, matching the sibling project's
        gpio.c precedent for these same 12 pins exactly (plain input,
        no pull -- actively driven by the gate driver ICs).

        New GDS? command (commands.c/commands.h, cmd_parser.c) reports
        all 12 states on a single OK line ("OK 01=HIGH 02=LOW ...",
        NN = 01-12 matching the GateDriverStatus_01..12 numbering,
        space-separated, PE0 first) -- kept to this project's existing
        single-"OK <value>"-line convention rather than adopting the
        sibling project's multi-line/bitmask GDS? reply shapes.

        Build note: gate_driver.c is a source file added directly (not
        via CubeIDE's UI), so Debug/Core/Src/subdir.mk and
        Debug/objects.list both needed hand-patching to pick it up from
        the command line -- see AGENTS.md's "Adding a new source file
        from outside CubeIDE" note (already documented there from
        earlier files; not new guidance).

        Verified on real hardware: clean rebuild (zero warnings),
        reflash, GDS? read twice back-to-back -- stable, repeatable
        result (11 of 12 pins LOW, one HIGH) on this board's current
        as-wired state. What that particular pattern means electrically
        (which pin, what it indicates) was not interpreted here -- raw
        readback only, by design.

2026-09-08  —  HRTIM native hardware fault input (PC10 / HRTIM1_FLT6)
-----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Change:  Wired up PC10, wired on this board directly to HRTIM1's own
        FLT6 fault input (docs/pin_mapping_v4.csv), as a real silicon-
        level fault mechanism -- the peripheral forces every fault-
        enabled channel's outputs to a safe (INACTIVE) level
        autonomously, in hardware, the instant the pin trips, with no
        CPU/interrupt/polling latency and no dependency on the CPU
        being alive. Deliberately NOT the sibling PFM-STM32G474
        project's HRTIM1_EmergencyStop()/fault_pins.c approach (a
        GPIO-polled pin, software-forced off after the fact) -- this
        project's hrtim.h had flagged that as "port verbatim when fault
        handling is added"; the real ask turned out to be stronger than
        a port, so it wasn't ported.

        Active-low (fault = pin driven low), per direct confirmation
        against the fault-sensing circuit. AF13 (GPIO_AF13_HRTIM1)
        confirmed via the same datasheet column-position method already
        trusted in this project, re-calibrated against the already-known
        -good PC8/AF3 result before trusting the new PC10 result.

        hrtim.c: HAL_HRTIM_MspInit() configures PC10 (AF13, GPIO_PULLUP
        -- a flagged defensive-default assumption, no external pull
        confirmed). HRTIM1_FullInit() adds HAL_HRTIM_FaultConfig() +
        HAL_HRTIM_FaultModeCtl() for FLT6 (DIGITALINPUT source, LOW
        polarity), pTimerCfg.FaultEnable = HRTIM_TIMFAULTENABLE_FAULT6
        for every timer, pOutputCfg.FaultLevel =
        HRTIM_OUTPUTFAULTLEVEL_INACTIVE for every output (the field that
        actually does the output-safing; FaultEnable alone does not).
        New HRTIM1_FaultIsTripped() (reads the ISR flag directly, no
        software cache) and HRTIM1_FaultClear() (stops counters, clears
        the flag -- deliberately does NOT re-issue
        HAL_HRTIM_WaveformOutputStart() itself; reconnecting outputs is
        left entirely to the next explicit FIRE -> PFM_Restart() ->
        HRTIM1_PWM_Start(), which already does a complete from-scratch
        reconnect with no assumption about prior state, so duplicating
        any part of it here would be unnecessary risk -- a deliberate
        simplification of this feature's own plan, reasoning recorded in
        the code comment).

        pfm.c: PFM_CycleBoundaryHandler() gains a bookkeeping check --
        if HRTIM1_FaultIsTripped(), stop the counters and get
        PFM_GetState() out of RUNNING (the hardware has already safed
        the outputs by the time this ever runs; this just stops the
        table from silently continuing to advance).

        commands.c/commands.h/cmd_parser.c: new FAULT? (OK 0|1) and
        FAULT:CLEAR (OK) commands, and FIRE now rejects with a new
        ERR 6 ("Fault latched -- send FAULT:CLEAR first") if a fault is
        latched, checked before the existing empty-table ERR 5 check.
        Latched-until-cleared by design (matching the sibling project's
        Lockout/CLEARFAULTS convention) -- FAULT:CLEAR must be sent
        explicitly even after the physical fault condition has gone
        away; a fault silently clearing itself with output resuming
        unnoticed is the hazard this avoids.

        Verified on real hardware so far: clean rebuild (zero warnings),
        reflash, command-layer pass with no fault forced -- FAULT? reads
        OK 0 at idle (PC10's idle-high state does not read as a spurious
        trip, the key regression risk from this change), FIRE still
        works normally. NOT yet verified: an actual PC10-low trip
        observed on scope/DSLogic, and the FAULT:CLEAR recovery sequence
        (whether clearing the flag alone is sufficient to resume PWM on
        the next FIRE) -- both need the pin physically driven low on the
        bench, still pending. docs/command_reference.md not yet updated
        with these commands/ERR 6 -- deferred until that bench
        verification is done, per the original plan's own verification
        checklist.

2026-09-08  —  Configurable HRTIM channel count (compile-time, 1-5)
-----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Change:  Requested as a planned, reviewed change (plan mode used
        directly): make version.h's PWM_NUM_CHANNELS -- documentation-
        only since it was added, "NOT read by hrtim.c today" -- into a
        real, working setting. New Core/Inc/ctrlr_config.h holds
        HRTIM_NUM_CHANNELS (1-5, #error-guarded outside that range),
        superseding PWM_NUM_CHANNELS; version.h now holds board/
        firmware IDENTITY only, a deliberate split from the prior "one
        config file" decision (both files' headers explain why).

        Range is 1-5, not 1-6, because the HRTIM Master timer has
        exactly 4 compare registers (5 total trigger points incl. its
        own PER event) -- confirmed against the HAL headers before
        committing to this scope, along with confirming real (if
        unused here) HAL support exists for a 6th phase-locked channel
        via cross-timer HRTIM_TIMRESETTRIGGER_OTHERx_CMPy reset
        triggers -- deliberately left as a follow-up, not bundled in,
        since it needs new phase math with no existing pattern to
        extend.

        Generalized (every hardcoded A/B/C block became a loop over
        0..N-1, or in HRTIM1_FullInit()'s case 0..5 with the
        phase-locked/free-running split moving to depend on N instead
        of being hardcoded per timer):
          - pfm.h/pfm.c: PFM_Step_t's cmpA/cmpB/cmpC -> cmp[N], sized
            exactly per build (N=3 stays 8 B/entry, unchanged;
            N=4->10 B, N=5->12 B). PFM_Phase120()/PFM_Phase240() ->
            PFM_PhaseForChannel(). PFM_Phase_t/PFM_SetPhaseEnabled()/
            PFM_GetPhaseEnabled() -> plain 0..N-1 channel index, same
            durability contract (not reset by PFM_Init() after the
            first call -- see that function's own comment for why a
            GCC __attribute__((constructor)) approach was tried and
            rejected in favor of a portable static-flag pattern, no
            dependency on __libc_init_array() actually running).
          - hrtim.h/hrtim.c: HRTIM1_FullInit()'s A/B/C-phase-locked +
            D/E/F-free-running blocks -> one loop, channel < N gets
            MASTER_PER (channel 0) or MASTER_CMP1..4 (channels 1..N-1),
            channel >= N gets ResetTrigger=NONE (identical to the old
            D/E/F treatment). HRTIM1_PWM_Start()'s per-phase cold-start
            settling (see the 2026-09-04 entries below) generalizes
            directly, unchanged in mechanism -- HRTIM1_WaitForPhase
            AndConnect() now called in a loop (channels 1..N-1 ascending,
            channel 0 last) instead of 3 named calls.
            HRTIM1_ApplyPfmStep() takes (per, cmp[], phase[]) instead of
            6 named scalars. Per-channel register-address getters
            (HRTIM1_GetTimerAPerRegAddress() etc.) -> single indexed
            getters (HRTIM1_GetTimerPerRegAddress(channel) etc.) backed
            by lookup tables confirmed against the HAL headers
            (HRTIM_TIMERINDEX_TIMER_A..F sequential 0x0-0x5;
            HRTIM_OUTPUT_Tx1/2 bitmasks arithmetic but looked up via
            named-constant tables anyway rather than relied on as raw
            bit tricks, since this path isn't latency-critical the way
            HRTIM1_PWM_Start()'s cold-start settling is).
          - commands.c: TABLE:STEP's fixed 4-token parse -> 1+N tokens
            (per + one compare per channel). New CONFig:CHANnels?
            (OK <N>) so host tooling can confirm a board's actual N
            instead of assuming it.
          - python/pfm_table_upload.py: build_table()/_ramp() build
            (per, *cmp[0..N-1]) tuples via a new _entry() helper instead
            of hardcoded 4-tuples; queries CONFIG:CHANNELS? at startup
            and refuses to upload on a mismatch against its own
            HRTIM_NUM_CHANNELS constant.

        A real bug was found and fixed during this work, not just
        designed around: cmd_table_step()'s first version tried to
        stringify TABLE_STEP_TOKEN_COUNT (= 1 + HRTIM_NUM_CHANNELS)
        with the standard #x/two-level preprocessor stringification
        idiom -- which only works on plain literals, not expressions,
        so the ERR 4 message printed the literal unevaluated text
        "(1U + (4U))" instead of "5". Caught by actually reading the
        real device reply during hardware testing, not just compiling.
        Fixed with a runtime snprintf() (SendTableStepCountErr())
        instead of compile-time stringification.

        Verified: full clean rebuild at the default N=3, zero warnings,
        .bss unchanged from before this change (PFM_Step_t stays
        exactly 8 B/entry at N=3 -- confirms the generalization adds no
        overhead at the project's actual current channel count).
        Reflashed over serial, *IDN? and the new CONFig:CHANnels? both
        correct, a real 500-entry table uploaded (with the new
        mismatch-check passing) and FIRE'd twice, board stayed fully
        responsive throughout.

        Then temporarily rebuilt at N=4 specifically to prove the
        generalization works at a DIFFERENT channel count, not just
        N=3 restated: .bss grew by exactly the predicted +10,000 B
        (2 B/entry x 5000 entries -- real .map numbers, not estimated),
        CONFig:CHANnels? correctly reported 4, a hand-built 4-wide
        table uploaded and FIRE'd cleanly, and the wrong-token-count
        rejection (ERR 4, with the now-fixed message) was confirmed
        against a deliberately-3-wide TABLE:STEP. Also confirmed the
        ctrlr_config.h #error guard actually fires for an out-of-range
        N=6, not just that the comment claims it does. Reverted to the
        real N=3 default before committing -- N=4/5 are proven to
        compile, flash, and respond correctly at the command layer;
        they are not the shipped setting.

        NOT done as part of this entry: a DSLogic capture confirming
        N=4/5's actual electrical waveform (even spacing at the new
        angle, correct dead time on the added channel) -- this
        project's physical probes have only ever been wired to 3
        channels, so that verification needs new wiring plus a bench
        pass, out of scope for a from-the-desk implementation session.
        Command-layer + RAM-math verification above is real and
        thorough, but it is not a substitute for that.

2026-09-04  —  New tool: python/dsl_viewer.py
------------------------------------------------
  Change:  Requested directly: an easier way to visualize/interact with
        saved DSLogic .dsl captures, specifically to zoom the time axis
        -- every .dsl decode this session up to now was a one-off
        script (unzip, parse the INI header, unpack the bit-packed
        L-<channel>/0 files, print edge timestamps by hand). Generalized
        that into a real, reusable interactive viewer instead of
        re-deriving it next time.

        Renders every channel as a stacked digital trace (matplotlib),
        picks up channel names already set in DSView's own session JSON
        automatically (or via --labels), and skips all-zero/disabled
        channels by default (e.g. this project's usual unconnected
        4/5) unless --all-channels is passed. Two custom interactions
        on top of matplotlib's own pan/zoom toolbar: scroll wheel zooms
        the time axis only, centered on the cursor (the actual ask --
        dragging a zoom box every time was the friction point); and a
        two-cursor measurement readout (left-click = cursor A,
        right-click = cursor B, delta time + implied frequency shown in
        the title) -- directly mirrors the manual edge-timing math this
        session kept doing by hand for dead time, phase spacing, and
        duty cycle.

        .dsl format handling documents its own scope honestly: only
        device mode 0 (plain digital capture) and single-block files
        are supported, both confirmed by every real capture decoded
        this session -- multi-block or oscilloscope-mode captures raise
        a clear error instead of silently misdecoding, since neither
        has ever actually been seen to verify against.

        Verified against a real capture (the 50176-sample profile-3
        capture from the per-phase cold-start fix, see the entry
        further below): decoded and rendered correctly headlessly
        (--save) at both the full 5ms view (a dense, expected "brush"
        pattern at 100 kHz) and a zoomed 22us window, matching the
        hand-decoded values from that same file earlier in this
        session exactly. Also caught and fixed a real argparse bug
        during testing: a single --window "start,end" argument silently
        failed on any negative start time (argparse mistook the leading
        "-" for another flag) -- replaced with separate --window-start/
        --window-end float arguments instead of working around it.
